1Schools of psychology: structuralism, functionalism, cognitive approach
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q11, Q92, Q45, Q9, Q73

From scratch

Psychology split off from philosophy and physiology in 1879, when Wilhelm Wundt opened the first formal psychology laboratory at the University of Leipzig, Germany. Wundt is the father of experimental psychology. He called his own system voluntarism (because he stressed the will and active attention, which he called apperception), and he studied immediate conscious experience using trained introspection: an observer, practised in thousands of trials, reports his inner experience while the experimenter controls the stimulus (a light, a click, a metronome beat). Wundt also proposed a tridimensional theory of feeling (pleasant-unpleasant, tension-relaxation, excitement-calm).

The whole story of the "schools" is a chain of reactions. Each new school defined itself by what it rejected in the one before. Learn each school as four things: founder, what it studied, what it rejected, method.

1. Structuralism (Edward Bradford Titchener, an Englishman who took his PhD with Wundt and moved to Cornell University in 1892). The name "structuralism" is Titchener's, not Wundt's. Titchener asked what the mind is made of. Like a chemist breaking water into hydrogen and oxygen, he broke consciousness into three elements: sensations (elements of perception), images (elements of ideas) and affections (elements of emotion). Each element had attributes: quality, intensity, duration, clearness and (for some) extensity. A perception, for Titchener, is a complex of sensory and imaginal processes: the sensations of the moment plus the images that past experience adds to them (his context theory of meaning). That exact definition was asked as IHBAS 2024 Q9. Method: strict analytic introspection, and observers had to avoid the stimulus error (Titchener's term): reporting the object ("an apple") instead of the raw sensations ("red, round, shiny, cool"). Structuralism rejected applied psychology, animal psychology, child psychology and abnormal psychology as not pure science. It ended with Titchener's death in 1927 because introspection was unreliable (labs could not agree), it could not study minds that cannot introspect, and it had no practical use.

2. Functionalism (America, about 1890 to 1930). William James (Harvard) wrote The Principles of Psychology (1890), in which he described consciousness as a continuous, ever-changing, personal stream of consciousness (his phrase, from the chapter "The Stream of Thought"). You cannot step into the same stream twice, so chopping it into elements, as Titchener did, misses what it is. James is the leading forerunner and the standard MCQ answer for functionalism (IHBAS 2024 Q11 and Q73). The school was formally organised at the University of Chicago by John Dewey, whose 1896 paper "The Reflex Arc Concept in Psychology" is called the founding document of the Chicago school, by James Rowland Angell, whose 1906 APA presidential address "The Province of Functional Psychology" set out its programme, and by Harvey Carr, who built it into a full system around the adaptive act. A parallel Columbia group (James McKeen Cattell, Edward Thorndike, Robert Woodworth) pushed mental testing and learning. Inspired by Charles Darwin, functionalists asked what the mind is for: consciousness exists because it helps the organism adapt and survive. Functionalism rejected structuralism's elementism and its narrow, "pure" lab focus. It welcomed animals, children, individual differences, mental tests and education. Method: introspection plus objective observation, testing and experiment. Functionalism never died; it was absorbed into applied, educational and evolutionary psychology.

3. Behaviourism (John B. Watson, manifesto "Psychology as the Behaviorist Views It", 1913). Watson took his PhD at Chicago under Angell, then turned on his teachers. He threw out consciousness and introspection entirely: psychology should be an objective natural science of observable behaviour, with the goal of the prediction and control of behaviour. The mind became a "black box". Method: observation, the conditioned-reflex method (borrowed from Ivan Pavlov), testing. His Little Albert study (Watson and Rayner, 1920) conditioned fear in an infant. B. F. Skinner later developed radical behaviourism and operant conditioning. Edward Tolman's purposive behaviourism and "cognitive maps" in rats (1948) were an early crack in the black box.

4. Gestalt psychology (Germany, 1912). Max Wertheimer founded it with his paper on apparent movement, the phi phenomenon (two lights flashed in quick alternation look like one light moving). His two subjects were Kurt Koffka and Wolfgang Kohler, who became co-founders. Gestalt means "form" or "whole". Their slogan (Koffka): the whole is different from the sum of its parts; a melody stays the same melody when transposed into another key, even though every note changes. Gestalt rejected the elementism of Wundt and Titchener and, later, the stimulus-response atomism of behaviourism. Method: phenomenological description of experience as a naive observer reports it, plus experiment. Kohler's The Mentality of Apes showed insight learning in chimpanzees (Sultan joining two sticks). Koffka wrote Principles of Gestalt Psychology (1935). Gestalt gave us the laws of perceptual organisation (proximity, similarity, closure, continuity).

5. Psychoanalysis (Sigmund Freud, Vienna; The Interpretation of Dreams, 1900). Freud came from medicine, not the lab. He studied the unconscious: hidden sexual and aggressive drives, conflict and defence. He rejected the idea that conscious experience is the whole of mind (the iceberg metaphor) and rejected the lab as the place to study it. Method: clinical case study, free association, dream analysis, analysis of slips and transference.

6. Humanistic psychology (1950s and 1960s), the "third force" (Abraham Maslow's phrase) after psychoanalysis and behaviourism. Maslow gave the hierarchy of needs (1943) topped by self-actualisation; Carl Rogers gave client-centred therapy and the core conditions (unconditional positive regard, empathy, congruence). It studied the healthy, growing, whole person and free will; it rejected the determinism of both Freud (driven by the unconscious) and Watson (driven by the environment). Method: case study, phenomenological self-report (Rogers used the Q-sort to measure self-concept).

7. Cognitive psychology, the reply to behaviourism in the cognitive revolution of the 1950s and 1960s: George Miller's "magical number seven, plus or minus two" (1956), Noam Chomsky's 1959 review of Skinner's Verbal Behavior, Donald Broadbent's filter model of attention (1958), and Allen Newell and Herbert Simon's computer models of problem solving. Ulric Neisser named the field in his book Cognitive Psychology (1967) and is called the father of cognitive psychology. He defined cognition as all the processes by which sensory input is transformed, reduced, elaborated, stored, recovered and used. The cognitive approach studies the internal mental processes between stimulus and response (IHBAS 2024 Q92): attention, perception, memory, language, thinking, problem solving, decision making. It rejected behaviourism's ban on the mind, but kept its objective experimental method. Core metaphor: information processing (input, encoding, storage, retrieval, output, like a computer). Method: controlled experiments, reaction time, error patterns, computer simulation.

Later cognitive models of memory: Collins and Quillian's hierarchical semantic network (1969); Collins and Loftus's spreading-activation network (1975); and connectionism or parallel distributed processing (David Rumelhart and James McClelland, 1986), in which knowledge is stored as weighted connections among many simple, neuron-like nodes that work in parallel (IHBAS 2024 Q45).

SchoolOwnerStudiedRejectedMethod

StructuralismTitchener (Cornell)Elements of consciousnessApplied, child, animal, abnormal workAnalytic introspection

BehaviourismWatson (1913); SkinnerObservable behaviour, S-RConsciousness, introspectionObjective experiment, conditioning
GestaltWertheimer, Koffka, Kohler (1912)Organised wholes, perception, insightElementism; S-R atomismPhenomenological observation

Hooks. Titchener = Table of elements (Titchener the chemist, three elements: S-I-A, "Sensations, Images, Affections").

James = a stream (picture a river through Harvard yard); Dewey, Angell, Carr = "Don't Ask Chicago" (the Chicago functionalists).

W-W-W: Wundt opened the lab, Watson closed the mind, Wertheimer saw the whole.

Neisser = New name for the mind (1967, he Named cognitive psychology); Maslow and Rogers = "Me and Real self", the third force.

Key distinctions

  • Wundt vs Titchener: Wundt founded experimental psychology and the first lab (Leipzig, 1879) and called his system voluntarism. Titchener coined and owns structuralism. "Founder of structuralism" = Titchener.
  • Structuralism vs functionalism: what the mind is (elements) vs what the mind is for (adaptation). Titchener vs James.
  • James vs Dewey/Angell: James is the forerunner and the MCQ answer for "most associated with functionalism"; Dewey and Angell formally organised it at Chicago.
  • Structuralism vs Gestalt: both studied conscious experience, but structuralism cut it into elements and Gestalt insisted on organised wholes.
  • Behaviourism vs cognitive: both are objective and experimental; behaviourism forbids internal processes, cognitive psychology studies them.
  • Cognitive vs psychoanalysis: both look "inside", but cognitive = information processing (mostly conscious, testable), psychoanalysis = unconscious drives and conflict.
  • Humanistic vs psychoanalysis/behaviourism: free will and growth vs determinism (by the unconscious or by the environment).
  • Connectionist vs network model: connectionist (Rumelhart and McClelland) = weighted nodes working in parallel, learning by changing weights; semantic network (Collins and Quillian) = concepts as nodes in a hierarchy of labelled links.

MCQ traps

  • Titchener on a functionalism question: wrong, he is the structuralist rival functionalism attacked.
  • Wundt for "stream of consciousness" or "founder of structuralism": wrong, the stream is James's phrase and the structuralism label is Titchener's.
  • G. Stanley Hall for functionalism: wrong, Hall founded the APA (1892) and the first US lab (Johns Hopkins, 1883), but James is the functionalism answer.
  • Watson for functionalism or for "internal mental processes": wrong, he founded behaviourism (1913), which rejected consciousness.
  • Skinner or Jung for "stream of consciousness": wrong, Skinner was a radical behaviourist and Jung is analytical psychology (collective unconscious, archetypes).
  • Bruner for "perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal events": wrong, Bruner is the New Look (motivation shapes perception); the elemental definition is Titchener's.
  • Psychoanalysis or humanistic for "internal mental processes": wrong, psychoanalysis is unconscious conflict and humanistic is growth and free will; the answer is cognitive psychology.
  • Network model for "interconnected nodes": tempting plain English, but the IHBAS key wants connectionist (PDP).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q11: Functionalism was most closely associated with which psychologist → William James. Distractors: Titchener (structuralism, the rival), Watson (behaviourism, came after and rejected consciousness), G. Stanley Hall (APA founder, developmental psychology).
  • Q73: Who coined "stream of consciousness" → William James (Principles, 1890). Distractors: Carl Jung (analytical psychology), Wilhelm Wundt (elements via introspection, not a stream), B. F. Skinner (rejected consciousness).
  • Q9: Who defined perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal events → Titchener. Distractors: Kimble (definitions of learning), Bruner and Schafer (New Look, value and need shaping perception).
  • Q92: Which theory emphasises internal mental processes → Cognitive psychology. Distractors: behaviourism (bans internal processes), psychoanalysis (unconscious drives), humanistic (free will, self-actualisation).
  • Q45: Which model says memory is a network of interconnected nodes → Connectionist model (Rumelhart and McClelland, PDP). Distractors: associative model (older, looser idea of association), network model (plain-language trap), linkage model (not a recognised term).

Self-test

  1. Who opened the first psychology lab, where and when? (Wilhelm Wundt, Leipzig, 1879)
  2. Name Titchener's three elements of consciousness. (Sensations, images, affections)
  3. Reporting "an apple" instead of "red, round, shiny" is called what, and whose term is it? (Stimulus error, Titchener)
  4. Who wrote Principles of Psychology (1890) and coined "stream of consciousness"? (William James)
  5. Name the three Chicago functionalists. (Dewey, Angell, Carr)
  6. Which school did Wertheimer, Koffka and Kohler found, and what did it reject? (Gestalt, 1912; rejected elementism and S-R atomism)
  7. Who named cognitive psychology, and in what year? (Ulric Neisser, 1967)
  8. Which school is the "third force", and who are its two key names? (Humanistic psychology; Maslow and Rogers)
2History: tabula rasa, stream of consciousness, first Indian psychology lab ★
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q27, Q73, Q42, Q11

From scratch

Exams love "history firsts" because they are pure name-to-idea links: one stem, one person, three familiar distractors. This topic has three strands: (1) the nature vs nurture argument in philosophy (tabula rasa), (2) William James and the stream of consciousness, and (3) the beginnings of psychology in India at Calcutta. Learn them as a story.

1. Where does knowledge come from? Empiricism vs nativism. Before psychology was a science it was a branch of philosophy, and its biggest question was: is the mind born with knowledge, or does experience write everything on it?

  • Nativism / rationalism says some knowledge is inborn. Plato (ancient Greece) held that the soul already knows the eternal Forms and learning is recollection (anamnesis). Rene Descartes (Discourse on Method, 1637; "cogito ergo sum", I think therefore I am) held that ideas such as God, self, infinity and geometric axioms are innate ideas; he is also the father of mind-body dualism (interactionism, with the pineal gland as the meeting point). Gottfried Leibniz replied to Locke that there is nothing in the intellect that was not first in the senses, "except the intellect itself". Immanuel Kant (Critique of Pure Reason, 1781) said the mind brings built-in categories (space, time, causality, unity) that organise raw sensation; experience fills the categories but does not create them.
  • Empiricism says the mind starts empty and experience supplies everything. Aristotle (De Anima) had compared the mind to a writing tablet on which nothing is yet written, and the Latin phrase tabula rasa ("scraped tablet", blank slate) comes from that tradition. John Locke, in An Essay Concerning Human Understanding (1690), made it the founding claim of British empiricism: the newborn mind is "white paper, void of all characters", with no innate ideas. Ideas arrive by two routes: sensation (the senses report the outer world) and reflection (the mind notices its own operations). Simple ideas combine into complex ones by association.

The empiricist line continues: Thomas Hobbes (often called the father of British empiricism), George Berkeley ("esse est percipi", to be is to be perceived), David Hume (laws of association: resemblance, contiguity, cause and effect), David Hartley (associationism tied to nerve vibrations), James Mill (mental mechanics) and John Stuart Mill ("mental chemistry": a compound idea can have properties its parts lack). That chain flows straight into Ebbinghaus's memory experiments (1885), Pavlov and Thorndike's learning research, and John B. Watson's behaviourism (1913), whose "give me a dozen healthy infants" boast is the tabula rasa taken to its extreme. The nativist line leads to Gestalt psychology (Wertheimer, Kohler, Koffka: the mind organises wholes) and, much later, Noam Chomsky's innate language acquisition device. In perception the same fight reappears: Hermann von Helmholtz was the empiricist (perception by "unconscious inference" from experience) and Ewald Hering the nativist.

Example to fix it: a child fears snakes. Locke: she learned it from a bad experience or from being told. Descartes or Kant: some readiness or structure was already in the mind. Modern psychology says both (interaction of nature and nurture), but the exam asks only "who said blank slate?" → Locke.

2. William James and the stream of consciousness. In 1879 Wilhelm Wundt opened the first formal psychology laboratory at Leipzig, and his student Edward Titchener took the method to Cornell as structuralism: break consciousness into its elements (sensations, images, affections) by trained introspection, the way a chemist breaks matter into atoms. William James at Harvard thought this was a mistake. In The Principles of Psychology (1890, two volumes, about twelve years in the writing), in the chapter "The Stream of Thought", he wrote that consciousness "does not appear to itself chopped up in bits"; it "is nothing jointed; it flows", so a river or stream is the natural metaphor. He listed five characteristics of thought:

James's characteristic (1890)Meaning
PersonalEvery thought is owned by some personal self.
Always changingNo state, once gone, recurs exactly the same.
Sensibly continuousNo real gaps; even after sleep we know the thought is ours.
Deals with objects other than itselfThought is about something (the cognitive function).
SelectiveIt chooses among objects: this is attention and will.

He also said the stream has substantive parts (resting places, like perched birds) and transitive parts (flights between them, like "and", "if", "but"). Why it matters: if consciousness flows and serves the organism's choices, the right question is not "what is it made of?" (structuralism) but "what is it for?" That is functionalism, built on Darwin and developed by John Dewey and James Rowland Angell at Chicago and James McKeen Cattell at Columbia. Other James facts exams use: the James-Lange theory of emotion (James 1884, Carl Lange 1885: we feel afraid because we tremble), the "Jimmy" (Psychology: Briefer Course, 1892), a small Harvard demonstration lab (1875), pragmatism, and the title "father of American psychology". Novelists such as James Joyce and Virginia Woolf later borrowed "stream of consciousness" as the name of a writing style, but the term is James's.

3. Psychology begins in India (Calcutta). Indian academic psychology started at the University of Calcutta. The philosopher Brajendranath Seal, with Vice-Chancellor Sir Ashutosh Mookerjee supporting it, planned an experimental psychology laboratory, and it was set up in 1915 (some books say 1916) under Narendra Nath Sengupta, who had trained at Harvard under Hugo Munsterberg, so it was built on the Wundtian experimental model. The first separate Department of Psychology in India followed at Calcutta in 1916. Sengupta later moved to Lucknow University and established psychology there.

Girindrasekhar Bose (a physician) joined the Calcutta department and earned the first doctorate in psychology from an Indian university (D.Sc., 1921, on the concept of repression, where he set out his own theory of opposite wishes). He founded the Indian Psychoanalytical Society (1922), corresponded with Sigmund Freud, and became head of the Calcutta department after Sengupta left. He also founded the Lumbini Park mental hospital in Calcutta. Sengupta and Bose were the founding figures of the Indian Psychological Association. Because Bose is the larger figure, many Indian guidebooks (and the IHBAS 2024 key) call him "the pioneer of psychology in India" and loosely credit him with the first lab. Other early centres: Mysore, where M.V. Gopalaswamy set up a laboratory (1924), and later Patna and Lucknow.

Two-line summary for Calcutta: Seal planned it, Sengupta ran the lab (1915), Bose was the first doctorate, the psychoanalyst and the "pioneer".

Other history firsts IHBAS-style papers ask:

LOCKE locks nothing in: the newborn mind has no lock and no stored ideas, just blank white paper. Locke = blank.

James Joyce took it from William James: both are "James", both use the stream. Picture William James sitting by a river at Harvard in 1890.

Wundt = Wood blocks, James = Water: Wundt and Titchener build consciousness out of blocks (elements); James says it is water (stream).

S-S-B at Calcutta: Seal (planned), Sengupta (lab 1915), Bose (doctorate, psychoanalysis 1922, "pioneer").

Key distinctions

PairDifference
Locke vs DescartesLocke: no innate ideas, experience writes all. Descartes: innate ideas exist (rationalist). Both are 17th century, which is why they get swapped.
Locke vs KantLocke: mind is passive paper. Kant: mind actively imposes categories on experience.
Empiricism vs rationalismEmpiricism: knowledge from senses (Locke, Berkeley, Hume). Rationalism: knowledge from reason and innate ideas (Descartes, Leibniz, Spinoza).
James vs Wundt / TitchenerJames: consciousness is a flowing whole, ask what it is for (functionalism). Wundt/Titchener: analyse it into elements (structuralism).
Stream of consciousness vs free associationStream: James's description of normal waking thought (1890). Free association: Freud's therapy technique for reaching the unconscious.
Wundt 1879 vs James 1875Wundt's Leipzig lab is the official first research lab; James's 1875 Harvard room was for teaching demonstrations.
Sengupta vs BoseSengupta: experimentalist who ran the first lab (1915). Bose: first Indian psychology doctorate, psychoanalyst, Indian Psychoanalytical Society (1922), labelled "pioneer".
Calcutta vs MysoreCalcutta: first lab and department (1915/1916). Mysore: Gopalaswamy's lab (1924).

MCQ traps

  • Descartes for tabula rasa: he is the most famous "mind" philosopher, so he tempts, but he argued for innate ideas, the opposite of a blank slate.
  • Kant for tabula rasa: Kant answered the empiricists with innate categories; he is a nativist.
  • Plato for tabula rasa: Plato's learning-as-recollection is the oldest nativism. It is his student Aristotle who gave the tablet image.
  • Wundt for stream of consciousness: Wundt studied consciousness, so the option looks right, but he analysed it into elements; the "stream" was James's argument against that.
  • Titchener for stream or functionalism: Titchener is structuralism, James's opponent. He also coined the names "structuralism" and "functionalism" (1898), which is a separate fact.
  • Jung or Freud for stream: "collective unconscious" and "free association" sound like flow-of-thought terms, but neither is James's stream.
  • Skinner for anything about consciousness: radical behaviourism set consciousness aside as an explanation, so he cannot have coined it.
  • Familiar Indian textbook authors (S.K. Mangal, A.K. Singh, G.S. Rathi): they appear only because students recognise the names from guidebooks; none founded the first lab. Also do not confuse Sengupta and Bose: read whether the stem says "set up the lab" or "pioneer / psychoanalysis".

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q27: In the nature vs nurture debate, which philosopher said the mind is a tabula rasa at birth → John Locke. Distractors: Plato (recollection, nativist), Rene Descartes (innate ideas, rationalist), Immanuel Kant (innate categories). All three are on the nature side; Locke is the only empiricist.
  • Q73: Who coined "stream of consciousness" for the flow of thought → William James (Principles, 1890). Distractors: Carl Jung (collective unconscious), Wilhelm Wundt (elements of consciousness, the view James attacked), B.F. Skinner (rejected consciousness). You missed this one: lock "James = stream = functionalism = Principles 1890".
  • Q11 (same James strand): Functionalism was most closely associated with → William James. Distractors: Edward Titchener (structuralism), John Watson (behaviourism), G. Stanley Hall (first US lab, APA, developmental psychology). If you know James = stream, you get Q11 and Q73 together.
  • Q42: Pioneer of psychology in India who established the first Indian psychological laboratory → official key Girindrasekhar Bose. Distractors: G.S. Rathi, S.K. Mangal and A.K. Singh, all textbook-author-type names, none a founding figure. Strictly, the 1915 Calcutta lab was run by N.N. Sengupta (planned by B.N. Seal), but Sengupta is not among the options, so Bose is the only defensible answer. If Sengupta ever appears next to Bose with "laboratory" in the stem, prefer Sengupta; if the stem says "pioneer" or "psychoanalysis", choose Bose.

Self-test

  1. Who called the newborn mind "white paper, void of all characters"? (John Locke, Essay Concerning Human Understanding, 1690)
  2. Name Locke's two sources of ideas. (Sensation and reflection)
  3. Which philosopher proposed innate categories such as space, time and causality? (Immanuel Kant, Critique of Pure Reason, 1781)
  4. Descartes stands for which two ideas? (Innate ideas and mind-body dualism)
  5. Who coined "stream of consciousness", in which book and year? (William James, The Principles of Psychology, 1890)
  6. Give the five characteristics of thought in James's stream. (Personal, always changing, sensibly continuous, about objects other than itself, selective)
  7. Who ran India's first psychology laboratory, where and when? (N.N. Sengupta, University of Calcutta, 1915, planned by Brajendranath Seal)
  8. Who earned the first Indian doctorate in psychology and founded the Indian Psychoanalytical Society? (Girindrasekhar Bose, D.Sc. 1921, society 1922)
3Perception theorists: Titchener and Bruner's New Look ★
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q9, Q10, Q52

From scratch

Every theory of perception answers one question: how much of what you "see" comes from the stimulus, and how much comes from you? Line the theorists up on that one dial and almost every MCQ in this area becomes a matching exercise. At one end the stimulus does nearly everything (Gibson). At the other end the perceiver does nearly everything: memory, expectation, need and value (Helmholtz, Titchener, Bruner, Gregory). Neisser sits in the middle and joins the two. The Gestalt school is a third answer: the brain organises the stimulus into wholes by fixed built-in laws.

1. Titchener: perception as a compound (structuralism). Edward Bradford Titchener, Wundt's student who carried structuralism to Cornell (An Outline of Psychology, 1896; A Text-Book of Psychology, 1909-1910), treated the mind like chemistry. Consciousness is built from three elements: sensations (the elements of perception), images (the elements of ideas, memories) and affections (the elements of feeling, only pleasant or unpleasant, since Titchener rejected Wundt's three-dimensional theory of feeling). Each element has attributes: quality, intensity, duration, clearness and (for some) extensity. Trained observers found these by introspection.

So what is a perception for Titchener? Not an element at all. It is a complex (compound) of sensory and imaginal elements: a core of present sensations to which images from past experience attach. That is exactly the wording of IHBAS 2024 Q9. Meaning comes through Titchener's context theory of meaning (also called the core-context theory): meaning is not a new element, it is the context, the images that accrue around the sensory core: a few bars of a song (core) call up images of the singer and the film (context), and the compound now "means" that song.

Because meaning was "added" by context, Titchener warned introspectors against the stimulus error: describing the object or its meaning ("I see an apple") instead of the raw conscious contents ("a red, round, glossy patch, bright at one spot"). Remember: stimulus error = Titchener, and it is a sin of introspection, not an illusion.

2. Helmholtz: unconscious inference (19th-century root of top-down). Hermann von Helmholtz (Handbook of Physiological Optics, 1860s) argued that the retinal image is ambiguous, so the brain fills the gap with unconscious inference: rapid, automatic conclusions drawn from past experience. You "infer" that a coin seen at a slant is round and that a far-off person is normal-sized. This is the grandparent of every constructivist theory, and the reason size constancy is learned rather than given.

3. The Gestalt school: the whole is different from the sum of its parts. Max Wertheimer launched Gestalt psychology with his study of the phi phenomenon (apparent movement, 1912), with Wolfgang Kohler and Kurt Koffka. They attacked Titchener directly: a melody stays the same melody when every note is transposed, so perception cannot be a sum of sensory elements. Instead the brain organises input into wholes by laws of perceptual organisation (Wertheimer, 1923): proximity (things close in space or time group together), similarity (alike items group), closure (we complete gaps in a figure), good continuation (we follow smooth lines), common fate (things moving together group together), and the master law of Pragnanz (we see the simplest, most stable organisation). Figure-ground segregation was described by Edgar Rubin (1915, the face-vase figure).

4. The New Look (Bruner and colleagues, late 1940s): need, value and set shape what you see. Jerome Bruner and Leo Postman led a movement that treated the perceiver as a person with needs, values, expectations and personality, not a camera. Their key studies:

  • Bruner and Goodman (1947), "Value and need as organizing factors in perception": ten-year-olds adjusted a circle of light to match the size of coins (1 cent to 50 cents) and of same-size grey cardboard discs. Coins were judged larger than discs; the more valuable the coin, the bigger the overestimate; and poor children overestimated more than rich children. Conclusion: economic need (deprivation) and value enlarge perceived size. This is IHBAS 2024 Q10.
  • Bruner and Postman (1949), the anomalous playing cards: subjects briefly shown a red six of spades or a black four of hearts "normalised" it to an ordinary card, or reported a mixed colour. Expectation (set) overrides the stimulus.
  • Postman, Bruner and McGinnies (1948): words tied to a person's dominant values (on the Allport-Vernon study of values) were recognised at shorter exposures (perceptual sensitisation / selective vigilance).
  • McGinnies (1949): taboo words needed longer exposure to be recognised and produced a GSR before being reported, called perceptual defence.
  • Related motivated-perception work: Schafer and Murphy (1943) rewarded subjects for one of two face profiles in an ambiguous figure-ground drawing, and later that face was seen as the figure more often; McClelland and Atkinson (1948) showed hungry sailors "saw" more food objects in blank or ambiguous slides.

The common thread is perceptual set: a readiness to perceive in a particular way, created by motivation, expectation, past experience, context, emotion and culture. The classic textbook demonstration is Bugelski and Alampay (1961): people shown animal pictures first saw the ambiguous rat-man figure as a rat; people shown faces first saw a man. Bruner later formalised the idea in "On perceptual readiness" (1957): perception is categorisation, and the perceiver tests hypotheses against cues.

5. Gibson: direct (ecological) perception, bottom-up. James J. Gibson (The Perception of the Visual World, 1950; The Ecological Approach to Visual Perception, 1979) said the light reaching a moving observer, the optic array, is already rich enough: no inference, no images, no stored knowledge needed. Perception is direct. The perceiver "picks up" invariants such as texture gradients and optic flow (the streaming of the visual field as you move, which tells a pilot where he is heading). Most famously, the environment offers affordances: what an object lets an animal do (a chair affords sitting, a handle affords grasping). Gibson is the pure bottom-up (data-driven) position. Do not confuse him with his wife Eleanor Gibson, whose visual cliff study with Richard Walk (1960) tested depth perception in infants.

6. Gregory: perception as hypothesis (constructivist, top-down). Richard Gregory (Eye and Brain, 1966; "Perceptions as hypotheses", 1980) revived Helmholtz: sensory data are incomplete, so the brain builds the best hypothesis using stored knowledge. Evidence: visual illusions (Gregory explained the Muller-Lyer illusion as misapplied size constancy) and the hollow-face illusion, where knowledge that faces are convex beats the depth cues. Gregory is the clearest modern top-down (concept-driven) theorist.

7. Neisser: the perceptual cycle (both directions at once). Ulric Neisser, who named the field in Cognitive Psychology (1967), proposed the perceptual cycle in Cognition and Reality (1976): a schema (top-down) directs exploration; exploration samples the actual environment (bottom-up); the information picked up modifies the schema, which directs the next exploration. So Neisser reconciles Gibson and Gregory: perception is continuous, active and cyclical.

PositionWhoDriven bySignature words

Gestalt organisationWertheimer, Kohler, KoffkaInnate laws of the wholeproximity, closure, Pragnanz, phi

Direct / ecologicalJ. J. GibsonStimulus alone (bottom-up)affordance, optic flow, optic array

Perceptual cycleNeisserSchema and world in a loopschema, exploration, sampling

Who said what

Titchener's "TIC" recipe: a percept is a Tune of sensations, Images added, Context gives meaning. Sensory + imaginal + context = Titchener.

Bruner = "Brune-r the poor kid's coin": picture a poor child staring at a coin that swells like a balloon. Need and value inflate size: Bruner and Goodman, 1947.

Gibson Gives (affords): the world "gives" you everything directly. Gregory Guesses: the brain guesses a hypothesis. Neisser Never stops: the cycle keeps going round.

Helmholtz Hides the inference: unconscious, automatic.

Key distinctions

  • Titchener vs Bruner: both say perception is more than sensation. Titchener adds images and context (structural elements found by introspection); Bruner adds needs, values and expectations (motives, found by experiment). Elements and "imaginal" = Titchener; deprivation, value, set = Bruner.
  • Titchener vs Gestalt: Titchener builds the percept up from elements; Gestaltists say the whole comes first and cannot be reduced to elements.
  • Bottom-up vs top-down: Gibson (direct, data-driven) vs Gregory and Helmholtz (constructive, concept-driven). Neisser's cycle combines both.
  • Gregory vs Bruner: both top-down. Gregory's top-down input is knowledge (hypotheses, illusions); Bruner's New Look stresses motivation and value.
  • Bruner and Goodman vs Segall, Campbell and Herskovits: both show perception is not universal. Bruner and Goodman: economic need changes perceived size. Segall et al.: culture and ecology change susceptibility to illusions.
  • Perceptual defence vs perceptual sensitisation: defence (McGinnies) raises the threshold for threatening words; sensitisation (Postman, Bruner and McGinnies) lowers it for valued words.
  • J. J. Gibson vs Eleanor Gibson: James = affordances and optic flow; Eleanor = visual cliff with Walk.
  • Stimulus error vs illusion: the stimulus error is a reporting mistake in introspection (Titchener); an illusion is a genuine misperception (Muller-Lyer, Ponzo).

MCQ traps

  • Bruner on a definition question: Bruner is the most famous perception name, so he pulls you in. But he explained how motives bias perception; he never defined perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal elements. That vocabulary is structuralist, so Titchener.
  • Kimble (often misspelled "Kimbel"): Gregory Kimble gave the classic definition of learning (relatively permanent change in behaviour potentiality). A definition stem tempts you to pick any "definition person"; check whether the stem is about learning or perception.
  • Schafer: worked with Murphy (1943) on reward and figure-ground, a motivated-perception near-miss. Not the structuralist definition and not the coin study.
  • Segall and Herskovits: culture and illusions (carpentered world, 1966), not economic deprivation. "Hers Kovits" in the paper is just a split spelling of Herskovits.
  • Broota and Ganguli: Indian names inserted for familiarity; neither did the coin study.
  • Gibson on a "hypothesis" or "inference" stem: wrong; Gibson explicitly denied inference. Hypothesis = Gregory (or Bruner); inference = Helmholtz.
  • Wundt for "stimulus error" or "context theory": Wundt founded the Leipzig lab (1879), but these specific terms are Titchener's.
  • Neisser as "father of direct perception": no; Neisser is the perceptual cycle and the 1967 book naming cognitive psychology. Direct perception is J. J. Gibson.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q9: Who defined perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal events? → Titchener. Distractors: Kimbel (Kimble, a learning definition), Bruner (New Look, motives shape perception), Schafer (reward and figure-ground with Murphy). You picked Bruner; the cue "sensory and imaginal" is structuralist vocabulary and points only to Titchener.
  • Q10: Who demonstrated that economic deprivation affects perception? → Bruner and Goodman (1947 coin study: poor children overestimated coin size more). Distractors: Broota and Ganguli (Indian names, no such study), Segall and "Hers Kovits" (Herskovits), whose work is culture and visual illusions, not need.
  • Q52 (related, Gestalt): items close together in space and time are perceived as a group → Proximity (Wertheimer). Distractors continuity, similarity and closure are other Gestalt laws with different grouping cues.

Self-test

  1. Who defined perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal elements? (Edward B. Titchener)
  2. What is Titchener's "stimulus error"? (Reporting the object or its meaning instead of the raw sensations during introspection)
  3. Name Titchener's theory of how a percept gets meaning. (Context, or core-context, theory of meaning)
  4. Who ran the 1947 coin-size study, and what did poor children do? (Bruner and Goodman; poor children overestimated coin size more than rich children)
  5. Who coined "affordances" and argued perception is direct? (James J. Gibson)
  6. Who said perceptions are hypotheses and used illusions as evidence? (Richard Gregory)
  7. Whose 19th-century idea is "unconscious inference"? (Hermann von Helmholtz)
  8. Who proposed the perceptual cycle of schema, exploration and sampling? (Ulric Neisser, 1976)
4Gestalt laws of perceptual organisation
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q52

From scratch

Gestalt psychology was founded in Germany by Max Wertheimer with Kurt Koffka and Wolfgang Kohler. The starting point was Wertheimer's 1912 paper on the phi phenomenon: two lights flashed in quick succession are seen as one light moving, a motion that exists in neither light alone. Their slogan: "the whole is different from (more than) the sum of its parts." This was a direct attack on Titchener's elementism. "Gestalt" means form or configuration. In 1923 Wertheimer listed the principles by which the brain automatically groups elements into wholes.

Figure-ground is the most basic organisation: we split a scene into a figure (the object, which seems in front, has a shape and contour) and a ground (the shapeless background behind it). Edgar Rubin (1915) showed it with the reversible vase-faces figure: you see either two faces or a vase, never both at once. The grouping laws are: Proximity: elements near each other in space or time are grouped (six dots spaced as three pairs look like three pairs). Similarity: elements alike in colour, size or shape are grouped (alternating rows of red and black dots look like rows). Closure: we fill gaps to perceive a complete figure (a circle with a gap is still seen as a circle; the Kanizsa triangle). Good continuation / continuity: we follow smooth, continuous lines rather than abrupt turns (two crossing curves are seen as two lines, not four pieces). Common fate: elements moving together in the same direction are grouped (a flock of birds, a "wave" in a stadium). Pragnanz (law of good figure, simplicity) is the master law: we perceive the simplest, most stable, most regular organisation possible (overlapping rings are seen as five circles, the Olympic logo, not complex odd shapes).

Later additions (Stephen Palmer, 1990s) are common region (items inside the same boundary group) and connectedness (physically linked items group). The Gestaltists explained these laws by isomorphism: brain fields mirror the structure of experience. Their influence extends to Kohler's insight learning in chimpanzees (Sultan and the sticks), Lewin's field theory, and Gestalt therapy (Fritz Perls, which is a separate thing).

Example from real life: a page of text is readable because proximity groups letters into words, and closure lets you read faded print. Logo designers use closure (the WWF panda) and figure-ground (the arrow in the FedEx logo).

Key distinctions

LawGrouping basisExample
ProximityNearness in space or timeDots in pairs seen as pairs
SimilaritySame colour, shape, sizeRows of same-coloured dots
ClosureFilling gaps to completeBroken circle seen whole
ContinuitySmooth continuous pathTwo crossing lines, not four segments
Common fateMoving togetherFlock of birds
PragnanzSimplest, most stable formOlympic rings as circles
Figure-groundObject vs backgroundRubin's vase-faces

MCQ traps

  • "Close together" means proximity, not closure. The word "close" in the stem tempts you to closure; closure is about completing gaps.
  • "In space and time" is the proximity wording; time is included (two taps close in time are heard as a pair).
  • Similarity vs common fate: if the shared feature is motion direction, it is common fate, even though moving together is a kind of "similarity".
  • Pragnanz is the overall law, sometimes called "law of good figure" or "law of simplicity"; do not mistake it for closure.
  • Gestalt founders vs Gestalt therapy: Wertheimer, Koffka, Kohler are perception; Fritz Perls is therapy. Rubin (figure-ground) was Danish, not a founder.
  • Phi phenomenon vs beta movement: both are apparent motion; Wertheimer's 1912 paper is the phi phenomenon.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q52: Items close together in space and time are perceived as belonging together, which law → Proximity, because the grouping cue is nearness; continuity, similarity and closure group by path, likeness and gap-filling.
5Attention: the Stroop effect
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q33

From scratch

John Ridley Stroop (1935, Journal of Experimental Psychology) showed people colour words printed in coloured ink. In the key condition the word and ink conflict (the word RED printed in blue ink), and the task is to name the ink colour ("blue") and ignore the word. People are markedly slower and make more errors on these incongruent trials than on neutral trials (coloured rectangles or XXXX) or congruent trials (RED in red ink). The delay is the Stroop interference effect. Reading the word aloud while ignoring the ink is barely affected, an asymmetry that matters.

The explanation is about attention and cognitive control. Reading is a highly practised, automatic process: fast, effortless and hard to suppress. Colour naming is slower and more controlled. When the two point to different responses, the automatic word response must be inhibited while attention is held on the relevant dimension (ink). Models include the speed-of-processing account (the word arrives first and must be suppressed), the automaticity account (Shiffrin and Schneider, 1977, automatic vs controlled processing), and Cohen, Dunbar and McClelland's (1990) connectionist model, where stronger word pathways win unless top-down attention boosts the colour pathway. So the Stroop effect is the classic demonstration of selective attention failing against automatic processing.

Clinically, the Stroop is a standard test of executive function: response inhibition, interference control and selective attention. It relies on the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and the anterior cingulate cortex (conflict monitoring). Performance is impaired in frontal lobe lesions, ADHD, schizophrenia and dementia. Versions include the Golden Stroop Color and Word Test and the Victoria Stroop. The emotional Stroop asks people to name ink colours of emotional words: anxious patients are slower on threat words ("spider" for spider phobics, "trauma" words in PTSD), which shows attentional bias toward threat. Other variants: numerical Stroop, spatial Stroop.

Example: in an exam hall, if a sign says "EXIT" in red and an arrow points the other way, the momentary hesitation you feel is Stroop-like conflict between automatic reading and the task you are attending to.

Key distinctions

TermMeaningDo not confuse with
Stroop effectSlower ink naming when word conflictsSimon effect (spatial response conflict)
Emotional StroopSlower on threat words: attentional biasDot-probe task (also attentional bias, different method)
Automatic processFast, effortless, hard to stop (reading)Controlled process (colour naming)
Cocktail party effectHearing your name in an unattended channel (Cherry, 1953)Stroop (visual, response conflict)
Flanker taskInterference from surrounding arrows/letters (Eriksen)Stroop (interference within one stimulus)

MCQ traps

  • "Perception" looks right because colours must be seen, but perception of both the word and the ink is intact; the bottleneck is attentional selection and response inhibition.
  • "Memory retrieval" is tempting because word meaning is retrieved from memory, but no recall task is involved.
  • "Language development" is only there because reading is used; the effect measures attention in literate adults.
  • If the options include executive function / response inhibition and attention, both are defensible; in basic MCQs "selective attention" is the textbook answer.
  • The effect is strongest when naming ink, not when reading the word; a reversed stem is a trap.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q33: The Stroop effect is most closely associated with which process → Attention, because the slowing comes from having to inhibit automatic reading while attending to ink colour.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Basics-level students know "Stroop = attention" but slip on three points. First, the direction: the interference is on naming the ink, not on reading the word (reading is automatic, so the ink barely slows it; this asymmetry is the whole point). Second, they confuse the Stroop with other conflict tasks: the flanker task (Eriksen and Eriksen, 1974) puts conflict in neighbouring items, the Simon effect (J. R. Simon) puts it in the location of the response. Third, they forget the theorists behind the explanation: Shiffrin and Schneider (1977) for automatic vs controlled processing, and Cohen, Dunbar and McClelland (1990) for the connectionist pathway model.

TheoristYearIdea
John Ridley Stroop1935Ink-naming slows when the colour word conflicts (Stroop interference effect)
Shiffrin and Schneider1977Automatic (fast, effortless) vs controlled (slow, effortful) processing; reading is automatic
Cohen, Dunbar and McClelland1990Connectionist model: strong word pathway wins unless attention boosts the colour pathway
Eriksen and Eriksen1974Flanker task: interference from surrounding letters or arrows
J. R. SimonSimon effect: slower when stimulus location and response side mismatch

Donald Broadbent1958Filter theory: early selection of attention
Anne Treisman1964Attenuation theory: unattended input is turned down, not blocked
  1. Which process is slowed in the Stroop task, word reading or ink naming? (ink naming; word reading is barely affected)
  2. Who framed reading as an automatic process and colour naming as controlled? (Shiffrin and Schneider, 1977)
  3. Interference from arrows on either side of the target arrow is called? (the flanker effect, Eriksen and Eriksen)
  4. Anxious patients slow on threat words in which Stroop variant, showing what? (the emotional Stroop; attentional bias toward threat)
  5. Which two brain regions does Stroop performance lean on? (dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and anterior cingulate cortex (conflict monitoring))
6Memory: serial position, interference, amnesia, context-dependent memory
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q71, Q95, Q76, Q63, Q82

From scratch

Serial position effect. If you hear a list of 15 to 20 words once and recall them in any order (free recall), accuracy plotted by position forms a U-shaped curve. The first items are recalled well (primacy effect) and so are the last few (recency effect); the middle sags. The curve was first noted by Ebbinghaus (1885) and analysed by Murdock (1962); Glanzer and Cunitz (1966) separated the two parts. Primacy reflects long-term memory: early items get more rehearsal. Slowing the presentation rate improves primacy only. Recency reflects short-term memory: the last items are still in the buffer. A 30-second distractor task (counting backwards) abolishes recency and leaves primacy intact. This double dissociation supported Atkinson and Shiffrin's (1968) multi-store model.

Interference theory says forgetting happens because memories compete, not just because traces fade (decay, Ebbinghaus's curve of forgetting). Retroactive interference: new learning disrupts recall of old learning (after learning your new phone number you cannot recall the old one). Proactive interference: old learning disrupts recall of new learning (you keep writing last year's date in January). Mnemonic: "pro" = forward (old pushes forward onto new); "retro" = backward (new reaches back onto old). Jenkins and Dallenbach (1924) showed less forgetting after sleep than after waking activity, evidence for interference over decay. Underwood (1957) showed proactive interference explains much forgetting in the lab. Release from PI (Wickens) occurs when the category of the material changes.

Amnesia is named by its direction relative to the onset (injury, illness). Anterograde amnesia: inability to form new long-term memories after onset. The classic case is H.M. (Henry Molaison), who lost both hippocampi in 1953 surgery (Scoville and Milner, 1957); he could not learn new facts but kept old memories, short-term memory and procedural learning (mirror drawing). Retrograde amnesia: loss of memories formed before onset, usually graded so that recent memories are lost and older ones spared (Ribot's law), as after head injury or ECT. Korsakoff syndrome (chronic alcohol use, thiamine/B1 deficiency, damage to mammillary bodies and dorsomedial thalamus) gives severe anterograde plus some retrograde amnesia and confabulation, usually following Wernicke's encephalopathy (confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia). Dissociative amnesia (DSM-5-TR) is psychological, not organic: loss of autobiographical information after trauma.

Context-dependent memory is a case of Tulving's encoding specificity principle (Tulving and Thomson, 1973): recall is best when cues at retrieval match those present at encoding. Godden and Baddeley (1975) had divers learn words underwater or on land; recall was about 40% better when learning and testing places matched. State-dependent memory is the internal version (Goodwin and colleagues, alcohol; mood-dependent memory, Bower). Example: you forget why you walked into the kitchen, then remember once you go back to the bedroom.

Key distinctions

PairFirstSecond
Primacy vs recencyFirst items; LTM; helped by slow rateLast items; STM; killed by delay
Retroactive vs proactiveNew disrupts oldOld disrupts new
Anterograde vs retrogradeCannot form new memories after onsetLoses memories from before onset
Context vs state dependentExternal place cuesInternal physiological or mood state
Interference vs decay vs retrieval failureCompeting memoriesTrace fades with disuse / cue missing (tip of tongue)

MCQ traps

  • Direction swap: "interference from new information" is retroactive; students pick proactive because "new" sounds forward-looking.
  • "Inability to retrieve old memories" is retrograde; it is the decoy on any anterograde question.
  • Made-up labels like "list position effect" or "order recall effect" sound plausible; the recognised names are serial position and primacy-recency.
  • Principle of congruity (Osgood and Tannenbaum) is an attitude-change theory; it is not about matching study and test rooms.
  • Korsakoff vs Wernicke: Wernicke's is the acute triad; Korsakoff's is the chronic amnesic stage. Korsakoff's hallmark is anterograde amnesia with confabulation.
  • The hippocampus, not the amygdala, is the "new memories" structure (IHBAS Q43 tests this too).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q71: Why the first and last list items are remembered better → Primacy-recency effect (serial position), with primacy from rehearsal into LTM and recency from STM.
  • Q95: Difficulty recalling due to interference from new information → Retroactive interference, because the new material works backward on the old.
  • Q76: Anterograde amnesia means → The inability to form new memories, as in H.M. after hippocampal removal.
  • Q63: Severe memory impairment and confusion from chronic alcohol use → Korsakoff's syndrome, a thiamine-deficiency amnesia with marked anterograde loss and confabulation.
  • Q82: Rahul does better when the exam is in his usual classroom → The principle of encoding specificity, since the room acts as a retrieval cue (context-dependent memory).

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

The basics are familiar, but three slips cost marks. (1) Direction swaps: retroactive = new disrupts old, proactive = old disrupts new; and anterograde = cannot form new, retrograde = loses the past. (2) Which manipulation kills which half of the serial position curve: a 30-second distractor wipes out recency (STM), a slower rate boosts primacy (LTM) only, as Glanzer and Cunitz (1966) showed. (3) Matching names to findings: context-dependent recall is Godden and Baddeley's divers, but the principle behind it is Tulving and Thomson's (1973) encoding specificity; Korsakoff is mostly anterograde with confabulation, not a pure retrograde loss. Also keep decay (Ebbinghaus) separate from interference and from retrieval failure (cue missing, as in tip-of-the-tongue), and keep context-dependent (external place) separate from state-dependent memory (internal drug or mood state, as in Bower's mood work).

Benton Underwood1957Proactive interference explains much lab forgetting

Scoville and Milner1957H.M.: bilateral hippocampal removal → anterograde amnesia
Sergei KorsakoffAlcoholic amnesic syndrome: thiamine deficiency, mammillary bodies, confabulation
  1. A 30-second counting-backwards task abolishes which effect? (recency; primacy survives)
  2. You keep using your old password when logging in with the new one. Which interference? (proactive (old disrupts new))
  3. Whose sleep study supported interference over decay? (Jenkins and Dallenbach, 1924)
  4. Recent memories lost, older ones spared after head injury: name the law. (Ribot's law (temporally graded retrograde amnesia))
  5. Context-dependent memory is an instance of which principle, by whom? (encoding specificity, Tulving and Thomson 1973)
7Semantic network models of memory
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q45

From scratch

Semantic memory (Tulving, 1972) is your store of general knowledge: facts, word meanings and concepts, with no record of when or where you learned them ("a canary is a bird"). It contrasts with episodic memory (personal, time-and-place-stamped events). Network models try to explain how this knowledge is organised and searched. The core idea: every concept is a node, and concepts are joined by links (associations). Remembering means moving through, or activating, this web.

Collins and Quillian (1969): the hierarchical network model. Concepts sit in a tree arranged by category level: ANIMAL at the top, BIRD and FISH below it, CANARY and OSTRICH below BIRD. Links are of two kinds: "is a" links (canary is a bird) and property links (bird can fly; animal has skin). The model assumes cognitive economy: a property is stored only once, at the highest node it applies to ("has skin" is stored at ANIMAL, not repeated at CANARY). Their evidence was the sentence verification task: people verified "A canary can sing" (same level) faster than "A canary can fly" (one level up) faster than "A canary has skin" (two levels up). Reaction time grew with the number of links traversed.

The model failed on two findings. (1) The typicality effect: "A robin is a bird" is verified faster than "A penguin is a bird", though both are one link away. (2) Some category statements violate the hierarchy: "A chicken is an animal" is verified faster than "A chicken is a bird", even though ANIMAL is further up. Pure link-counting cannot explain either.

Collins and Loftus (1975): spreading activation model. They dropped the strict hierarchy. Concepts form a looser web where link length reflects semantic relatedness (short link from FIRE ENGINE to RED and TRUCK, longer to SUNSET). When a node is activated, activation spreads outward along links, weakening with distance and time. Closely related, typical items are reached quickly, which explains typicality. It also explains semantic priming: Meyer and Schvaneveldt (1971) showed people decide that "nurse" is a word faster after seeing "doctor" than after "bread", because "doctor" has already sent activation to "nurse". Example: hearing "ambulance" makes "hospital" and "siren" pop to mind faster.

Connectionist / Parallel Distributed Processing (PDP) model (Rumelhart, McClelland and the PDP group, 1986). Knowledge is a network of simple neuron-like units joined by weighted connections, but a concept is not one node: it is a pattern of activation distributed across many units, and memory lives in the connection weights. Processing runs in parallel, learning means adjusting weights (e.g. backpropagation), and damage produces graceful degradation rather than total loss of one fact. A rival non-network account is the feature comparison model (Smith, Shoben and Rips, 1974), which compares lists of defining and characteristic features.

Key distinctions

ModelWho, whenStructureSignature evidence or idea
Hierarchical networkCollins and Quillian, 1969Tree of categories, "is a" and property linksCognitive economy; RT rises with levels crossed
Spreading activationCollins and Loftus, 1975Non-hierarchical web; link length = relatednessSemantic priming, typicality effect
Connectionist / PDPRumelhart and McClelland, 1986Units with weighted links; distributed patternsParallel processing, graceful degradation, learning by weight change
Feature comparisonSmith, Shoben and Rips, 1974Not a network; feature listsDefining vs characteristic features
ACT / ACT-RAnderson, 1976 onwardPropositional network plus production rulesFan effect

MCQ traps

  • "Network model" vs "connectionist model": both sound right for "nodes in a network". IHBAS keyed connectionist, the formal textbook name for interconnected-node, parallel architecture. If the stem says "hierarchical" or "cognitive economy", the answer is Collins and Quillian instead.
  • Spreading activation belongs to Collins and Loftus (1975), not Collins and Quillian (1969). The pair names share "Collins", so examiners swap them.
  • Typicality effect is evidence against the hierarchical model and for spreading activation; do not list it as support for Collins and Quillian.
  • Semantic vs episodic: network models are about semantic memory (Tulving). "Remembering your 10th birthday" is episodic and is not what these models describe.
  • "Associative model" is a loose historical idea (Aristotle, British associationists), not the formal node-and-weight model.
  • Priming is unconscious and implicit; it is the classic experimental support for spreading activation, not for levels of processing (Craik and Lockhart, 1972).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q45: Which model proposes memory is organised as a network of interconnected nodes? → Connectionist model, because connectionism (PDP) is the standard term for interconnected, weighted-node architecture; "network model" is the tempting plain-language distractor (textbook note: Collins and Quillian's model is also called a semantic network model, so read the options carefully and go with the official key, connectionist).

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Students at level 2 know "memory as a network" but blur three things. (1) The two Collins models: Collins and Quillian (1969) is the strict hierarchy with cognitive economy; Collins and Loftus (1975) is the loose web with spreading activation. (2) Which finding supports which model: the typicality effect (robin faster than penguin) and semantic priming (Meyer and Schvaneveldt, 1971) count against the hierarchy and for spreading activation. (3) In a connectionist/PDP model (Rumelhart and McClelland, 1986) a concept is a distributed pattern across many units, not a single node; that is the feature IHBAS keyed in Q45. Watch for distractors that are real theories but not network models at all: feature comparison (Smith, Shoben and Rips, 1974) uses feature lists, and levels of processing (Craik and Lockhart, 1972) is about depth of encoding. Finally, all of these describe semantic memory (Tulving, 1972), never episodic memory of personal events.

TheoristYearIdea
Endel Tulving1972Semantic vs episodic memory distinction
Collins and Quillian1969Hierarchical network; cognitive economy; sentence verification RTs
Collins and Loftus1975Spreading activation; link length = relatedness
Meyer and Schvaneveldt1971Semantic priming in lexical decision (doctor → nurse)
Rumelhart, McClelland and the PDP group1986Connectionist / parallel distributed processing; graceful degradation
Smith, Shoben and Rips1974Feature comparison model (defining vs characteristic features), not a network
John Anderson1976ACT model: propositional network plus productions; fan effect
Craik and Lockhart1972Levels of processing (a distractor, not a network model)
  1. Cognitive economy belongs to which model and authors? (hierarchical network, Collins and Quillian 1969)
  2. Which model says activation spreads along links whose length reflects relatedness? (spreading activation, Collins and Loftus 1975)
  3. Does the typicality effect support or refute Collins and Quillian? (refutes it; it supports spreading activation)
  4. In PDP models, where is a memory stored? (in the connection weights, as a distributed activation pattern)
  5. Defining vs characteristic features is which model, by whom? (feature comparison, Smith, Shoben and Rips 1974)
8Classical vs operant conditioning, extinction
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q65, Q77, Q100, Q7

From scratch

Classical (Pavlovian, respondent) conditioning was discovered by Ivan Pavlov (Nobel 1904 for digestion; conditioning work published 1927, Conditioned Reflexes). An unconditioned stimulus (UCS), food, naturally produces an unconditioned response (UCR), salivation. A neutral stimulus, a bell, is repeatedly presented just before the food. After pairings the bell becomes a conditioned stimulus (CS) that alone produces salivation, now called the conditioned response (CR). The learner is passive: an involuntary reflex comes under the control of a new signal. Best timing is delay conditioning (CS starts before and overlaps UCS); backward conditioning (UCS before CS) works poorly. Watson and Rayner (1920) used it to condition fear in Little Albert (white rat + loud noise). Rescorla (1968) showed what matters is the CS predicting the UCS (contingency), not mere pairing; Garcia showed taste aversion can form in one trial with a long delay (biological preparedness).

Operant (instrumental) conditioning starts with Thorndike's Law of Effect (1898, cats in puzzle boxes): responses followed by satisfying consequences are stamped in. B.F. Skinner (1938, The Behavior of Organisms) systematised it with the Skinner box. Here the learner is active: a voluntary response is strengthened or weakened by its consequence. Reinforcement increases behaviour, punishment decreases it. Positive means adding a stimulus, negative means removing one. So negative reinforcement (removing a headache by taking a pill) increases pill-taking; it is NOT punishment. Complex behaviour is built by shaping (reinforcing successive approximations). Skinner's philosophy is radical behaviourism: even thoughts are behaviour controlled by environmental contingencies.

Schedules of reinforcement: continuous reinforcement gives fastest learning; partial schedules are fixed ratio (FR, piecework), variable ratio (VR, slot machine), fixed interval (FI, scalloped pattern, monthly salary) and variable interval (VI, checking email). VR gives the highest, steadiest responding and the greatest resistance to extinction (the partial reinforcement extinction effect).

Extinction means the learned response weakens when its support is removed. Classical: the CS is presented repeatedly without the UCS (bell, no food), so salivation fades. Operant: the response is no longer reinforced (lever-pressing, no pellet), so it fades, often after a brief extinction burst. Extinction is new inhibitory learning, not erasure: after a rest the response returns (spontaneous recovery), it can return in a new context (renewal, Bouton) or after the UCS alone (reinstatement). Related phenomena: generalisation (responding to similar stimuli) and discrimination (responding only to the trained one).

Clinical use. Exposure and Response Prevention (ERP) for OCD (Victor Meyer, 1966; refined by Edna Foa): the patient faces the obsessional cue and is prevented from ritualising, so anxiety habituates and the cue-anxiety link extinguishes; the negatively reinforced ritual also extinguishes. Systematic desensitisation (Wolpe, 1958) instead uses reciprocal inhibition / counterconditioning: relaxation is paired with a fear hierarchy. Token economy (Ayllon and Azrin, 1968) is operant.

Key distinctions

FeatureClassicalOperant
Key figuresPavlov, WatsonThorndike, Skinner
What is associatedStimulus with stimulus (CS-UCS)Response with consequence
Response typeInvoluntary, elicited reflexVoluntary, emitted behaviour
LearnerPassiveActive
Extinction procedureCS without UCSResponse without reinforcer
Therapy examplesSystematic desensitisation, aversion therapy, ERP (habituation and extinction)Token economy, shaping, contingency management

MCQ traps

  • "Classical relies on reinforcement" is wrong; classical pairs two stimuli, operant relies on reinforcement and punishment.
  • Negative reinforcement is not punishment: both reinforcements increase behaviour.
  • Extinction is not forgetting; spontaneous recovery proves the original learning survives.
  • ERP = habituation and extinction, while reciprocal inhibition / counterconditioning = Wolpe's systematic desensitisation. "Graded exposure" is a technique, not the principle.
  • Generalisation vs discrimination vs shaping are all real terms placed as distractors for extinction; match the definition exactly.
  • Theory matching: Skinner = radical behaviourism; Dollard and Miller (1950) = early social learning (Freud in Hullian terms); Bandura and Walters (1963) = later social learning; Maslow = self-actualisation.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q65: Main difference between classical and operant conditioning? → Classical pairs two stimuli; operant relies on reinforcement and punishment, because that is the stimulus-stimulus vs response-consequence split.
  • Q77: Previously reinforced behaviour no longer reinforced and gradually fades? → Extinction, since withholding the reinforcer weakens the operant.
  • Q100: ERP is based on which behaviour therapy principle? → Habituation and extinction, because anxiety fades with prolonged exposure while the ritual is blocked (not reciprocal inhibition, which is Wolpe).
  • Q7: Match theorists to theories → A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2 (Dollard and Miller early social learning, Skinner radical behaviourism, Bandura and Walters later social learning, Maslow self-actualisation).
9Sheldon's somatotypes and Rotter's locus of control
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q8, Q58

From scratch

Type theories sort people into discrete categories. The oldest is Hippocrates (about 400 BCE) and Galen's four humours: sanguine (blood, cheerful), choleric (yellow bile, irritable), melancholic (black bile, sad) and phlegmatic (phlegm, sluggish). Ernst Kretschmer (1921, Physique and Character) linked body build to psychosis: pyknic (short, round) with manic-depressive illness, asthenic / leptosomic (thin) with schizophrenia, athletic (muscular), and dysplastic (mixed, disproportionate).

William Sheldon (1940, Varieties of Human Physique; 1942, Varieties of Temperament) refined this using photographs of thousands of male college students. He named three body components after the embryonic germ layers and paired each with a temperament:

  • Endomorph (soft, round, digestive viscera dominate) → viscerotonia: relaxed, sociable, loves food, comfort and affection, even-tempered.
  • Mesomorph (muscular, broad-shouldered) → somatotonia: assertive, energetic, adventurous, loves physical activity and dominance, noisy, callous, competitive, aggressive.
  • Ectomorph (thin, fragile, large nervous system relative to mass) → cerebrotonia: restrained, introverted, hypersensitive, private, intellectual, prefers solitude.

Each person is rated 1 to 7 on all three, so a somatotype is a three-digit code: 7-1-1 is an extreme endomorph, 1-7-1 an extreme mesomorph, 4-4-4 balanced. Sheldon reported correlations near .80, but his ratings were made by the same people who knew the physiques (rater bias); later studies found weak links. Remember it as historically important, scientifically weak. Modern "types" you should keep separate: Jung's introvert/extravert (1921), Friedman and Rosenman's Type A / Type B (1959, coronary-prone; hostility is the toxic element), Type C (cancer-prone, suppressed emotion), Denollet's Type D (distressed: negative affectivity plus social inhibition).

Julian Rotter: locus of control. Rotter's social learning theory (1954) says behaviour potential depends on expectancy (will this act get reinforced?) and reinforcement value (how much do I want the reward?) in a given psychological situation. Locus of control (Rotter, 1966) is a generalised expectancy about who controls reinforcement. Internals believe outcomes follow from their own effort and ability ("I failed because I did not study"). Externals believe outcomes depend on luck, fate, chance or powerful others ("the paper was unfair"). It is a continuum, not a type. Measured by the Rotter I-E Scale (1966): 29 forced-choice pairs, 23 scored plus 6 fillers; higher score = more external. Internals tend to seek information, resist influence, achieve more and cope better; externality is linked to depression and helplessness.

Extensions: Levenson's IPC scale splits externality into Powerful others and Chance; Wallston's Multidimensional Health Locus of Control applies it to health behaviour.

Key distinctions

ConceptWhoCore question
Locus of controlRotter, 1966Who controls my outcomes: me or outside forces? (general belief)
Self-efficacyBandura, 1977Can I perform this specific action? (task-specific)
Learned helplessnessSeligman and Maier, 1967Uncontrollable aversives teach that responses do not matter
Attributional styleAbramson, Seligman and Teasdale, 1978Internal/external, stable/unstable, global/specific explanations
Attribution dimensionsWeiner, 1979Locus, stability, controllability of causes for success/failure
Body typesKretschmer 1921 vs Sheldon 1940sPyknic/asthenic/athletic vs endo/ecto/mesomorph

MCQ traps

  • Endomorph vs mesomorph: "fond of physical activity" pulls toward mesomorph; "fond of food and comfort" is endomorph. Noisy, callous, assertive = mesomorph.
  • Ectomorph is not "athletic"; it is thin, sensitive, introverted (cerebrotonia).
  • Kretschmer's terms mixed with Sheldon's: pyknic, asthenic and athletic are Kretschmer, not Sheldon.
  • Locus of control is not self-efficacy: Rotter's is a general belief about where control lies; Bandura's is confidence in performing a specific behaviour.
  • Locus of control is not attribution theory by Heider (1958), though Heider also distinguished internal and external causes.
  • On the I-E Scale a high score means external, a common reversed trap.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q8: Personality type described as noisy, callous and fond of physical activity? → Mesomorph, because Sheldon's somatotonia is assertive, aggressive and action-loving.
  • Q58: Rotter's locus of control describes whether outcomes are controlled by? → External forces or internal efforts, the internal-external generalised expectancy.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Level-2 students can define endomorph and "internal vs external", but lose marks on three fine points. (1) Sheldon's temperament labels: endomorph → viscerotonia, mesomorph → somatotonia, ectomorph → cerebrotonia; and "pyknic, asthenic, athletic, dysplastic" are Kretschmer, not Sheldon. (2) Locus of control is a continuum and a generalised expectancy (Rotter, 1966), not a type, and on the I-E Scale a high score = external. (3) Look-alike constructs: self-efficacy (Bandura, 1977) is task-specific confidence; learned helplessness (Seligman and Maier, 1967) and attribution (Heider, 1958; Weiner, 1979) are separate theories with separate owners. Also remember that Sheldon's ratings were made by raters who could see the physiques, so his high correlations (around .80) are now treated as rater bias; examiners like a "scientifically weak" option. Levenson later split externality into powerful others and chance, and Wallston applied locus of control to health.

TheoristYearIdea

Ernst Kretschmer1921Pyknic (manic-depressive), asthenic (schizophrenia), athletic, dysplastic
Hippocrates and GalenancientFour humours: sanguine, choleric, melancholic, phlegmatic
Julian Rotter1954Social learning theory: expectancy x reinforcement value
Julian Rotter1966Locus of control; I-E Scale (29 forced-choice pairs, 6 fillers)
Albert Bandura1977Self-efficacy: belief one can perform a specific act
