  1. Mesomorph pairs with which Sheldon temperament? (somatotonia: assertive, noisy, callous, active)
  2. Pyknic body type and manic-depressive illness: whose theory? (Kretschmer, 1921)
  3. A high score on the Rotter I-E Scale indicates? (external locus of control)
  4. Confidence in doing one specific task is whose construct? (Bandura's self-efficacy, not Rotter's locus of control)
  5. Locus, stability and controllability of causes: whose model? (Weiner, 1979)
10Positive psychology: savoring, broaden-and-build, flow ★
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q13, Q56

From scratch

For most of the 20th century, clinical psychology was a science of what goes wrong: depression, anxiety, schizophrenia. In 1998, Martin Seligman, in his APA presidential address, argued that psychology had forgotten its other two jobs: making ordinary lives more fulfilling and nurturing talent. He called for a positive psychology, the scientific study of what makes life worth living. In 2000, Seligman and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi edited a special issue of American Psychologist that formally launched the field. Its three pillars: positive subjective experiences (joy, contentment, hope), positive individual traits (character strengths, courage), and positive institutions (families, schools, workplaces that let people thrive). The word itself is older: Abraham Maslow used "positive psychology" as a chapter title in Motivation and Personality (1954), and humanists such as Carl Rogers (the "fully functioning person") had the same spirit, but without the experimental, measurement-based method Seligman demanded.

Seligman's own path: from helplessness to optimism. Seligman first became famous for learned helplessness (Seligman and Maier, 1967): dogs given inescapable shocks later failed to escape even when escape was possible. Humans show the same pattern, and it became a model of depression. The attributional reformulation (Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale, 1978) said what matters is how a person explains bad events. This explanatory style has three dimensions, the "3 Ps": permanence (stable vs temporary), pervasiveness (global vs specific) and personalization (internal vs external). A pessimist says "I failed because I am stupid (internal), I always will be (stable), and I am bad at everything (global)." In Learned Optimism (Seligman, 1990) he argued that optimism can be taught: you learn to dispute pessimistic explanations, using Albert Ellis's ABC model extended to ABCDE (Adversity, Belief, Consequence, Disputation, Energization). Keep this separate from dispositional optimism (Scheier and Carver, 1985), which is a general expectation that good things will happen, measured by the Life Orientation Test (LOT).

Seligman's models of the good life. In Authentic Happiness (Seligman, 2002) he described three routes: the pleasant life (positive emotion), the engaged life (using your strengths, flow) and the meaningful life (serving something bigger than yourself). In Flourish (Seligman, 2011) he widened this into PERMA: Positive emotion, Engagement, Relationships, Meaning, Accomplishment. The goal shifted from "happiness" to well-being / flourishing.

Character strengths: the VIA. Christopher Peterson and Seligman (2004), in Character Strengths and Virtues, built a "manual of the sanities", a positive counterpart to the DSM. The VIA classification (Values in Action) lists 6 virtues containing 24 character strengths: wisdom and knowledge (creativity, curiosity, love of learning...), courage (bravery, persistence...), humanity (love, kindness, social intelligence), justice (fairness, leadership, teamwork), temperance (forgiveness, humility, prudence, self-regulation) and transcendence (gratitude, hope, humor, spirituality, appreciation of beauty). Each person's top few strengths are their signature strengths; Seligman's intervention is to use a signature strength in a new way each day.

Fredrickson's broaden-and-build theory (the Q you missed). Barbara Fredrickson asked a simple question (1998, "What good are positive emotions?"; full statement in American Psychologist, 2001). Negative emotions are easy to explain in evolutionary terms: they narrow the thought-action repertoire to one specific survival action (fear → flee, anger → attack). Positive emotions do not prompt a specific action. Her answer: they broaden momentary thought-action repertoires (joy → urge to play; interest → explore; contentment → savor and integrate; love → all of these in close relationships). Play, exploration and integration, repeated over time, build durable personal resources: physical (health, skills), intellectual (knowledge), social (friendships) and psychological (resilience, optimism). The resources outlast the emotion. Two add-ons:

  • Undoing effect / undoing hypothesis (Fredrickson and Levenson, 1998): after a fear-inducing film raised heart rate and blood pressure, participants who then watched a contentment or amusement clip returned to cardiovascular baseline faster than those who saw a neutral or sad clip. Positive emotion "undoes" the lingering arousal of negative emotion.
  • Upward spiral (Fredrickson): positive emotion → broadened thinking → better coping and more resources → more positive emotion.

A caution: her positivity ratio (Fredrickson and Losada, 2005, a "tipping point" of about 2.9 positive to 1 negative emotion) was shown to rest on faulty mathematics (Brown, Sokal and Friedman, 2013). The broaden-and-build core is unaffected.

Csikszentmihalyi's flow. Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi (Beyond Boredom and Anxiety, 1975; Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience, 1990) studied rock climbers, chess players, surgeons and artists, later using the Experience Sampling Method (pagers beeping at random times so people record what they are doing and feeling). Flow is complete absorption in an activity, and it happens when both challenge and skill are high and matched. Features: clear goals, immediate feedback, intense concentration, merging of action and awareness, sense of control, loss of self-consciousness, transformed sense of time, and an autotelic quality (auto = self, telos = goal: the activity is its own reward). A person who often enters flow has an autotelic personality.

ChallengeSkillState (Csikszentmihalyi)
HighHighFlow
HighLowAnxiety
LowHighBoredom / relaxation
LowLowApathy

Bryant and Veroff's savoring (Q13). Fred Bryant and Joseph Veroff (Savoring: A New Model of Positive Experience, 2007) define savoring as the capacity to attend to, appreciate and enhance the positive experiences in one's life. It is the positive mirror of coping: coping manages negative events, savoring amplifies positive ones. Three time frames: anticipation (future: looking forward to Diwali at home), savoring the moment (present) and reminiscence (past: re-living a happy memory, not a trauma). Four processes: basking (pride, receiving praise), thanksgiving (gratitude), marveling (awe) and luxuriating (physical pleasure). Amplifiers: sharing with others, memory building, sharpening the senses, absorption, self-congratulation. Dampener: kill-joy thinking ("this will not last", "I do not deserve this"). Example: eating a mango slowly, noticing the taste, then telling a friend about it.

Two traditions of well-being. Hedonic well-being (feeling good) vs eudaimonic well-being (functioning well, living in line with one's true self, from Aristotle's eudaimonia).

  • Subjective well-being (SWB), Ed Diener (1984): the hedonic model. SWB = high life satisfaction (cognitive part) + frequent positive affect + infrequent negative affect (affective parts). Measured by the Satisfaction With Life Scale (Diener, Emmons, Larsen and Griffin, 1985), 5 items. Diener is nicknamed "Dr Happiness".
  • Psychological well-being (PWB), Carol Ryff (1989): the eudaimonic model with six dimensions: self-acceptance, positive relations with others, autonomy, environmental mastery, purpose in life, personal growth. Mnemonic: "SPA-EPP".
  • Flourishing, Corey Keyes (2002): the mental health continuum. Mental health is not just the absence of mental illness (the dual continua model: illness and health are separate axes). A person high on emotional, psychological and social well-being is flourishing; one low on them is languishing, even without any diagnosis. Keyes (1998) also defined social well-being (integration, acceptance, contribution, actualization, coherence).

Snyder's hope theory. C. R. Snyder (1991) defined hope as a cognitive set built from two parts aimed at a goal: agency ("willpower": the belief I can start and keep going) and pathways ("waypower": the ability to generate routes to the goal, including detours when blocked). Measured by the Adult Hope Scale (Snyder and colleagues, 1991). Snyder and Shane Lopez also edited the Handbook of Positive Psychology (2002), a standard textbook.

Lyubomirsky and the "how" of happiness. Sonja Lyubomirsky, with Sheldon and Schkade (2005), proposed that individual differences in happiness are roughly 50% genetic set point, 10% life circumstances, 40% intentional activity (the "happiness pie", later criticised as too precise, but widely examined). Her book The How of Happiness (2007) tests interventions such as gratitude, acts of kindness and savoring. She also built the 4-item Subjective Happiness Scale (Lyubomirsky and Lepper, 1999). The 10% figure links to the hedonic treadmill (Brickman and Campbell, 1971): we adapt to good and bad events and drift back to baseline, shown when lottery winners were not much happier than controls (Brickman, Coates and Janoff-Bulman, 1978).

Who said what

TheoristYearTerm / theoryOne-line meaning

Aaron Beck1960s-1976Cognitive distortions (Q56 distractor)Thinking errors in depression; CBT, not positive psychology
Sigmund Freud1890s onwardPsychodynamic theory (Q56 distractor)Unconscious conflict drives behaviour
Abraham Maslow1954First used "positive psychology" (chapter title); self-actualizationHumanistic precursor, not the 1998 founder

Fredrickson = Flower that opens. A flower broadens (opens its petals) in sunshine and builds (grows) over the season. Fred-rick-son: "Fred builds bricks". Positive emotion opens the mind, then builds the house of resources.

Csikszentmihalyi = the river. His name is long and flowing, and so is his idea: flow. Challenge and skill are the two banks; flow happens when both are high.

Bryant and Veroff savor a "BTML" thali: Basking, Thanksgiving, Marveling, Luxuriating, eaten across yesterday, today and tomorrow (reminiscence, moment, anticipation).

Diener = Dinner (feels good, hedonic); Ryff = Rifle-range of six targets (eudaimonic, six dimensions); Snyder's hope = "Will and Ways".

Key distinctions

  • Broaden-and-build (Fredrickson) vs flow (Csikszentmihalyi): broaden-and-build is a theory of what positive emotions do over time; flow is a state of absorption during an activity. In flow you often feel little emotion at the time; enjoyment comes afterwards.
  • Savoring (Bryant and Veroff) vs coping (Lazarus and Folkman): savoring regulates positive experience upward; coping manages stress and negative experience.
  • Savoring vs mindfulness: mindfulness is non-judgmental awareness of any experience; savoring targets positive experience and actively amplifies it.
  • Learned optimism (Seligman, explanatory style) vs dispositional optimism (Scheier and Carver, expectancy): how you explain past events vs what you expect of the future.
  • Learned helplessness (Seligman and Maier, 1967) vs learned optimism (Seligman, 1990): same man, opposite ends of his career.
  • Subjective well-being (Diener, hedonic) vs psychological well-being (Ryff, eudaimonic): feeling good vs functioning well.
  • Hope (Snyder) vs optimism: hope needs both will and routes; optimism is only the expectation that things will turn out well.
  • Flourishing (Keyes) vs PERMA (Seligman, Flourish): both use "flourish"; Keyes's is a diagnostic continuum (flourishing vs languishing), Seligman's is a five-element model.
  • Undoing effect (Fredrickson) vs general adaptation syndrome (Selye): recovery speeded by positive emotion vs the alarm-resistance-exhaustion stress response.

MCQ traps

  • "Flow theory" for Fredrickson: wrong; flow is Csikszentmihalyi. This is exactly the pull that cost you Q56. Fredrickson = broaden-and-build.
  • "Cognitive distortions" for Fredrickson: wrong; that is Aaron Beck's cognitive theory of depression (and David Burns's popular list).
  • "Founded positive psychology" = Maslow or Rogers: wrong for modern MCQs; Maslow coined the phrase in 1954, but Seligman (1998) founded the field.
  • PERMA or learned optimism given to Csikszentmihalyi: wrong; both are Seligman. Csikszentmihalyi co-founded the field but his own theory is flow.
  • Savoring = "minimizing stress through relaxation": wrong; that is stress management. Savoring actively enhances positives.
  • Savoring = "reflecting on past traumas": wrong; reminiscence in savoring means re-living positive memories.
  • Six dimensions of well-being given to Diener: wrong; six dimensions = Ryff. Diener = SWB (satisfaction + affect).
  • Signature strengths / VIA given to Seligman alone or to Snyder: the VIA is Peterson and Seligman (2004); Snyder is hope theory.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q13: Savoring in positive psychology involves? → Enhancing the enjoyment of positive experiences (Bryant and Veroff). Distractors: "avoiding negative experiences" (avoidance coping, passive not active), "minimizing stress through relaxation" (stress management), "reflecting on past traumas" (the opposite of reminiscence, which re-lives positive memories).
  • Q56 (MISSED): Barbara Fredrickson is known for her work on? → Broaden-and-build theory. Distractors: "flow theory" (Csikszentmihalyi, the lure you took), "cognitive distortions" (Beck), "psychodynamic theory" (Freud). Lock in: Fredrickson broadens and builds; Csikszentmihalyi flows.

Self-test

  1. Who is known for broaden-and-build theory? (Barbara Fredrickson, 1998/2001)
  2. Flow is the theory of whom, and when does it occur? (Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi; when high challenge matches high skill)
  3. Who defined savoring, and what are its three time frames? (Bryant and Veroff, 2007; anticipation, savoring the moment, reminiscence)
  4. What does PERMA stand for, and whose is it? (Positive emotion, Engagement, Relationships, Meaning, Accomplishment; Seligman, 2011)
  5. The VIA lists how many strengths and virtues, and who built it? (24 strengths, 6 virtues; Peterson and Seligman, 2004)
  6. Life satisfaction + high positive affect + low negative affect is whose model? (Ed Diener, subjective well-being, 1984)
  7. Agency plus pathways defines what, and whose theory is it? (Hope; C. R. Snyder, 1991)
  8. Flourishing vs languishing on a mental health continuum is whose idea? (Corey Keyes, 2002)
11Stress: Lazarus transactional model, appraisal, job stressors ★
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q89, Q40, Q44

From scratch

The one question behind this whole topic: where does stress live? In the event that hits you, in your body's reaction to it, or in the way you size the event up? Psychology answered this in three historical waves, and every name you must know belongs to one of them. Learn the waves in order and the names fall into place.

WaveStress is...Key names
1. Response (body)what the body doesWalter Cannon (fight-or-flight), Hans Selye (General Adaptation Syndrome)
2. Stimulus (event)what happens to youHolmes and Rahe (SRRS, 1967), Kanner, Coyne, Schaefer and Lazarus (daily hassles, 1981)
3. Transaction (appraisal)the fit between demands and your judged resourcesRichard Lazarus (1966), Lazarus and Susan Folkman (1984)

Wave 1a: Walter Cannon and fight-or-flight. Walter B. Cannon, a Harvard physiologist, described the fight-or-flight response in Bodily Changes in Pain, Hunger, Fear and Rage (1915). Faced with danger, the sympathetic nervous system fires and the adrenal medulla releases adrenaline (epinephrine) and noradrenaline: heart rate and blood pressure rise, breathing speeds up, glucose floods the blood, digestion stops, pupils dilate. The body is readied to fight or to run. Cannon also coined homeostasis (The Wisdom of the Body, 1932): the body keeps its internal variables (temperature, blood sugar) at fixed set points. Fight-or-flight is a fast, short emergency burst.

Wave 1b: Hans Selye and the General Adaptation Syndrome. Hans Selye, an endocrinologist in Montreal, injected rats with many different noxious substances (and exposed them to cold, injury, forced exercise) and found the same pattern every time: enlarged adrenal glands, shrunken thymus and lymph tissue, stomach ulcers. He concluded that the body's stress reaction is non-specific: whatever the stressor, the response is the same. He first reported this in 1936 and popularised it in The Stress of Life (1956). He named the three-stage pattern the General Adaptation Syndrome (GAS):

  1. Alarm reaction: the initial shock (resistance briefly drops below normal) then countershock, with sympathetic arousal and adrenaline, essentially Cannon's fight-or-flight as stage one.
  2. Resistance: if the stressor continues, the body adapts and resistance stays above normal, sustained by the HPA axis (hypothalamus releases CRH, pituitary releases ACTH, adrenal cortex releases cortisol). You look as if you are coping, but resources are being spent.
  3. Exhaustion: with prolonged stress, resources run out, resistance collapses, and "diseases of adaptation" appear (ulcers, hypertension, lowered immunity, even death in his rats).

Selye later separated eustress (good, motivating stress, such as a promotion or a wedding) from distress (harmful stress), in Stress Without Distress (1974). Weakness: it ignores the mind, the gap Lazarus filled.

Wave 2: stress as events you can count. Thomas Holmes and Richard Rahe (1967) built the Social Readjustment Rating Scale (SRRS): 43 life events, each weighted in Life Change Units (LCU) by how much readjustment it demands. Death of a spouse = 100 (top), divorce = 73, marriage = 50 (the anchor event). Positive events also score: change itself demands adjustment. A yearly total above about 300 LCU was linked to a higher risk of illness. Then Allen Kanner, James Coyne, Catherine Schaefer and Richard Lazarus (1981) argued that the small, repeated irritations of daily life, daily hassles, predict health and mood better than rare major events. Their Hassles Scale came with an Uplifts Scale (small positive moments). So: Holmes and Rahe = big life events; Kanner and Lazarus = small daily hassles.

Wave 3: Lazarus's transactional model (the one you missed). Richard Lazarus (Psychological Stress and the Coping Process, 1966) and Lazarus with Susan Folkman (Stress, Appraisal, and Coping, 1984) defined psychological stress as a particular relationship between the person and the environment that is appraised by the person as taxing or exceeding his or her resources and endangering his or her well-being. Stress is not in the event and not in the body; it is in the transaction, decided by cognitive appraisal. Proof: in Lazarus's film studies (Speisman, Lazarus and colleagues, 1964), the same distressing film produced less arousal when the soundtrack framed it intellectually or with denial. Same film, different appraisal, different stress.

The model has three appraisal steps:

  • Primary appraisal: "Is this relevant to me? What is at stake?" The event is judged irrelevant, benign-positive, or stressful. A stressful appraisal comes in three flavours: harm/loss (damage done), threat (future harm) and challenge (chance for gain).
  • Secondary appraisal: "What can I do about it? Do I have the resources to cope?" Here the person evaluates coping options, personal resources (skills, health, money, time, social support) and how likely each option is to work. This is the Q89 answer.
  • Reappraisal: updating both judgements as new information or feedback arrives (the threat turns out smaller, a strategy fails).

Warning: "primary" and "secondary" do not mean first and second in time or in importance. The two run together and feed each other. Stress is high when primary appraisal says "big stakes" and secondary appraisal says "not enough resources".

Worked example: IHBAS entrance in six weeks. Primary: "This decides my career" (threat; or challenge if you feel ready). Secondary: "I have 20 mocks, the notes app and six weeks" (resources adequate, stress drops). Reappraisal after a bad mock score: "My weak area is names, which is fixable" (the threat shrinks again).

Coping is what you do after appraising. Lazarus and Folkman (1984) defined coping as constantly changing cognitive and behavioural efforts to manage demands appraised as taxing. Two families:

Problem-focused copingEmotion-focused coping
Targetthe stressor itselfyour emotional reaction to it
Examplesmake a timetable, gather information, solve the problemrelaxation, venting, seeking comfort, positive reappraisal, distraction, denial
Works best whenthe stressor is controllablethe stressor is uncontrollable (bereavement, waiting for a result)

Body costs over time: allostasis and allostatic load. Peter Sterling and Joseph Eyer (1988) coined allostasis, "stability through change": instead of holding a fixed set point (Cannon's homeostasis), the brain anticipates demands and adjusts stress hormones (cortisol via the HPA axis, adrenaline via the sympathetic system) to meet them. Bruce McEwen (1998) developed allostatic load: the cumulative wear and tear from repeated or chronic activation, linked to hypertension, immune suppression and hippocampal damage. This is the Q44 answer.

Who copes better: personality and sex differences. Suzanne Kobasa (1979) studied executives under high stress and found that those who stayed healthy had hardiness, made of the 3 Cs: commitment (involvement rather than alienation), control (belief you can influence events) and challenge (seeing change as an opportunity). Shelley Taylor and colleagues (2000) argued that fight-or-flight was built on male research and that females more often show tend-and-befriend: protecting offspring (tend) and seeking social affiliation (befriend), linked to oxytocin.

Job stress (Q40). Organisational behaviour texts (Robbins) sort job stressors into three sources:

SourceExamples
Organisational (inside the job)task demands (workload, time pressure, job design), role demands (role conflict, role ambiguity, role overload), interpersonal demands, organisational structure, leadership style
Environmental (outside the organisation)economic, political and technological uncertainty
Individual / personalfamily problems, personal economic problems, number of dependents, personality

Role conflict = incompatible expectations (your boss wants speed, your client wants perfection). Role ambiguity = not knowing what is expected. Role overload = too many roles or demands. These role concepts come from Robert Kahn and colleagues (1964, Organizational Stress).

Three job-stress models to tie to names:

  • Robert Karasek (1979), job demand-control model: strain comes from the combination of high demands + low decision latitude (control) = "high-strain job". High demand + high control = "active job", which is stimulating rather than harmful. Johnson and Hall (1988) added social support (demand-control-support model).
  • Johannes Siegrist (1996), effort-reward imbalance: strain comes when high effort is met with low reward (pay, esteem, job security, promotion). Overcommitment worsens it. Karasek is about control, Siegrist about reciprocity.
  • Christina Maslach, burnout: a syndrome from chronic job stress, especially in helping professions, with three dimensions: emotional exhaustion, depersonalisation (cynicism, treating clients as objects) and reduced personal accomplishment. Measured by the Maslach Burnout Inventory (Maslach and Jackson, 1981). Herbert Freudenberger coined the word "burnout" (1974).

Who said what

TheoristYearTerm / theoryOne-line meaning

Hooks. (1) La-ZAR-us = "LAZy Appraiser Rates Us twice": first "what's at stake?" (primary), then "what have I got?" (secondary). Secondary = Supplies: your coping supplies. (2) SelyE = Exhaustion, and his stages spell ARE (Alarm, Resistance, Exhaustion): "Selye's rats ARE exhausted." (3) Kobasa's 3 Cs = Commitment, Control, Challenge; picture a hardy KOBRA snake coiled in three Cs. (4) Karasek = Control, Siegrist = Salary (reward); Holmes and Rahe = the big Hits of life, Kanner = the daily Knocks.

Key distinctions

PairFirstSecond
Primary vs secondary appraisal (Lazarus)Is this a threat, harm/loss, or challenge? What is at stake?Do I have the resources to cope? What can I do?
Threat vs challenge (both primary)Anticipated harm, felt as fearPotential gain, felt as eagerness
Problem- vs emotion-focused copingChange the stressor; best when controllableChange the feeling; best when uncontrollable
Cannon vs SelyeFight-or-flight: fast, sympathetic, adrenalineGAS: three-stage, prolonged, cortisol, non-specific
Selye vs LazarusPhysiological, same response to all stressorsCognitive, response depends on appraisal
Holmes and Rahe vs KannerMajor life events (SRRS, LCU)Daily hassles and uplifts

Karasek vs SiegristDemand vs controlEffort vs reward
Role conflict vs role ambiguityIncompatible expectationsUnclear expectations
Organisational vs personal vs environmental stressorsTask, role, interpersonal demands, structure, leadershipFamily, personal economics, dependents (personal); political, economic, technological uncertainty (environmental)
Fight-or-flight vs tend-and-befriendCannon, adrenalineTaylor (2000), oxytocin, affiliation

MCQ traps

  • "Coping resource assessment" or "situational capacity evaluation" (Q89 c, d): descriptive paraphrases invented by the paper setter. Lazarus's real term for judging your resources is secondary appraisal. When an option sounds like a plain-English definition, it is usually the fake.
  • "Initial threat evaluation" (Q89 a): this describes primary appraisal (is it a threat?), not resource assessment.
  • Selye for the transactional model: wrong; Selye's GAS is physiological and ignores appraisal. Transactional = Lazarus and Folkman.
  • Cannon for GAS: wrong; Cannon = fight-or-flight and homeostasis. GAS = Selye. Cannon-Bard is an emotion theory, unrelated.
  • Holmes and Rahe for daily hassles: wrong; they measured major life events. Hassles = Kanner, Lazarus and colleagues (1981).
  • Homeostasis for hormone regulation of stress (Q44 b): homeostasis is Cannon's fixed set point; active, anticipatory stress-hormone regulation is allostasis (Sterling and Eyer; McEwen).
  • Family, political or economic factors as organisational stressors (Q40 a, b, d): these are personal or environmental. Organisational = task and role demands.
  • Kobasa for burnout or Maslach for hardiness: swap trap. Kobasa = hardiness (3 Cs); Maslach = burnout (3 dimensions). Karasek vs Siegrist is the same kind of swap: control vs reward.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q89 (you MISSED this): Transactional Model, the individual's assessment of whether they have the resources to cope → Secondary appraisal (Lazarus and Folkman, 1984). Distractors: "initial threat evaluation" describes primary appraisal; "coping resource assessment" and "situational capacity evaluation" are made-up paraphrases, not terms in the model. Rule: pick the theorist's actual term over a description of it.
  • Q40: which is an organisational stressor leading to job stress → Task and role demands. Distractors: family and economic problems and number of dependents are personal (individual) stressors; political uncertainty is an environmental stressor outside the organisation.
  • Q44: the brain's process of regulating its responses to stress by controlling hormone release → Allostasis (Sterling and Eyer, 1988; McEwen). Distractors: homeostasis is a fixed set point (Cannon); neuroplasticity is the brain's capacity to change with experience; circadian rhythm is the 24-hour body clock.

Self-test

  1. In Lazarus's model, "Do I have the resources to cope?" is which appraisal? (Secondary appraisal, Lazarus and Folkman 1984)
  2. Name the three forms of a stressful primary appraisal. (Harm/loss, threat, challenge)
  3. Who described the General Adaptation Syndrome, and what are its stages? (Hans Selye: alarm, resistance, exhaustion)
  4. Who coined fight-or-flight and homeostasis? (Walter Cannon)
  5. The SRRS with death of spouse = 100 LCU is by whom, and when? (Holmes and Rahe, 1967)
  6. High job demand plus low control = high strain: whose model? (Robert Karasek, 1979, job demand-control)
  7. Commitment, control, challenge describe what, by whom? (Hardiness, Suzanne Kobasa 1979)
  8. Tend-and-befriend and allostatic load belong to whom? (Shelley Taylor 2000; Bruce McEwen 1998)
12Applied psychology: psychologist vs psychiatrist, I/O, biopsychosocial model, reading
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q1, Q31, Q47, Q61, Q70

From scratch

Clinical psychology is the branch devoted to the assessment, diagnosis, treatment and prevention of mental disorders, combining science and practice. Lightner Witmer opened the first psychological clinic (University of Pennsylvania, 1896) and coined the term "clinical psychology" (1907, journal The Psychological Clinic). The scientist-practitioner (Boulder) model dates from 1949.

Psychologist vs psychiatrist. A psychiatrist is a medical doctor (in India MBBS plus MD/DNB Psychiatry) who diagnoses, does physical workups and prescribes medication, ECT and other physical treatments. A clinical psychologist is not a medical doctor; in India they hold an RCI-recognised professional qualification (traditionally M.Phil Clinical Psychology, now also the professional MA/MSc route) and must be registered with the Rehabilitation Council of India (RCI Act, 1992; recognised as a mental health professional under the Mental Health Care Act, 2017). They do psychometric assessment (IQ, personality, neuropsychological testing) and psychotherapy (CBT, behaviour therapy, etc.), and typically cannot prescribe (a few US states such as New Mexico, 2002, and Louisiana, 2004, allow specially trained psychologists limited prescription rights). Other team members: psychiatric social worker, psychiatric nurse, counselling psychologist (milder adjustment problems).

Industrial-organisational (I/O) psychology applies psychology to the workplace to optimise performance and well-being. Founders: Hugo Münsterberg (Psychology and Industrial Efficiency, 1913, father of industrial psychology) and Walter Dill Scott (advertising, selection). The Hawthorne studies (Western Electric, 1924 to 1932, Elton Mayo) showed social factors and being observed change productivity (Hawthorne effect). Taylor's scientific management (1911) was engineering, not psychology. Areas: personnel psychology (selection, training, appraisal), organisational psychology (motivation, leadership, culture, job stress) and human factors/ergonomics.

Biopsychosocial model: George Engel (1977, Science, "The need for a new medical model: a challenge for biomedicine"), building on general systems theory. He attacked the biomedical model's reductionism and argued that illness arises from interacting biological (genes, neurotransmitters, physical illness), psychological (thoughts, emotions, coping, personality) and social (family, culture, poverty, stigma, support) factors. Example: depression after job loss in someone with a family history and poor support. It is the umbrella under which the diathesis-stress model (Zubin and Spring, 1977, for schizophrenia) sits.

Cognition behind reading. Reading is built on spoken language. Children first have oral vocabulary and grammar, then develop phonological awareness (hearing and manipulating phonemes) and learn the alphabetic principle (letters map onto speech sounds). The Simple View of Reading (Gough and Tunmer, 1986) states Reading comprehension = Decoding x Language comprehension. Coltheart's dual-route model has a lexical route (whole-word recognition, needed for irregular words like "yacht") and a sublexical grapheme-phoneme route (needed for non-words); damage gives surface vs phonological dyslexia. Phonological deficit is the core of specific learning disorder with impairment in reading.

Key distinctions

PairDistinction
Psychiatrist vs clinical psychologistMedical degree, prescribes vs RCI-registered, assessment and psychotherapy, no prescribing
Clinical vs counselling psychologySerious psychopathology vs adjustment and normal-range problems
I/O vs counselling vs forensicWorkplace vs personal adjustment vs legal system
Biomedical vs biopsychosocialDisease = biology only vs biology + psychology + social context (Engel, 1977)
Biopsychosocial vs diathesis-stressBroad framework vs specific vulnerability-plus-trigger model
Theory of mind vs phonological awarenessUnderstanding others' mental states (social cognition) vs sound awareness that underpins reading

MCQ traps

  • Reversed prescription option: "a psychologist prescribes, a psychiatrist does not" is planted to catch fast readers.
  • "Biological, psychological and cultural" looks right, but Engel's third term is social; culture is only part of it.
  • I/O vs organisational management: management is a business discipline; I/O is the psychology branch.
  • Clinical psychology is not about animals or industry: comparative psychology studies animals, I/O studies workplaces.
  • Engel, not Freud or Meyer: Adolf Meyer's psychobiology was a precursor, but the 1977 biopsychosocial model is Engel's.
  • Reading item: theory of mind and imagination are real cognitive skills but not the foundation of decoding; spoken language is.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q1: Clinical psychology primarily focuses on? → Assessment and treatment of mental illness, the Witmer-era definition of the field.
  • Q31: Main distinction between psychologist and psychiatrist? → A psychiatrist can prescribe medication, while a psychologist typically cannot, because only the psychiatrist is medically qualified.
  • Q47: The biopsychosocial model integrates? → Biological, psychological and social factors, per Engel (1977).
  • Q61: Branch concerned with optimising human behaviour in workplaces? → Industrial-organisational psychology, Münsterberg's applied field.
  • Q70 (debated): Which cognitive skill is involved in learning to read? → official key Spoken language, because reading is mapped onto oral language through phonological awareness; "mental representation" is arguably also involved, but the textbook concept is that spoken language and phonological skill are the foundation, so go with the key.
13Festinger's cognitive dissonance
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q12, Q62

From scratch

Leon Festinger (1957), A Theory of Cognitive Dissonance. Dissonance is an unpleasant state of psychological tension (a drive state, like hunger) that arises when a person holds two or more cognitions (beliefs, attitudes, knowledge of one's own behaviour) that are psychologically inconsistent. "I smoke" and "smoking causes cancer" are dissonant. Because the state is aversive, the person is motivated to reduce it, just as a hungry person is motivated to eat.

Magnitude of dissonance depends on (1) the importance of the cognitions, (2) the ratio of dissonant to consonant cognitions, and (3) how little external justification exists for the behaviour. The less justification you had, the more dissonance you feel.

Ways to reduce dissonance: (1) change the attitude ("smoking is not that harmful"), (2) change the behaviour (quit), (3) add consonant cognitions ("smoking keeps my weight down"), (4) trivialise the inconsistency ("everyone dies of something"). Because behaviour is usually harder to undo than an attitude, people most often change the attitude to fit what they already did. This is the core surprise of the theory: behaviour can drive attitudes, not just the reverse.

Classic experiment: Festinger and Carlsmith (1959), the $1 vs $20 study. Participants did a boring peg-turning task and were then paid either $1 or $20 to tell the next participant it was fun. Later, the $1 group rated the task as more enjoyable. The $20 group had sufficient external justification ("I lied for the money"); the $1 group did not, so they reduced dissonance by changing their private attitude. This is induced (forced) compliance and the principle of insufficient justification.

Other paradigms: post-decision dissonance (Brehm, 1956: after choosing between two similar appliances, people rate the chosen one higher and the rejected one lower, "spreading of alternatives"); effort justification (Aronson and Mills, 1959: a harsh initiation makes a dull group seem more attractive); insufficient deterrence (Aronson and Carlsmith, 1963: children given a mild threat not to play with a toy later devalued it more than children given a severe threat). Field example: When Prophecy Fails (Festinger, Riecken and Schachter, 1956), a doomsday cult that proselytised more after the prophecy failed.

Rival account: Daryl Bem's self-perception theory (1967) says no aversive arousal is needed: we infer our attitudes by watching our own behaviour, as an observer would. Self-perception explains attitude change best when attitudes are weak or vague; dissonance explains it best when behaviour clearly contradicts a strong attitude. Later revisions (Cooper and Fazio's "new look", 1984) stress personal responsibility and foreseeable aversive consequences.

Key distinctions

ConceptTheoristCore idea
Cognitive dissonanceFestinger, 1957Inconsistent cognitions create tension that motivates attitude or behaviour change
Self-perceptionBem, 1967We infer attitudes from our behaviour; no tension required
Balance theoryHeider, 1946/1958P-O-X triads; imbalance among liking relations motivates change
Social comparison theoryFestinger, 1954We evaluate our abilities and opinions by comparing with others (same theorist, different theory)
ReactanceBrehm, 1966Threatened freedom motivates restoring it
Elaboration likelihood modelPetty and Cacioppo, 1986Central vs peripheral routes to persuasion

MCQ traps

  • Big reward = big attitude change? No. In dissonance, small reward ($1) produces more attitude change. Reinforcement logic predicts the reverse, which is why "$20 group liked it more" is the tempting wrong option.
  • Festinger also wrote social comparison theory (1954). If a stem says "evaluating opinions by comparing with others", the answer is still Festinger but the theory is social comparison, not dissonance.
  • Dissonance needs inconsistency. Options like "thoughts align with behaviour" or "being praised" describe consonance; they never produce dissonance.
  • Free choice matters. Dissonance is strongest when the act feels chosen. Under heavy coercion, external justification is high and dissonance is low, so "forced" in a stem means induced compliance with minimal pressure, not a gun to the head.
  • Bem vs Festinger. "Attitudes inferred from behaviour without arousal" is self-perception, not dissonance.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q12: Leon Festinger is best known for which theory → Cognitive dissonance; operant (Skinner), classical (Pavlov) and social learning (Bandura) are distractor theorists.
  • Q62: When is cognitive dissonance most likely → When forced to make a decision that goes against their beliefs; acting against an attitude creates the inconsistency, whereas alignment, praise or positive memories do not.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Students who can explain dissonance still trip on three things. (1) The $1 vs $20 result (Festinger and Carlsmith, 1959): the $1 group liked the task more, because insufficient justification forces an attitude change; reinforcement logic predicts the opposite. (2) Festinger owns two theories: cognitive dissonance (1957) and social comparison (1954), so read the stem, not just the name. (3) Neighbouring consistency theories have other owners: balance theory is Heider, congruity is Osgood and Tannenbaum, self-perception (no arousal needed) is Bem, reactance is Brehm. One more fine point: dissonance is strongest when the act is freely chosen and external justification is low; heavy coercion or a big reward supplies justification and lowers dissonance. So in a stem, "forced to act against beliefs" means induced compliance with minimal pressure, which is exactly how IHBAS Q62 was keyed.

Jack Brehm1956Post-decision dissonance: spreading of alternatives
Aronson and Mills1959Effort justification: harsh initiation, dull group seems better

Cooper and Fazio1984New look: responsibility for foreseeable aversive consequences
  1. In Festinger and Carlsmith (1959), which group rated the task as more enjoyable? (the $1 group)
  2. Comparing your opinions with others to evaluate them is whose theory, and what is it called? (Festinger, social comparison theory 1954)
  3. Rating the chosen option higher after a hard choice is called, and shown by? (post-decision dissonance (spreading of alternatives), Brehm 1956)
  4. Attitude inferred from watching one's own behaviour, no tension: whose theory? (Bem, self-perception 1967)
  5. P-O-X triads seeking balance: whose theory? (Heider, balance theory)
14Tajfel's social identity theory
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q14

From scratch

The puzzle. Why do people who have never been harmed by another group still favour their own side? Why does a cricket fan feel personally proud when India wins, or a hostel student defend "our wing" against the next one? Two big answers exist in social psychology, and IHBAS-style papers love to set one against the other: realistic conflict theory (Muzafer Sherif, 1950s-1966) says groups fight because they compete for real, scarce resources; social identity theory (Henri Tajfel and John Turner, 1979) says groups favour themselves because group membership is part of who we are, and we want that part to look good.

Step 1: Sherif's realistic conflict theory and the Robbers Cave experiment. Muzafer Sherif (with Harvey, White, Hood and Carolyn Sherif) ran the Robbers Cave experiment in 1954 at a summer camp in Robbers Cave State Park, Oklahoma (full report published 1961). Twenty-two well-adjusted 11 to 12 year old boys were split into two groups who did not know about each other at first. The study ran in three stages:

  1. In-group formation: each group bonded, picked a name (the Eagles and the Rattlers), made norms and a flag.
  2. Friction (competition): a tournament with prizes only the winners could get. Hostility exploded: name-calling, flag burning, cabin raids. Competition for a scarce resource produced prejudice.
  3. Integration: mere contact (eating, watching films together) did not end the hostility. What worked were superordinate goals (Sherif): goals both groups wanted but neither could reach alone, such as fixing the camp water supply, pooling money to rent a film, and pulling a stuck truck.

Lesson (Sherif): conflicting goals create hostility, superordinate goals reduce it. Donald Campbell (1965) gave the label "realistic group conflict theory".

Step 2: Tajfel's question, and the minimal group paradigm. Henri Tajfel, a Polish-born Jewish psychologist who survived the Second World War as a prisoner of war and later worked at Bristol, asked: what is the minimum condition needed for in-group bias? He stripped away everything Sherif had: no competition, no history, no face-to-face contact, no personal gain. This is the minimal group paradigm (Tajfel, 1970; Tajfel, Billig, Bundy and Flament, 1971). Bristol schoolboys (about 14 to 15 years old) were assigned to groups on a trivial basis: in one version, whether they "over-estimated" or "under-estimated" dots on a screen; in another, whether they preferred paintings by Klee or Kandinsky (in reality, assignment was random). Each boy then privately distributed points (worth money) between an anonymous in-group member and an anonymous out-group member, using reward matrices. He never got points himself.

Result: boys consistently favoured the in-group. Strikingly, they often chose maximum difference (making the in-group beat the out-group) even when another choice (maximum joint profit or maximum in-group profit) would have given their own group more in absolute terms. Winning relative to "them" mattered more than gaining. Conclusion: mere categorisation into groups is sufficient to produce in-group favouritism. Real conflict is not necessary.

Step 3: Social identity theory (Tajfel and Turner, 1979). To explain the minimal-group results, Tajfel and his student John Turner wrote "An integrative theory of intergroup conflict" (1979; restated 1986). The self-concept has two parts: personal identity (my traits, abilities, achievements, preferences) and social identity, defined by Tajfel (1972) as that part of the self-concept that comes from knowing one belongs to social groups, together with the value and emotional significance attached to that membership. Three processes follow in order:

ProcessWhat happensExample
1. Social categorisationWe sort people, including ourselves, into groups. This exaggerates similarity within a group and differences between groups (accentuation effect)."We are psychology students, they are engineers."
2. Social identificationWe take on the identity of the group, adopting its norms, emotions and fate as our own.Feeling hurt when someone mocks psychology as "not a real science".
3. Social comparisonWe compare our in-group with out-groups. Because we want positive self-esteem, we seek positive distinctiveness: our group should come out better."Engineers just memorise; we actually understand people."

Memory order: Categorise, Identify, Compare (CIC). The motive behind it all is the self-esteem drive: a positive social identity raises self-esteem, so we tilt comparisons in our favour. The result is in-group bias (in-group favouritism), and sometimes out-group derogation and prejudice.

Interpersonal-intergroup continuum (Tajfel). Behaviour lies on a continuum from purely interpersonal (two friends chatting as individuals) to purely intergroup (two soldiers of opposing armies, who react only as members of their sides).

What a low-status group does (Tajfel and Turner, 1979). If your group compares badly, three strategies are possible, depending on whether group boundaries are permeable and whether the status gap seems stable and legitimate:

  • Individual mobility: leave the group if boundaries are permeable (a person hides or drops a stigmatised identity).
  • Social creativity: change the comparison: pick a new dimension ("we are poorer but more hospitable"), revalue the old one ("black is beautiful"), or compare with a different, lower out-group.
  • Social competition: directly challenge the higher-status group (social movements, collective action), likely when the gap feels illegitimate and unstable.

Step 4: Self-categorisation theory (John Turner, 1987). Turner, with Hogg, Oakes, Reicher and Wetherell, published Rediscovering the Social Group (1987). Self-categorisation theory explains when a social identity switches on. We categorise ourselves at different levels (human, group, personal). When a group identity becomes salient, we undergo depersonalisation: we see ourselves as interchangeable members who think and act like the group prototype. The meta-contrast principle (Turner) says a category forms when differences between groups are larger than differences within them. Salience depends on accessibility and fit (Penelope Oakes). Self-categorisation theory explains conformity, group polarisation and leadership: the most prototypical member gains influence.

Step 5: Reducing prejudice.

  • Contact hypothesis (Gordon Allport, 1954, The Nature of Prejudice): contact reduces prejudice only under four conditions: equal status, common goals, intergroup cooperation, and support of authorities, law or custom. Thomas Pettigrew and Linda Tropp's (2006) meta-analysis of over 500 studies confirmed that contact generally reduces prejudice.
  • Jigsaw classroom (Elliot Aronson, 1971): each pupil holds one piece of the lesson, so cooperation across groups is required. Sherif's superordinate goals applied to schools.
  • Common in-group identity model (Samuel Gaertner and John Dovidio): recategorisation, turning "us and them" into a bigger "we" (e.g. "we are all Indians").
  • Decategorisation (Marilynn Brewer and Norman Miller, 1984): see out-group members as individuals.

Why it matters. SIT explains communal and caste prejudice, sports fandom, nationalism, workplace team rivalry, and even why patients in a ward split into "old-timers" and "new admissions". Clinically, stigma research uses its strategies: individual mobility (concealment) versus social creativity (pride movements).

Who said what

Hooks:

  • Tajfel = "Trivial groups" (T and T): Klee vs Kandinsky boys favour their group though they never meet.
  • Sherif = Sheriff at a camp breaking up a fight between Eagles and Rattlers over a trophy, then making them fix the water tank together (superordinate goal).
  • SIT steps = CIC: Categorise, Identify, Compare. Then Turner turns it into self-categorisation (1987, eight years after 1979).
  • Allport's "port" of entry to friendship has four gates: Equal status, Common goals, Cooperation, Authority support (ECCA).

Key distinctions

PairHow to tell them apart
Social identity theory (Tajfel and Turner, 1979) vs realistic conflict theory (Sherif)SIT: categorisation and self-esteem alone produce bias. RCT: real competition for scarce resources is needed.
Minimal group paradigm (Tajfel) vs Robbers Cave (Sherif)Minimal: strangers, no contact, random labels, lab, Bristol schoolboys. Robbers Cave: field camp, real tournament, Oklahoma boys.
SIT vs self-categorisation theory (Turner, 1987)SIT explains intergroup bias and its motive (self-esteem). SCT explains the cognitive switch to group identity (depersonalisation, prototypes, meta-contrast).
Social comparison in SIT vs Festinger's social comparison theory (1954)SIT: group vs group, for positive distinctiveness. Festinger: individual vs individual, for accurate self-evaluation.
Social identity vs personal identitySocial: group memberships and categories. Personal: own traits, achievements, preferences.
Contact hypothesis (Allport) vs superordinate goals (Sherif)Allport lists four contact conditions; Sherif showed mere contact failed until a shared goal appeared. Both stress cooperation.
Recategorisation (Gaertner and Dovidio) vs decategorisation (Brewer and Miller)Re: build a bigger common "we". De: see individuals, not categories.

MCQ traps

  • "Robbers Cave was Tajfel's study." Wrong: Robbers Cave is Muzafer Sherif (1954). Tajfel's evidence is the minimal group paradigm.
  • "SIT says in-group bias requires competition for resources." That is Sherif's realistic conflict theory; Tajfel showed bias with no competition at all.
  • "Social comparison theory = Tajfel." Social comparison theory as a stand-alone theory is Leon Festinger (1954) and is interpersonal; Tajfel borrowed the idea for group-level comparison.
  • "Self-categorisation theory was Tajfel's." It is John Turner (1987), after Tajfel's death in 1982.
  • "Identity comes from personal achievements / preferences / economic status." Achievements and preferences are personal identity; economic status is only one possible category, too narrow an answer.
  • "Contact hypothesis = Sherif" or "= Festinger". Contact hypothesis is Gordon Allport (1954). Do not confuse with Floyd Allport (social facilitation, his brother).
  • "Identity crisis = Tajfel." Identity crisis and identity vs role confusion are Erik Erikson, a developmental theory, not intergroup.
  • "Minimal group boys chose maximum joint profit." No: their striking choice was maximum difference in favour of the in-group, even at an absolute cost.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q14: Social identity theory says individuals derive part of their identity from → Their group memberships and social categories (option b). Distractors: "economic status" (a) is just one possible group marker, too narrow; "personal achievements" (c) and "personal preferences" (d) belong to personal identity, the individual side of the self that SIT explicitly separates from social identity.

Self-test

  1. Who ran the minimal group experiments with Bristol schoolboys? (Henri Tajfel, 1970-1971, with Billig, Bundy and Flament)
  2. Social identity theory was proposed by whom, and when? (Tajfel and John Turner, 1979)
  3. Name SIT's three processes in order. (Social categorisation, social identification, social comparison)
  4. Robbers Cave and superordinate goals belong to which theorist and theory? (Muzafer Sherif, realistic conflict theory, 1954)
  5. Who proposed self-categorisation theory and its meta-contrast principle? (John Turner, 1987)
  6. Allport's four conditions for contact to reduce prejudice? (Equal status, common goals, intergroup cooperation, support of authorities; Gordon Allport, 1954)
  7. Recategorising "us and them" into one "we" is which model? (Common in-group identity model, Gaertner and Dovidio)
  8. A low-status group says "we are poorer but kinder". Which strategy? (Social creativity, Tajfel and Turner, 1979)
15Attribution biases: actor-observer, FAE, halo effect
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q15, Q51, Q54

From scratch

Attribution is how we explain the causes of behaviour. Fritz Heider (1958), the founder, distinguished internal (dispositional) attributions (personality, ability, effort) from external (situational) ones (luck, task difficulty, other people). Jones and Davis (1965) proposed correspondent inference theory (when do we infer a trait from an act?), and Harold Kelley (1967) the covariation model using consensus, consistency and distinctiveness: low consensus + high consistency + low distinctiveness points to an internal cause. Bernard Weiner added stability and controllability, applied to achievement.

Fundamental attribution error (FAE), named by Lee Ross (1977), also called correspondence bias (Jones and Harris, 1967): when explaining other people's behaviour, we overestimate dispositional causes and underestimate situational ones. In Jones and Harris's study, readers judged an essay writer to hold pro-Castro views even when told the writer was assigned that side. Example: a classmate arrives late and you think "irresponsible", ignoring the traffic jam. FAE is weaker in collectivist cultures (Miller, 1984, comparing Indian and American participants).

Actor-observer effect (Jones and Nisbett, 1971): we attribute our own behaviour to situations but others' behaviour to dispositions. "I was late because of traffic; he was late because he is careless." Explanation: perceptual salience (as actors we see the situation; as observers we see the person) and more information about our own varied behaviour. It differs from FAE in that it is a comparison of self vs other.

Self-serving bias: success is attributed internally ("I am smart"), failure externally ("the paper was unfair"). It is driven by self-esteem, so it depends on outcome valence, unlike the actor-observer effect, which depends on perspective.

Halo effect: Edward Thorndike (1920) found that officers' ratings of soldiers on separate traits were too highly correlated. One salient positive trait (attractiveness, confidence) spreads to judgements of unrelated traits (intelligence, honesty). Its reverse is the horns (devil) effect. Dion, Berscheid and Walster (1972) showed "what is beautiful is good". In assessment and interviewing, the same phenomenon is called halo error: rating a candidate on all dimensions based on one attribute. Other rater errors: central tendency (rating everyone average), leniency/severity, contrast error (rating relative to the previous candidate), similar-to-me error. Halo is a bias in impression formation, close to implicit personality theory.

Key distinctions

BiasWho is judgedPattern
Fundamental attribution errorOthers onlyOverweight disposition, underweight situation
Actor-observer effectSelf vs othersSelf: situational; others: dispositional
Self-serving biasSelfSuccess internal, failure external
Ultimate attribution error (Pettigrew, 1979)GroupsOut-group failure dispositional, success situational
Halo effect / halo errorAny targetOne trait colours judgement of all others
Primacy effect (Asch, 1946)Any targetFirst information dominates the overall impression
Projection (Freud)DefenceAttributing one's own unacceptable impulses to others

MCQ traps

  • FAE vs actor-observer. If the stem mentions both "own behaviour external" and "others' internal", the answer is actor-observer. FAE only concerns judging others.
  • Self-serving bias looks right for "own behaviour external", but it hinges on success vs failure, not self vs other.
  • Halo vs primacy. "Effect of initial impressions on later judgements" is the primacy effect, not halo. Halo is one trait spreading to other traits.
  • Halo vs stereotyping. "Judging others based on group membership" is a stereotype, not halo.
  • Halo vs contrast error. Contrast error is judging relative to the previous ratee; central tendency is clustering in the middle; halo is "one single attribute".

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q15: What is the halo effect → The influence of a positive trait on the perception of other traits; group-based judgement is stereotyping and initial-impression influence is primacy.
  • Q51: Attributing own behaviour to external causes and others' to internal causes → Actor-Observer effect; FAE covers only the "others internal" half, and self-serving bias depends on success/failure.
  • Q54: Rater judges an individual on the basis of one single attribute → Halo error; similarity, central tendency and contrast are different rater errors.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

The basics are solid, but the 2 → 3 step is about precise boundaries. (1) FAE vs actor-observer: FAE (named by Ross, 1977) is only about judging others; the actor-observer effect (Jones and Nisbett, 1971) needs the self-vs-other contrast. (2) Self-serving bias runs on success vs failure, not on perspective. (3) Halo (Thorndike, 1920) is one trait colouring other traits; first-information dominance is the primacy effect (Asch, 1946), group-based judgement is stereotyping, and in rating scales the same bias is called halo error beside central tendency, leniency and contrast errors. Keep the founders straight too: Heider started attribution theory, Kelley gave the covariation cues, Jones and Davis asked when an act reveals a trait, and Weiner added stability and controllability. FAE is weaker in collectivist cultures (Miller, 1984), which is why it is not truly "fundamental".

TheoristYearIdea
Fritz Heider1958Founder of attribution theory: internal vs external causes
Jones and Davis1965Correspondent inference theory
Harold Kelley1967Covariation model: consensus, consistency, distinctiveness
Jones and Harris1967Castro essay study: correspondence bias
Lee Ross1977Named the fundamental attribution error
Jones and Nisbett1971Actor-observer effect
Thomas Pettigrew1979Ultimate attribution error (group level)
Edward Thorndike1920Halo effect in officers' ratings of soldiers
Solomon Asch1946Primacy in impression formation; warm-cold central traits
Dion, Berscheid and Walster1972"What is beautiful is good"
  1. Who coined the term fundamental attribution error? (Lee Ross, 1977)
  2. Consensus, consistency and distinctiveness belong to whose model? (Kelley's covariation model, 1967)
  3. "I failed because of traffic; he failed because he is lazy": FAE or actor-observer? (actor-observer (self situational, other dispositional))
  4. Who first described the halo effect, and in what setting? (Thorndike 1920, officers rating soldiers)
  5. Rating every candidate as average is which rater error? (central tendency error)
16Groups: groupthink, social loafing, social facilitation, bystander effect
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q4, Q22, Q34, Q57, Q79

From scratch

Social facilitation. Norman Triplett (1898), often called the first social psychology experiment, found cyclists rode faster with others and children wound fishing reels faster in pairs. Robert Zajonc (1965) resolved contradictory findings with drive theory: the mere presence of others raises arousal, which strengthens the dominant response. On simple or well-learned tasks the dominant response is correct, so performance improves; on complex or new tasks it is wrong, so performance worsens (social inhibition). Zajonc even showed it in cockroaches. Cottrell (1968) proposed evaluation apprehension; Baron proposed distraction-conflict.

Social loafing. Max Ringelmann (1913) found that individuals pulled a rope with less force per person as group size increased (the Ringelmann effect). Latané, Williams and Harkins (1979) named it social loafing: people exert less effort on a collective task where individual output is pooled and unidentifiable. Explained by the collective effort model (Karau and Williams, 1993). Reduced when contributions are identifiable, the task matters, groups are small and cohesive. It is weaker in collectivist cultures and among women. Key contrast with facilitation: in facilitation your performance is individually evaluated; in loafing it is pooled.

Groupthink. Irving Janis (1972), analysing the Bay of Pigs fiasco, defined groupthink as a mode of thinking in highly cohesive groups where the striving for unanimity overrides realistic appraisal of alternatives. Antecedents: high cohesion, insulation, a directive leader, high stress, no procedures for evaluating options. Symptoms: illusion of invulnerability, illusion of unanimity, collective rationalisation, belief in the group's inherent morality, stereotyped views of out-groups, direct pressure on dissenters, self-censorship, and mindguards. Remedies: a devil's advocate, an impartial leader, outside experts, subgroups, a "second-chance" meeting.

Bystander effect. Triggered by the 1964 murder of Kitty Genovese in New York (reported as 38 witnesses not helping). Darley and Latané (1968), in the seizure-over-intercom study, found that 85 percent helped when they believed they were alone, but only 31 percent when they thought four others heard. So the more bystanders, the less likely any one person helps, and the slower help comes. Their five-step decision model: notice the event, interpret it as an emergency, assume responsibility, know how to help, decide to help. Causes: diffusion of responsibility (main cause), pluralistic ignorance (others' calm makes you think it is not an emergency, as in the smoke-filled room study, Latané and Darley 1968), and audience inhibition / evaluation apprehension.

Related group phenomena: group polarisation (Moscovici and Zavalloni, 1969; discussion makes the group's initial leaning more extreme; the "risky shift" of Stoner, 1961); deindividuation (Festinger, Pepitone and Newcomb, 1952; Zimbardo, 1969; anonymity in crowds lowers self-awareness and restraint).

Key distinctions

PhenomenonKey name/yearSignature
Social facilitationTriplett 1898; Zajonc 1965Others present, individual effort evaluated: better on easy tasks, worse on hard
Social loafingRingelmann 1913; Latané 1979Pooled, unidentifiable effort: less effort per person
GroupthinkJanis 1972Cohesive group suppresses dissent, poor decision
Group polarisationMoscovici and Zavalloni 1969Initial tendency becomes more extreme
Bystander effectDarley and Latané 1968More witnesses, less helping
Diffusion of responsibilityDarley and LatanéThe mechanism behind the bystander effect
DeindividuationFestinger 1952; Zimbardo 1969Loss of self-awareness in anonymous crowds

MCQ traps

  • Groupthink is not peer pressure or brainstorming. It is a collective decision process that discourages dissent; "peer pressure" is too generic.
  • Facilitation vs loafing in one question: "improved performance in presence of others" is facilitation; "less effort when working with others" is loafing.
  • Bystander direction. People help more when alone. The option "more likely to help in a group" reverses it. "Less likely to notice emergencies in a group" is tempting but the effect is about helping, not noticing.
  • Mechanism vs phenomenon. The cause of the bystander effect is diffusion of responsibility; social loafing also involves diffused accountability but is about effort on group tasks, not helping.
  • Polarisation vs groupthink. Polarisation is about extremity of attitudes; groupthink is about suppressing dissent to keep harmony.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q4: What does groupthink refer to → Collective decision-making that discourages dissent; Janis's core is unanimity over critical appraisal.
  • Q22: Less effort when working with others → Social Loafing; facilitation is improvement, deindividuation is loss of self-awareness, sleeper effect is delayed persuasion.
  • Q34: Improved performance on tasks in the presence of others → Social facilitation; Zajonc's dominant response on easy tasks.
  • Q57: The bystander effect suggests → People are more likely to help when they are alone than in a group; Darley and Latané's 85 vs 31 percent.
  • Q79: The bystander effect occurs because of → Diffusion of responsibility; each witness feels less personally responsible as numbers grow.
17Social influence: normative vs informational, conformity, compliance, obedience, reactance, roles
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q25, Q26, Q41, Q94

From scratch

Two reasons we yield to others (Deutsch and Gerard, 1955): Normative social influence is going along to be liked, accepted and avoid rejection; it typically produces public compliance without private acceptance. Informational social influence is going along because we believe others are correct, especially when the situation is ambiguous, a crisis, or others are experts; it produces genuine private acceptance (internalisation). Kelman (1958) described three levels: compliance, identification, internalisation.

Conformity is changing behaviour or beliefs to match a group norm, without anyone directly asking. Muzafer Sherif (1935), using the autokinetic effect (a still light in a dark room seems to move), showed that estimates converged into a group norm: informational influence in an ambiguous task. Solomon Asch (1951, 1956), using unambiguous line-judgement tasks with confederates giving wrong answers, found about 37 percent conformity on critical trials and about 75 percent of participants conformed at least once: normative influence. Conformity rose with group size up to about 3 to 4, and dropped sharply if even one ally dissented or responses were private.

Compliance is changing behaviour in response to a direct request from someone without special authority. Techniques: foot-in-the-door (Freedman and Fraser, 1966: small request first), door-in-the-face (Cialdini et al., 1975: large request refused, then smaller one), low-ball (agree, then the cost rises), that's-not-all. Cialdini's principles: reciprocity, commitment/consistency, social proof, liking, authority, scarcity.

Obedience is following a direct command from an authority figure. Stanley Milgram (1963), Yale: participants as "teachers" were told to give increasing "shocks" (up to 450 V) to a learner; 65 percent went to the maximum. Obedience fell when the learner was close, the experimenter was absent, or peers rebelled. Milgram explained it with the agentic state.

Power of roles: Philip Zimbardo's Stanford Prison Experiment (1971). Healthy college men randomly assigned as guards or prisoners in a mock prison quickly adopted their roles: guards became abusive, prisoners passive and distressed; the planned two-week study was stopped after six days. It illustrates how social roles and situations shape behaviour, linked to deindividuation (uniforms, mirrored sunglasses). It has been criticised for demand characteristics and Zimbardo's own role as superintendent. Do not confuse with Sherif's Robbers Cave (intergroup conflict) or Milgram (obedience).

Reactance (Jack Brehm, 1966): when people perceive a threat to a behavioural freedom, they experience an aversive motivational state that pushes them to restore the freedom, often by doing the forbidden thing or rejecting the persuasive message (the boomerang effect). Example: a "Do not touch" sign increases touching; heavy-handed anti-smoking warnings can strengthen a teen's urge to smoke. Reactance explains why too much pressure undermines compliance.

Key distinctions

TypeSource of pressureClassic study
ConformityImplicit group norm, no direct requestAsch 1951; Sherif 1935
ComplianceDirect request from a peer/strangerFreedman and Fraser 1966
ObedienceDirect command from authorityMilgram 1963
Power of rolesAssigned social role and situationZimbardo 1971
Normative influenceDesire to be liked/acceptedAsch
Informational influenceDesire to be correctSherif
ReactanceThreatened freedomBrehm 1966

MCQ traps

  • Normative vs informational swap. "Desire to be correct and accurate" is informational; "desire to be accepted and liked" is normative. "Perceived power and authority" points to obedience.
  • Conformity vs compliance. Conformity: behaviour changes due to group pressure, nobody asks; compliance: a direct request. "Both refer to obedience" is wrong because obedience needs an authority's command.
  • Garbled names. Options like "Milgram's message experiment" (Milgram did the small-world letter study, not about roles) or "Zilstein's shock research" are decoys. Power of roles = Zimbardo.
  • Robbers Cave is about realistic group conflict, not roles.
  • Reactance is resistance, not compliance. "Tendency to follow norms without question" or "comply with direct requests" are the opposite of reactance.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q25: Example of the power of roles → Zimbardo's prison experiment; guards and prisoners were randomly assigned yet became their roles.
  • Q26: Reactance is → The motivation to restore freedom when it is threatened; Brehm's theory of resistance to pressure.
  • Q41: Normative social influence → Influence based on the desire to be accepted and liked by others; desire to be correct is informational.
  • Q94: Conformity vs compliance → Conformity is behaviour change due to social pressure, compliance is following direct requests; neither is obedience, which needs authority.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

At level 2 you know conformity, compliance and obedience apart. What still costs marks: (1) mapping the two classic conformity studies to the right type of influence: Sherif (1935) autokinetic = informational (ambiguous task), Asch (1951) lines = normative (clear task, wrong group); (2) mixing up the two compliance tricks and their authors: foot-in-the-door is Freedman and Fraser (1966), door-in-the-face is Cialdini et al. (1975); (3) attaching the wrong name to a famous study: roles = Zimbardo, obedience = Milgram, intergroup conflict at Robbers Cave = Sherif, reactance = Jack Brehm.

TheoristYearIdea
Deutsch and Gerard1955Normative (be liked) vs informational (be correct) social influence
Muzafer Sherif1935Autokinetic effect: group norm forms in an ambiguous task (informational)
Solomon Asch1951Line judgements: about 37 percent conformity on critical trials (normative)
Herbert Kelman1958Compliance, identification, internalisation: three levels of influence
Freedman and Fraser1966Foot-in-the-door: small request first, then the big one
Robert Cialdini et al.1975Door-in-the-face: big request refused, then the smaller real one
Stanley Milgram1963Obedience to authority: 65 percent went to 450 V; agentic state
