Memory hooks. Kanner = Kid alone (1943 comes first, the classic loner); Asperger = Articulate (1944, one year later, talkative "little professors"). Wing gave autism three wings: the triad. Braid braided Greek "Hypnos" into a new word: Braid named it. Charcot vs Nancy: Charcot's hypnosis was Sick (only hysterics, Salpetriere = S), Nancy's hypnosis was Normal (Bernheim = suggestion in everyone, Nancy = N). Freud went to both: Charcot 1885, Bernheim 1889.

Key distinctions

PairFirstSecond
DSM-IV vs DSM-5Triad (Wing), 4 to 5 separate PDD diagnosesDyad, single ASD + severity levels
Criterion A vs B countA: all 3 of 3 social-communication deficitsB: at least 2 of 4 RRBs
Core criterion vs specifierSocial communication, repetitive behaviourIntellectual impairment, language impairment, catatonia
ASD vs social (pragmatic) communication disorderSocial deficits + RRBsSocial deficits, no RRBs
Kanner vs Asperger1943, Baltimore, language delay, aloneness1944, Vienna, fluent speech, narrow interests
Salpetriere vs NancyCharcot: hypnosis is a hysterical, pathological stateLiebeault and Bernheim: hypnosis is suggestion in normal people
Mesmer vs BraidAnimal magnetism, "mesmerism"Coined "hypnosis", attention-based
Hilgard vs SpanosNeodissociation, special stateSociocognitive, role enactment, no special state
Indian vs American autism toolISAA (NIMH, 40 items)CARS (Schopler, 15 items)

MCQ traps

  • "IQ below 70" looks clinical, but it is the marker of intellectual disability; in ASD it appears only as the specifier "with accompanying intellectual impairment", never as a core symptom.
  • "Communication skills" tempts you because DSM-5 removed the separate communication domain of Wing's triad, but communication was folded into criterion A, so it is still core.
  • Lorna Wing as "first to describe autism" is wrong: Kanner (1943) and Asperger (1944) described it; Wing gave the triad and popularised the Asperger label.
  • Bleuler coined the word "autism" but for schizophrenia, not for a childhood disorder; if the stem says "first described the childhood syndrome", answer Kanner.
  • Mesmer as the coiner of "hypnosis" is wrong: Mesmer gave animal magnetism; Braid named hypnosis.
  • Bernheim vs Charcot: if the stem says hypnosis works on normal people through suggestion, it is Bernheim (Nancy), not Charcot, who tied hypnosis to hysteria.
  • Charcot Q: choosing only "Hypnosis" or only "Suggestion" loses the mark; he used hypnotic suggestion, so both (A) and (B). "Relaxation" is a later behavioural idea (Jacobson), unrelated.
  • Hypnosis distractors: "heightened arousal" is wrong (EEG resembles relaxed waking); "enhanced creativity" and "increased self-esteem" are not defining features. The answer is increased suggestibility.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q97: which is NOT a DSM-5 core symptom of autism → I.Q. is below 70. Communication skills and social reciprocity are criterion A, unusual repetitive behaviour is criterion B; IQ is only a specifier.
  • Q18: which disorder is neurodevelopmental → ADHD. OCD is in the obsessive-compulsive chapter, GAD in anxiety disorders, borderline in personality disorders.
  • Q55: Charcot showed hysterical limb anaesthesia or fainting could be produced by → Both (A) and (B), hypnosis and suggestion. "Relaxation" is the throwaway.
  • Q39: chief characteristic of hypnosis → Increased suggestibility. Heightened arousal, enhanced creativity and increased self-esteem are wrong for the reasons above.
  • Q85 (key disputed): Indian tool for severity and disability in autism → official key Childhood Autism Rating Scale, but the textbook-correct answer is ISAA, the Indian scale prescribed for certification. CARS is American; CGAS is a global functioning scale; CBCL (Achenbach) is a broad behaviour checklist. Full detail in topic 40.

Self-test

  1. Who described "early infantile autism" in 1943? (Leo Kanner)
  2. Who coined the word "autism", and for which disorder? (Eugen Bleuler, 1911, schizophrenia)
  3. Triad of impairments: who, and the three parts? (Lorna Wing and Judith Gould, 1979: social interaction, communication, imagination)
  4. DSM-5-TR criterion A needs how many of three; criterion B how many of four? (All 3; at least 2)
  5. Name the three severity levels. (Requiring support; substantial support; very substantial support)
  6. Who coined "hypnosis" and in which year? (James Braid, 1843)
  7. Which school said hypnosis is suggestion in normal people, and who led it? (Nancy school: Liebeault and Bernheim)
  8. Hidden observer and neodissociation theory belong to whom? (Ernest Hilgard, 1977)
31Defense mechanisms and Freud's id, ego, superego
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q30, Q49

From scratch

Sigmund Freud proposed the structural model of personality in The Ego and the Id (1923). It sits on top of his earlier topographical model (1900: conscious, preconscious, unconscious). The structural model divides the psyche into three agencies that are always negotiating with one another.

The id is present from birth, is entirely unconscious, and is the reservoir of instinctual drives (libido or Eros, and the aggressive or death drive, Thanatos). It runs on the pleasure principle: immediate gratification, no logic, no sense of time. Its mode of thinking is primary process (wish-fulfilling imagery, as in dreams). The ego develops out of the id through contact with the outside world. It runs on the reality principle and secondary process thinking (logic, planning, delay of gratification). It is partly conscious, partly unconscious, and it is the executive: it mediates between the id's desires, the superego's moral demands, and the constraints of reality. The superego forms around age 5 to 6, at the resolution of the Oedipus complex, by internalising parental and social standards. It has two parts: the conscience (punishes with guilt) and the ego ideal (rewards with pride). It runs on what is sometimes called the morality principle and can be as irrational and absolute as the id.

When the demands of id, superego and reality clash, the ego experiences anxiety (Freud named reality, neurotic and moral anxiety). To protect itself, the ego uses defense mechanisms: unconscious strategies that distort or deny reality to reduce anxiety. Freud described several; his daughter Anna Freud systematised them in The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936). George Vaillant later arranged them in a maturity hierarchy: psychotic (denial of external reality, distortion, delusional projection), immature (projection, acting out, passive aggression), neurotic (repression, displacement, reaction formation, intellectualisation, rationalisation) and mature (sublimation, humour, altruism, suppression, anticipation).

Core mechanisms with examples: Repression (the basic defense): pushing a threatening memory or wish out of awareness, e.g. no memory of a childhood trauma. Projection: attributing your own unacceptable impulse to someone else, e.g. a man who is attracted to a colleague accuses her of flirting with him. Displacement: redirecting an impulse from a threatening target to a safer one, e.g. shouted at by the boss, you shout at your younger brother. Reaction formation: converting an impulse into its exaggerated opposite, e.g. excessive sweetness toward a hated sibling. Rationalisation: giving a plausible but false reason, e.g. "I did not want that job anyway" (sour grapes). Sublimation: channelling an impulse into a socially valued outlet, e.g. aggression into surgery or boxing; Freud saw it as the only fully healthy defense. Regression: retreating to an earlier developmental stage. Denial: refusing to accept an external reality. Intellectualisation: discussing a painful event in cold abstract terms. Identification with the aggressor (Anna Freud): taking on the traits of a feared person.

Key distinctions

PairHow to tell them apart
Projection vs displacementProjection moves the impulse onto another person ("he hates me"); displacement keeps the impulse as yours but changes the target ("I hate my brother instead of my boss").
Repression vs suppressionRepression is unconscious forgetting; suppression is conscious, deliberate postponement and is a mature defense.
Sublimation vs displacementBoth redirect, but sublimation's outlet is socially valued and productive; displacement's is simply safer.
Reaction formation vs rationalisationReaction formation shows the opposite feeling; rationalisation offers a false reason.
Ego vs superegoEgo mediates (reality principle); superego judges (moral standards, guilt).
Id vs egoId: pleasure principle, primary process, fully unconscious. Ego: reality principle, secondary process, partly conscious.

MCQ traps

  • "Mediates between desires and social rules" sounds moral, so superego tempts; but mediating is the ego's executive job. The superego is one of the parties, not the referee.
  • "Self" as an option belongs to Rogers or Jung, not to Freud's structural model.
  • Projection vs displacement: look for whether the feeling is attributed to someone (projection) or discharged on someone (displacement).
  • Defenses are unconscious by definition; a stem describing deliberate avoidance points to suppression or coping, not a classic defense.
  • The systematiser of defenses is Anna Freud (1936), not Sigmund; the maturity hierarchy is Vaillant.
  • Structural model (id/ego/superego, 1923) is not the topographical model (conscious/preconscious/unconscious, 1900).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q30: defense where one unconsciously attributes one's own unacceptable thoughts or feelings to someone else → Projection, because the impulse is relocated into another person; rationalisation, displacement and sublimation do not attribute.
  • Q49: part of personality that mediates between desires and social rules → Ego, the executive operating on the reality principle between id and superego.
32Rogers' client-centred core conditions
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q48

From scratch

Carl Rogers founded non-directive therapy (Counseling and Psychotherapy, 1942), renamed it client-centred therapy (Client-Centered Therapy, 1951) and later person-centred approach. It is a pillar of humanistic psychology, the "third force" alongside psychoanalysis and behaviourism (with Maslow).

His theory of personality: every organism has an actualising tendency, an innate drive toward growth and self-actualisation. Each person builds a self-concept. Children need positive regard from others; when love is given only under conditions of worth ("I love you when you get good marks"), the child learns to disown experiences that do not fit. The gap between the self-concept (or real self) and actual experience is called incongruence; the gap between real self and ideal self produces anxiety and maladjustment. The goal is the fully functioning person.

Therapy therefore does not interpret, advise or teach. It provides a relationship in which the client's own actualising tendency can resume. In his 1957 paper "The necessary and sufficient conditions of therapeutic personality change" Rogers listed six conditions; three, provided by the therapist, are the famous core conditions: (1) Congruence or genuineness: the therapist is real, transparent, not hiding behind a professional facade. (2) Unconditional positive regard (UPR): warm, non-judgemental acceptance of the client as a person, with no conditions of worth. This directly undoes the conditions of worth that created the problem, which is why it is usually called the critical or defining feature. (3) Accurate empathic understanding: sensing the client's inner world "as if" it were one's own, without losing the "as if" quality, and communicating that understanding back. The other three conditions: two persons in psychological contact, the client is in a state of incongruence (vulnerable, anxious), and the client perceives the therapist's UPR and empathy at least to a minimal degree.

Key technique: reflection of feeling (and restatement, clarification). Example: client says "Nobody at home ever listens to me." Therapist: "You feel unheard and alone, even with your own family." No advice, no interpretation of childhood causes. Rogers also pioneered recording sessions for research and the Q-sort technique to measure self versus ideal-self discrepancy.

Key distinctions

ConceptNot to be confused with
Empathy (feeling with, "as if")Sympathy (feeling sorry for); identification (losing the "as if")
Unconditional positive regard (accept the person)Approving every behaviour; positive reinforcement (contingent, behavioural)
Congruence (therapist genuineness)Incongruence (client's self vs experience mismatch)
Rogers: real self vs ideal self, client-centred therapyMaslow: hierarchy of needs, self-actualisation, peak experiences
Client-centred (non-directive, reflection)Psychoanalysis (interpretation of unconscious), behaviour therapy (modify behaviour), Gestalt (Perls, here-and-now, directive)

MCQ traps

  • "Analysing unconscious desires" is psychoanalysis; Rogers explicitly rejected interpretation.
  • "Strict behaviour modification" and "aversive stimuli" are behaviour therapy; Rogerian therapy is non-directive.
  • If asked for the three core conditions, do not pick "interpretation", "advice" or "transference"; the triad is genuineness, UPR, empathy.
  • Self-actualisation as a term is shared by Rogers, Maslow and originally Kurt Goldstein; the hierarchy of needs is Maslow only.
  • Q-sort (self-concept measurement) is Rogers' research tool, though the technique was devised by William Stephenson.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q48: critical feature of Rogers' client-centred therapy → Therapist providing unconditional positive regard, because accepting the client without conditions of worth is the core relational condition; the other options belong to psychoanalysis and behaviour therapy.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

At level 2 you know the three core conditions. The step to 3: (1) congruence is the therapist's genuineness, while incongruence is the client's mismatch between self-concept and experience; (2) Rogers' 1957 paper listed six necessary and sufficient conditions, of which the therapist supplies three; (3) names cluster around self-actualisation: the term is Kurt Goldstein's, the hierarchy is Maslow's, the actualising tendency in therapy is Rogers'; (4) the Q-sort was devised by William Stephenson and used by Rogers to measure self vs ideal-self gaps. Finally, the hallmark technique is reflection of feeling, not interpretation or advice, and empathy means feeling with the client, not sympathy (feeling sorry for them). Options offering transference, free association or reinforcement belong to Freud or the behaviourists, not Rogers. Goal of therapy in his words: the fully functioning person.

TheoristYearIdea
Carl Rogers1942Counseling and Psychotherapy: non-directive therapy
Carl Rogers1951Client-Centered Therapy: renamed approach
Carl Rogers1957Six necessary and sufficient conditions; core triad: congruence, UPR, empathy
Abraham Maslow1943Hierarchy of needs; self-actualisation; peak experiences
Kurt Goldstein1939Coined self-actualisation (The Organism)
William Stephenson1953Q-sort technique
Fritz Perls1951Gestalt therapy: here-and-now, directive (distractor)
Albert Ellis1955REBT: disputes irrational beliefs, directive (distractor)
Sigmund Freud1890s onwardPsychoanalysis: interpretation of the unconscious (distractor)
Aaron Beck1960sCognitive therapy: challenges automatic thoughts (distractor)
  1. Therapist being real, with no professional facade: which condition? (Congruence or genuineness)
  2. How many conditions did Rogers list in 1957, and how many are the therapist's? (Six; three)
  3. Who coined "self-actualisation" before Maslow and Rogers? (Kurt Goldstein)
  4. Which condition directly undoes "conditions of worth"? (Unconditional positive regard)
  5. Sensing the client's world "as if" it were your own, without losing the "as if": the term? (Accurate empathic understanding)
33Bandura (Bobo doll) and theorist-to-theory matching
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q20, Q7, Q9, Q73, Q56

From scratch

The problem Bandura solved. Classical behaviourism (John B. Watson, 1913) and operant conditioning (B. F. Skinner, 1938) said behaviour is learned when you perform it and you are reinforced. But a child picks up an accent, a swear word or a cricket shot without ever being rewarded for it. Albert Bandura (1925-2021, Canadian-born, Stanford) argued that most human learning is observational learning (also called modelling or vicarious learning): we learn by watching a model and the consequences the model receives.

The Bobo doll experiments.

  • Bandura, Ross and Ross (1961): Stanford nursery-school children (about 3 to 6 years) watched an adult in the playroom either attack an inflatable Bobo doll (punching it, hitting it with a mallet, tossing it, saying things like "Sock him in the nose") or play quietly. Left alone with toys (after a mild frustration), children who saw the aggressive model copied the specific novel acts and words, far more than control children. Boys showed more imitative physical aggression than girls.
  • Bandura, Ross and Ross (1963): the aggression was shown live, on film, or by a cartoon character (a "cat lady"). All three increased aggression, so media models work too. This study is the root of the television-violence debate.
  • Bandura (1965): children saw the model rewarded, punished, or receive no consequence. The model-punished group imitated less. But when the experimenter then offered every child a reward to show what the model did, all three groups reproduced the acts equally well. This proved the learning-performance distinction: learning happened in all groups; consequences only decided whether the child chose to perform it.

Four processes of observational learning (Bandura, 1977, Social Learning Theory). Mnemonic ARRM (or "ARMM"):

ProcessQuestion it answersExample
1. AttentionDid you notice the model? (Attractive, similar, high-status models get more attention.)Watching a senior's viva closely.
2. RetentionCan you store it, as images or verbal codes, and rehearse it?Remembering how she structured each answer.
3. (Motor) reproductionAre you physically and cognitively able to do it?Actually delivering a fluent answer.
4. MotivationIs there a reason to perform it (direct, vicarious or self-reinforcement)?You saw her get selected.

Vicarious reinforcement and punishment (Bandura). Seeing a model rewarded raises the chance you will perform the act (vicarious reinforcement); seeing the model punished lowers it (vicarious punishment). Bandura also described self-reinforcement: rewarding yourself against internal standards. Modelling can teach new behaviour, inhibit or disinhibit existing behaviour, or simply facilitate it.

Reciprocal determinism (Bandura, 1978). Behaviour is not caused one-way by the environment (Skinner's view). Three factors influence each other: Person (cognitions, beliefs, expectations), Behaviour, and Environment, often drawn as a triangle (triadic reciprocal causation). Example: an anxious student (person) avoids class (behaviour), falls behind so teachers become stricter (environment), which deepens the anxiety.

Self-efficacy (Bandura, 1977, "Self-efficacy: Toward a unifying theory of behavioral change"). Self-efficacy is the belief that I can successfully carry out the behaviour needed in a specific situation. Bandura separated efficacy expectations ("can I do it?") from outcome expectations ("will doing it lead to the result?"). Four sources, strongest first:

  1. Mastery (performance) experiences: your own past successes; the most powerful source.
  2. Vicarious experiences: seeing someone similar succeed ("if she can, I can").
  3. Verbal (social) persuasion: encouragement from others.
  4. Physiological and emotional states: reading calm as capability and a racing heart as inability.

Mnemonic: "My Very Valuable Physiology" (Mastery, Vicarious, Verbal, Physiological). Bandura later added collective efficacy (a group's shared belief) and wrote Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control (1997).

The name history (a favourite match question).

  • John Dollard and Neal Miller (Yale): Social Learning and Imitation (1941) and Personality and Psychotherapy (1950). They translated Freud into Clark Hull's drive-reduction theory. Four elements of learning: drive, cue, response, reward. They also gave approach-avoidance conflict, and (with Doob, Mowrer and Sears, 1939) the frustration-aggression hypothesis. Imitation still needed the imitator's own reinforcement, so this is early social learning theory.
  • Bandura and Richard Walters: Adolescent Aggression (1959) and Social Learning and Personality Development (1963). Learning without the observer's own reinforcement = later social learning theory.
  • Bandura (1986), Social Foundations of Thought and Action: renamed his approach social cognitive theory.
  • Julian Rotter had a separate "social learning theory" (1954): behaviour depends on expectancy and reinforcement value; his famous construct is locus of control (1966).

Why it matters clinically. Modelling therapy for phobias (Bandura, Blanchard and Ritter, 1969, participant modelling with snake phobia), social skills training, behavioural rehearsal, self-efficacy in health behaviour change (quitting smoking, adherence), and the media violence debate all come from this work.

Master matching table: theorist to theory (frequent pairs in Indian entrance MCQs).

Who said what

Hooks:

  • BOBO = Bandura Observes, Bandura Obtains: the child observes and obtains the behaviour without any reward.
  • ARRM: you need an ARM plus one more R to copy a model: Attention, Retention, Reproduction, Motivation.
  • Dollard and Miller came first ("D" before the alphabet's later "W" of Walters): early = Dollard-Miller 1941, later = Bandura-Walters 1963.
  • Skinner is RADICAL (skinhead punk = radical); Watson is the METHOD man who only watched behaviour.

Key distinctions

PairHow to tell them apart
Early (Dollard and Miller) vs later (Bandura and Walters) social learningEarly: Hullian, imitation must itself be reinforced. Later: learning by observation alone, cognitive.
Bandura's social learning vs Rotter's social learningBandura: modelling, self-efficacy. Rotter: expectancy, reinforcement value, locus of control.
Self-efficacy (Bandura) vs self-esteem vs locus of control (Rotter)Efficacy: task-specific "I can do this". Esteem: global self-worth. Locus: who controls outcomes, me or outside forces.
Vicarious reinforcement vs direct reinforcementVicarious: the model is rewarded. Direct: the learner is rewarded (Skinner).
Learning vs performance (Bandura, 1965)Learning can occur silently; reinforcement governs performance.
Radical (Skinner) vs methodological (Watson) behaviourismSkinner treats private events as behaviour; Watson excluded them from science.
Reciprocal determinism (Bandura) vs reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe)Bandura: person-behaviour-environment loop. Wolpe: relaxation inhibits anxiety in desensitisation.

MCQ traps

  • Skinner for the Bobo doll. Wrong: Skinner needed the learner's own reinforcement; the Bobo doll shows learning without it, which is Bandura.
  • Carl Rogers or Freud for observational learning. Rogers is client-centred humanistic therapy; Freud is psychoanalysis. Neither ran modelling experiments.
  • Dollard and Miller = later social learning. No: they are the earlier (1941/1950) Hullian version; Bandura and Walters are later (1963).
  • Radical behaviourism = Watson. Radical behaviourism is Skinner; Watson's is methodological behaviourism.
  • Self-actualisation given to Skinner or a behaviourist. It belongs to Maslow (and also appears in Rogers), humanistic psychology.
  • Locus of control = Bandura. Locus of control is Julian Rotter (1966); Bandura's construct is self-efficacy.
  • Verbal persuasion is the strongest source of self-efficacy. No: mastery experiences are the strongest (Bandura, 1977).
  • Stream of consciousness = Wundt, broaden-and-build = Csikszentmihalyi. Stream of consciousness is William James (1890); broaden-and-build is Barbara Fredrickson; flow is Csikszentmihalyi.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q20: who is known for observational learning and the Bobo doll experiment → Albert Bandura. Distractors: Freud (psychoanalysis, unconscious), Skinner (operant conditioning, needs direct reinforcement), Rogers (client-centred therapy).
  • Q7: match Dollard and Miller, Skinner, Bandura and Walters, Maslow with radical behaviourism, self-actualisation, early and later social learning → A-3, B-1, C-4, D-2. Fix Skinner = radical behaviourism first: that kills options a and d; then Dollard and Miller = early kills c. Wrong options gave self-actualisation to Skinner or Dollard and Miller.
  • Q9: perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal events → Titchener (structuralism); Bruner (perceptual readiness, "New Look") and the other names are distractors.
  • Q73: "stream of consciousness" coined by → William James (1890); Wundt is structural elements and the first lab, Jung is analytical psychology, Skinner is operant conditioning.
  • Q56: Barbara Fredrickson is known for → broaden-and-build theory; flow is Csikszentmihalyi, cognitive distortions are Beck, psychodynamic is Freud.

Self-test

  1. Who ran the Bobo doll studies, and in which years? (Bandura, Ross and Ross, 1961 and 1963; Bandura 1965)
  2. Name Bandura's four processes of observational learning. (Attention, retention, motor reproduction, motivation)
  3. What did the 1965 study prove? (The learning-performance distinction: consequences affect performance, not learning)
  4. Person, behaviour and environment influencing each other is called? (Reciprocal determinism, Bandura)
  5. The strongest of the four sources of self-efficacy (Bandura, 1977)? (Mastery experiences)
  6. Drive, cue, response, reward: whose early social learning theory? (Dollard and Miller)
  7. Locus of control belongs to which theorist? (Julian Rotter, 1966)
  8. Who coined "stream of consciousness", and who gave broaden-and-build? (William James, 1890; Barbara Fredrickson)
34Hypnosis
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q39, Q55

From scratch

Hypnosis is a procedure in which a hypnotist suggests changes in sensation, perception, thought or behaviour, and a participant responds. Its chief characteristic is increased suggestibility: heightened responsiveness to suggestion together with narrowed, focused attention and reduced critical judgement. Induction usually involves relaxation and focusing on one stimulus, so arousal goes down, not up. EEG during hypnosis resembles relaxed wakefulness, not sleep.

History in order: Franz Anton Mesmer (1770s, Vienna and Paris) claimed cures through "animal magnetism"; a 1784 French commission (including Benjamin Franklin) attributed the effects to imagination. James Braid, a Scottish surgeon, coined "hypnosis" (from Greek hypnos, sleep) in 1843 and recast it as focused attention. Jean-Martin Charcot at the Salpetriere in Paris showed in dramatic demonstrations that hysterical symptoms (paralysis, anaesthesia, loss of feeling in limbs, fainting) could be produced and removed by hypnosis and suggestion; he believed only hysterics could be hypnotised. The rival Nancy school (Liebeault and Bernheim) argued hypnosis is simply suggestion and normal people are hypnotisable, and Bernheim won that debate. Freud studied with Charcot (1885 to 1886), used hypnosis with Breuer (Studies on Hysteria, 1895, Anna O. and the cathartic method), then abandoned it for free association. Pierre Janet developed dissociation theory from hypnotic work.

Two modern theories. Neodissociation theory (Ernest Hilgard, 1970s): consciousness splits; a "hidden observer" remains aware of pain while the hypnotised person reports none (ice-water experiments). Social-cognitive or role theory (Theodore Sarbin, Nicholas Spanos, Barber, Kirsch): hypnotised people are enacting the role of a hypnotised person, driven by expectations and motivation, not an altered state. Hypnotisability is a stable trait measured by the Stanford Hypnotic Susceptibility Scale (Weitzenhoffer and Hilgard) and the Harvard Group Scale; roughly 10 to 15 percent are highly hypnotisable.

What hypnosis can and cannot do: effective for pain relief (hypnoanalgesia), as an adjunct in anxiety, IBS and habit control; produces posthypnotic suggestion and posthypnotic amnesia. It does not reliably enhance memory accuracy (it increases confidence and false memories, hence hypnotically refreshed testimony is suspect), does not give superhuman strength, and generally cannot make people act against their values. Example: told "your arm is becoming light and will float up", a highly hypnotisable person's arm rises and feels involuntary.

Key distinctions

PairDistinction
Mesmer vs BraidMesmer: animal magnetism (1770s). Braid: coined "hypnosis" (1843).
Charcot vs Nancy schoolCharcot: hypnotisability is a sign of hysteria. Liebeault and Bernheim: it is normal suggestibility.
Hilgard vs SpanosHilgard: neodissociation, hidden observer. Spanos/Sarbin: social-cognitive role enactment.
Hypnosis vs sleepHypnosis: awake, alpha-like EEG, responsive. Sleep: unresponsive, sleep stages.
Hypnosis vs meditationHypnosis: responding to someone's suggestions. Meditation: self-regulated attention, no suggestion needed.

MCQ traps

  • "Heightened arousal" is wrong: induction lowers arousal; the defining feature is suggestibility.
  • "Enhanced memory" or "enhanced creativity" sound appealing but are not defining; hypnotic recall is less reliable.
  • For Charcot, "hypnosis" alone and "suggestion" alone are both partial; when "both" is offered, pick it.
  • The term hypnosis is Braid's, not Mesmer's; "mesmerism" is Mesmer's.
  • Freud dropped hypnosis for free association; do not list hypnosis as a core psychoanalytic technique.
  • Hidden observer = Hilgard, not Charcot or Janet.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q39: chief characteristic of hypnosis → Increased suggestibility, the defining hallmark; arousal actually falls and creativity or self-esteem are not core.
  • Q55: Charcot showed hysterical phenomena such as loss of feeling in limbs or fainting could be produced by → Both hypnosis and suggestion, since his Salpetriere demonstrations induced and removed symptoms with both.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

At level 2 you know hypnosis means heightened suggestibility. The step to 3 is the history chain and the two modern theories: (1) Mesmer = animal magnetism (1770s), Braid = coined "hypnosis" (1843); (2) Charcot (Salpetriere) tied hypnotisability to hysteria, the Nancy school (Liebeault and Bernheim) said it was normal suggestion; (3) Hilgard's neodissociation and the hidden observer vs the social-cognitive role theory of Sarbin and Spanos; (4) Freud used hypnosis with Breuer (1895) and then dropped it for free association. Remember too that induction lowers arousal, EEG looks like relaxed wakefulness rather than sleep, and hypnotically refreshed memories are less, not more, reliable. Hypnotisability is a stable trait; only about 10 to 15 percent of people are highly hypnotisable, and it is measured with the Stanford scale.

TheoristYearIdea
Franz Anton Mesmer1770sAnimal magnetism ("mesmerism")
James Braid1843Coined "hypnosis"; recast it as focused attention
Jean-Martin Charcot1880sSalpetriere: hysterical symptoms produced and removed by hypnosis and suggestion
Liebeault and Bernheim1880sNancy school: hypnosis is suggestion, normal people are hypnotisable
Breuer and Freud1895Studies on Hysteria: cathartic method, Anna O.
Pierre Janet1889Dissociation theory from hypnotic work
Ernest Hilgard1970sNeodissociation theory; hidden observer

Weitzenhoffer and Hilgard1959Stanford Hypnotic Susceptibility Scale
  1. Who coined the word "hypnosis", and when? (James Braid, 1843)
  2. Hidden observer during ice-water pain: whose theory? (Ernest Hilgard, neodissociation)
  3. Which school argued hypnosis is normal suggestion, against Charcot? (Nancy school: Liebeault and Bernheim)
  4. Hypnosis is role enactment, not an altered state: name two theorists. (Theodore Sarbin, Nicholas Spanos)
  5. Does hypnosis raise or lower arousal, and does it improve memory accuracy? (Lowers arousal; no, it raises confidence and false memories)
35Double bind (Bateson) and countertransference
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q98, Q99

From scratch

Double bind: introduced by Gregory Bateson with Don Jackson, Jay Haley and John Weakland in "Toward a theory of schizophrenia" (1956), from the Palo Alto (later Mental Research Institute) group. A double bind is a communication in which a person, usually a child, receives two contradictory messages at different levels from a significant other, typically a verbal message contradicted by a non-verbal one, while a third, implicit injunction forbids commenting on the contradiction or leaving the field. Whatever the child does is wrong. Classic example: a mother says "Come and give me a hug, I love you", then stiffens and pulls away when the child approaches; if the child withdraws she says "Don't you love your mother?" Bateson proposed that repeated exposure could contribute to schizophrenia. The causal claim is no longer accepted (schizophrenia is understood through the stress-vulnerability model with strong genetic loading), but the concept shaped family systems and communication therapy.

Related family-theory concepts for schizophrenia, often put together in MCQs: schizophrenogenic mother (Frieda Fromm-Reichmann, 1948: cold, domineering, rejecting), marital schism and marital skew (Theodore Lidz), pseudomutuality and pseudohostility (Lyman Wynne), and expressed emotion (George Brown, Vaughn and Leff: criticism, hostility, emotional overinvolvement), which predicts relapse, not onset, and is the one with strong empirical support.

Transference (Freud): the patient unconsciously redirects feelings from earlier significant figures (usually parents) onto the therapist. Positive transference = affection, idealisation; negative transference = hostility, distrust. Analysis of transference is central to psychoanalysis. Countertransference (Freud, 1910): the therapist's emotional reactions to the patient, originally from the therapist's own unresolved conflicts. Freud saw it as an obstacle to be overcome by the analyst's own analysis; later writers (Paula Heimann, 1950; Racker; Winnicott's "Hate in the countertransference", 1949) treated it as a useful clue to the patient's inner world. It can be positive countertransference (over-liking, rescuing) or negative countertransference (irritation, boredom, dislike, hostility toward the patient).

The key to all four transference terms: first ask whose feelings (patient = transference, therapist = countertransference), then ask which direction (warm = positive, hostile = negative). Example: a therapist feels impatient and cold toward a demanding client who reminds him of his critical father: negative countertransference.

Key distinctions

TermWhose feelingsValence
Positive transferencePatient toward therapistWarm, idealising
Negative transferencePatient toward therapistHostile
Positive countertransferenceTherapist toward patientWarm, over-involved
Negative countertransferenceTherapist toward patientHostile, rejecting
  • Double bind (Bateson) vs schizophrenogenic mother (Fromm-Reichmann) vs marital skew/schism (Lidz) vs pseudomutuality (Wynne) vs expressed emotion (Brown).
  • Double bind vs mixed message or ordinary faulty communication: the double bind needs the contradiction, a significant relationship, and the impossibility of escape or comment.

MCQ traps

  • "Faulty communication" is a vague umbrella; when the stem describes two simultaneous conflicting messages, the technical term is double bind.
  • Bateson is the double-bind name, not Lidz or Wynne; do not give the schizophrenogenic mother to Bateson.
  • Expressed emotion predicts relapse, not causation of schizophrenia.
  • "Negative transference" tempts when you see "negative feelings", but the feelings are the therapist's, so it must be countertransference.
  • Plain "countertransference" is right but less precise; when the stem specifies negative feelings and "negative countertransference" is offered, the specific option wins.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q98: one parent conveys two conflicting messages to the child at the same time → Double bind communication, Bateson and colleagues' 1956 term for a no-win contradiction across message levels.
  • Q99: therapist expresses his negative feelings towards patients → Negative counter transference, because the feelings are the therapist's (counter) and hostile (negative); negative transference would be the patient's.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

At level 2 you know a double bind is two contradictory messages. What still costs marks: (1) forgetting the third ingredient, the hidden ban on commenting on the contradiction or leaving the relationship, which is what makes it a bind rather than a mere mixed message (Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland, 1956); (2) handing the family-theory labels to the wrong person, because Bateson, Fromm-Reichmann, Lidz and Wynne all wrote about families of people with schizophrenia; (3) on transference items, answering the valence (positive or negative) before deciding whose feelings they are. Patient's feelings = transference; therapist's feelings = countertransference. Only then add positive or negative.

TheoristYearIdea
Gregory Bateson (with Jackson, Haley, Weakland)1956Double bind: contradictory messages at two levels, no escape, no comment allowed
Frieda Fromm-Reichmann1948Schizophrenogenic mother: cold, domineering, rejecting
Theodore Lidz1957Marital schism (open parental conflict) and marital skew (one dominant disturbed parent)
Lyman Wynne1958Pseudomutuality: a surface of harmony that hides conflict and blocks individuality
George Brown, Birley and Wing1972Expressed emotion (criticism, hostility, emotional overinvolvement) predicts relapse
Sigmund Freud1910Countertransference: the analyst's own unresolved reactions, first seen as an obstacle

  1. Which part of a double bind turns a mixed message into a trap? (the implicit ban on commenting on the contradiction or leaving the field)
  2. Marital schism and marital skew: whose terms? (Theodore Lidz)
  3. Pseudomutuality: whose term? (Lyman Wynne)
  4. Expressed emotion predicts onset or relapse of schizophrenia? (relapse (Brown, Birley and Wing 1972; Vaughn and Leff))
  5. A client feels intense anger toward her therapist that really belongs to her father. Term? (negative transference, because the feelings are the patient's)
36Exposure and response prevention (ERP) and its principle
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q100

From scratch

Exposure and response prevention (ERP, also ExRP) is the first-line psychological treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder. It was pioneered by Victor Meyer (1966, Middlesex Hospital, London, "apotrepic therapy") and developed by Isaac Marks, Stanley Rachman and especially Edna Foa (University of Pennsylvania). Its rationale comes from Mowrer's two-factor theory (1947, 1960): fear of a stimulus is acquired by classical conditioning and maintained by operant (negative) reinforcement, because escape or avoidance reduces anxiety. In OCD, obsessions raise anxiety and compulsions (washing, checking, mental rituals) bring quick relief, which negatively reinforces the compulsion and prevents the person from ever learning that the feared outcome would not occur.

ERP has two parts. Exposure: systematic, prolonged contact with the feared stimulus, in vivo or imaginal (touching a "contaminated" doorknob, leaving the stove unchecked). Response prevention: the client refrains from the ritual and from subtle neutralising or reassurance seeking. Exposure is usually graded up a hierarchy using SUDS (subjective units of distress, Wolpe's 0 to 100 scale). Example: a patient with contamination fears touches a bathroom floor, does not wash for an hour, and records SUDS falling from 85 to 30 over the session and lower again across sessions.

The principle behind it is habituation and extinction. Habituation is the decrease in response to a repeated or prolonged stimulus: anxiety, if not escaped, rises, peaks and then declines on its own (within-session habituation), and starts lower each session (between-session habituation). Extinction is the weakening of a conditioned response when the CS occurs without the UCS (respondent extinction), and the weakening of the ritual when it no longer produces relief (operant extinction). Foa and Kozak's emotional processing theory (1986) adds that the fear structure must be activated and then corrected with disconfirming information. The newer inhibitory learning model (Michelle Craske, 2008, 2014) argues that extinction creates a new "safe" association that competes with the old one, with expectancy violation as the key; this is why relapse can happen through spontaneous recovery, renewal and reinstatement.

Contrast with systematic desensitisation (Joseph Wolpe, 1958), based on reciprocal inhibition or counterconditioning: a hierarchy is climbed while the client is deeply relaxed (Jacobson's progressive muscle relaxation), so a response incompatible with anxiety replaces it. ERP uses no competing response; the anxiety is allowed to run its course. Flooding (in vivo, maximal exposure from the start) and implosion (Stampfl, imaginal and exaggerated, with psychodynamic themes) are also extinction-based exposure methods.

Key distinctions

TechniqueFounderUnderlying principle
ERPMeyer (1966), FoaHabituation and extinction
Systematic desensitisationWolpe (1958)Reciprocal inhibition / counterconditioning
FloodingExposure therapistsExtinction (in vivo, intense)
Implosive therapyStampfl (1967)Extinction (imaginal, exaggerated)
Aversion therapyvariousClassical conditioning (pairing with an aversive UCS)
  • Habituation (non-associative, response to a stimulus wanes with exposure) vs extinction (associative, a learned CS-UCS or response-reinforcer link weakens).
  • Graded exposure is a method of pacing, not a learning principle.

MCQ traps

  • Reciprocal inhibition is Wolpe's principle for systematic desensitisation; it is the most common wrong answer for ERP.
  • Counterconditioning needs a competing response (relaxation, eating); ERP has none.
  • Graded exposure looks right because ERP often is graded, but the question asks for the principle, not the procedure.
  • Do not stop at "exposure" alone; without response prevention the ritual keeps being negatively reinforced and treatment fails.
  • ERP is behavioural; pharmacological first line for OCD is SSRIs (clomipramine the TCA). Do not mix up the two when asked which is "psychological first line".

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q100: ERP is based on the behaviour therapy principle of → Habituation and extinction, because anxiety is allowed to fall naturally without the ritual, so the fear and the compulsion both lose their reinforcement; counterconditioning and reciprocal inhibition need a competing response, and graded exposure is only a technique.
37Reliability vs validity
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q75

From scratch

Every psychological test is judged on two core psychometric properties. Reliability is the consistency of a measure: if you measured the same person again, under similar conditions, would you get the same score? Validity is the accuracy or meaningfulness of a measure: does the test actually measure what it claims to measure, and do the inferences drawn from its scores hold up? The modern framework comes from classical test theory (Spearman, early 1900s), in which every observed score X = true score T + error E. Reliability is the proportion of observed-score variance that is true-score variance, so it runs from 0 to 1; most clinical tests aim for 0.80 or higher, and 0.90 for high-stakes individual decisions.

Types of reliability. (1) Test-retest: give the same test twice and correlate the scores; the result is a coefficient of stability, threatened by practice and memory effects and by real change over the interval. (2) Parallel or alternate forms: two equivalent versions given to the same people; a coefficient of equivalence. (3) Split-half: split one test into two halves (usually odd versus even items), correlate them, then correct upward with the Spearman-Brown prophecy formula, because a half-length test underestimates reliability. (4) Internal consistency: Kuder-Richardson formulas (KR-20, KR-21, Kuder and Richardson 1937) for dichotomous items, and Cronbach's alpha (Cronbach 1951) for multi-point items. (5) Inter-rater (inter-scorer) reliability: agreement between two scorers, indexed by percentage agreement, Cohen's kappa (chance-corrected) or the intraclass correlation. The standard error of measurement (SEM) translates reliability into a band of error around an individual's score: the higher the reliability, the smaller the SEM.

Types of validity. (1) Face validity: the test looks like it measures the construct to the test-taker; it is not a technical form of validity. (2) Content validity: the items adequately sample the whole domain, judged by experts (for example, an anxiety scale that covers cognitive, somatic and behavioural symptoms). (3) Criterion-related validity: scores correlate with an external criterion, either concurrent (criterion measured at the same time, such as a new depression scale against a psychiatrist's diagnosis today) or predictive (criterion measured later, such as an entrance test against final-year marks). (4) Construct validity (Cronbach and Meehl 1955): the test behaves as theory predicts; evidence includes convergent validity (high correlation with measures of the same construct) and discriminant/divergent validity (low correlation with unrelated constructs), formalised in Campbell and Fiske's (1959) multitrait-multimethod matrix. Messick (1989) argued all validity is ultimately construct validity.

The relationship. Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity. An unreliable test cannot be valid, because random error cannot correlate with anything; the validity coefficient can never exceed the square root of the reliability coefficient. But a test can be perfectly reliable and still invalid. The classic picture is the dartboard: tight clustering away from the bullseye is reliable but not valid; tight clustering on the bullseye is both. Example: a bathroom scale that always reads 5 kg too heavy is reliable (consistent) but not valid (inaccurate).

Do not confuse test validity with research validity. Internal validity (Campbell and Stanley 1963) is whether the independent variable truly caused the change in the dependent variable; external validity is whether findings generalise to other people, settings and times. Generalizability theory (Cronbach et al. 1972) is an extension of reliability theory that splits error into multiple sources (raters, occasions, items).

Key distinctions

TermCore questionTypical index
ReliabilityIs it consistent?r tt, alpha, kappa
ValidityIs it accurate, does it measure the construct?Validity coefficient, factor analysis
Test-retest vs parallel formsStability over time vs equivalence of formsCoefficient of stability vs equivalence
Split-half vs alphaTwo halves (needs Spearman-Brown) vs all possible splitsSpearman-Brown, Cronbach's alpha
Concurrent vs predictiveCriterion now vs criterion laterBoth criterion-related
Convergent vs discriminantCorrelates with same construct vs not with differentBoth construct validity (MTMM)
Sensitivity vs specificityDetects true cases vs correctly rejects non-casesDiagnostic accuracy, not reliability

MCQ traps

  • "Consistency" is reliability, "accuracy/meaning" is validity. Validity is the most tempting distractor because both words sound like "good test".
  • Generalizability looks right because G-theory is a reliability framework, but the plain term for consistency is still reliability.
  • Sensitivity is a diagnostic-accuracy index (true positive rate), not consistency.
  • Face validity is not real psychometric validity; if asked which validity is "judged by experts on item coverage", it is content, not face.
  • "A reliable test is always valid" is false; "a valid test must be reliable" is true.
  • Spearman-Brown corrects split-half reliability; it is not a validity formula. KR-20 is for dichotomous (right/wrong) items, alpha for Likert items.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q75: Which term refers to the consistency of a measure? → Reliability, because reliability is by definition the consistency (stability, equivalence, internal agreement) of scores, while validity is about accuracy.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Basics-level students know reliability = consistency and validity = accuracy. The marks are lost on the fine print: (1) matching each reliability coefficient to its method and author (Spearman-Brown corrects split-half; KR-20 is for right/wrong items; Cronbach's alpha for multi-point items; Cohen's kappa for raters); (2) mixing the validity subtypes, especially content (experts judge item coverage) versus face (looks right to the test-taker) and concurrent (criterion now) versus predictive (criterion later); (3) the direction of the relationship: a valid test must be reliable, but a reliable test need not be valid, and the validity coefficient cannot exceed the square root of the reliability coefficient. Keep test validity apart from research validity (internal and external), which is about study designs.

TheoristYearIdea
Charles Spearman1904Classical test theory: observed score = true score + error
Spearman and Brown1910Prophecy formula: steps up split-half reliability to full length
Kuder and Richardson1937KR-20 and KR-21: internal consistency for dichotomous items
Lee Cronbach1951Coefficient alpha: internal consistency for multi-point items
Jacob Cohen1960Kappa: chance-corrected inter-rater agreement
Cronbach and Meehl1955Construct validity: the test behaves as theory predicts
Campbell and Fiske1959Multitrait-multimethod matrix: convergent and discriminant validity
Samuel Messick1989All validity is ultimately construct validity
Campbell and Stanley1963Internal and external validity of research designs (not test validity)
Cronbach and colleagues1972Generalizability theory: splits error into several sources
  1. Which formula corrects a split-half coefficient? (Spearman-Brown prophecy formula)
  2. A 40-item true/false test: which internal-consistency index? (KR-20 (Kuder and Richardson 1937))
  3. Who introduced construct validity, and when? (Cronbach and Meehl, 1955)
  4. New depression scale correlated with a psychiatrist's diagnosis made the same week: which validity? (concurrent, a type of criterion-related validity)
  5. Reliability 0.64: what is the maximum possible validity coefficient? (0.80, the square root of 0.64)
38Rorschach and projective tests
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q17

From scratch

A projective test presents an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus and asks the person to interpret it. The projective hypothesis holds that, because the stimulus has no single correct meaning, people impose their own needs, conflicts, defences and thought organisation onto it. Lawrence Frank (1939) coined the term "projective methods". The approach grew from psychoanalytic theory, and these tests are contrasted with objective (self-report) inventories like the MMPI, which have structured items, fixed response options and standardised scoring.

The Rorschach Inkblot Test. Swiss psychiatrist Hermann Rorschach published it in Psychodiagnostik (1921). It has 10 symmetrical inkblot cards: five achromatic (black and grey: I, IV, V, VI, VII), two black-and-red (II, III), and three fully chromatic (VIII, IX, X). Administration has two phases: the free association (response) phase, where the person says what each blot might be, and the inquiry phase, where the examiner asks where and what made it look that way. Responses are coded for location (W whole, D common detail, Dd unusual detail, S white space), determinants (form F, colour C, human movement M, shading, texture), content (human H, animal A, anatomy etc.), popular responses (P) and form quality (how well the percept fits the blot). Poor form quality and unusual verbalisations index disordered thinking, which is why the test is classically described as assessing thought processes and perceptual-cognitive organisation. The most used scoring system is John Exner's Comprehensive System (1974 onward), now succeeded by R-PAS (Rorschach Performance Assessment System, Meyer et al. 2011). Exner's Perceptual-Thinking Index is used to detect psychotic thought disorder. Earlier systems: Beck, Klopfer, Hertz, Piotrowski, Rapaport-Schafer.

Thematic Apperception Test (TAT). Henry Murray and Christiana Morgan (1935), Harvard, based on Murray's need-press theory. 31 cards (30 pictures plus one blank card, card 16); typically about 20 are given in two sessions. The person tells a story: what led up to the scene, what is happening, what the characters feel and think, how it ends. It assesses needs, motives, interpersonal themes rather than thought organisation. Children's versions: CAT (Bellak, animal figures), and the Indian adaptation by Uma Chowdhury (Indian TAT, CAT).

Other projectives, using Lindzey's (1959) classification by response type: association (Rorschach, Holtzman Inkblot Technique with 45 blots, two parallel forms, one response per card; Jung's word association test), construction (TAT, CAT), completion (Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank, Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study), choice or ordering (Szondi test), and expression (Draw-a-Person, Machover 1949; House-Tree-Person, Buck 1948; Kinetic Family Drawing).

Strengths and criticisms. Projectives are hard to fake, bypass defensiveness and give rich idiographic material. But they have been criticised for weak inter-scorer reliability, inconsistent norms and modest validity (Lilienfeld, Wood and Garb 2000). The Comprehensive System and R-PAS were built specifically to improve reliability and norms. Example: a patient who sees "a bleeding face melting into the walls" on card II with poor form quality suggests perceptual distortion; a TAT story full of failure and punishment suggests a theme of guilt.

Key distinctions

TestAuthor, yearStimulusMain target
RorschachHermann Rorschach, 192110 inkblotsPerception, thought processes, reality testing
HoltzmanHoltzman, 196145 inkblots, forms A and BSame, with better psychometrics
TATMurray and Morgan, 193531 pictures incl. blankNeeds, motives, themes (stories)
CATBellak, 1949Animal picturesChildren's conflicts
MMPIHathaway and McKinley, 1943True/false itemsObjective psychopathology profile
MBTIBriggs and MyersForced-choice itemsJungian type, objective, non-clinical

MCQ traps

  • TAT is the main distractor for "projective" questions; it uses pictures and stories, never inkblots.
  • MMPI assesses psychopathology but is objective, not projective.
  • MBTI is a self-report type indicator, not a clinical or projective tool.
  • Rorschach has 10 cards, TAT 31, Holtzman 45; "the blank card" belongs to TAT (card 16).
  • Rorschach is an association technique; TAT is construction; sentence completion is completion.
  • Exner developed the Comprehensive scoring system, not the test itself.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q17: Which personality assessment tool uses inkblots to assess thought processes? → Rorschach Test, the only inkblot test among the options; TAT uses pictures, MMPI and MBTI are objective inventories.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Basics-level students can name the Rorschach and TAT. The step up is precision: (1) separating the test author from the scoring-system author (Rorschach made the blots in 1921; Exner built the Comprehensive System; Meyer and colleagues built R-PAS); (2) the numbers (Rorschach 10 cards, TAT 31 including blank card 16, Holtzman 45 blots in two forms); (3) Lindzey's (1959) response-type classes: association (Rorschach, word association), construction (TAT, CAT), completion (sentence completion, Rosenzweig), choice or ordering (Szondi), expression (drawings). Also, Lawrence Frank (1939) coined "projective methods"; he did not create any of the tests. Finally, the Rorschach targets perception and thought organisation, while the TAT targets needs and motives; IHBAS Q17 hinged on exactly that line, and the MMPI and MBTI distractors are objective inventories.

Lawrence Frank1939Coined the term "projective methods"

  1. Who wrote the test, and who wrote the most-used scoring system, for the inkblots? (Hermann Rorschach 1921; John Exner, Comprehensive System)
  2. Which projective has a blank card, and which number? (TAT, card 16)
  3. Is the TAT association or construction in Lindzey's scheme? (construction)
  4. Which inkblot test has 45 blots and two parallel forms? (Holtzman Inkblot Technique)
  5. Animal-figure version of the TAT for children: name and author? (CAT, Leopold Bellak)
39Rater errors: halo, leniency, central tendency
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q54, Q15

From scratch

Whenever one person rates another (interviews, performance appraisals, rating scales, clinical impressions), the ratings are contaminated by systematic biases in the rater, not just random noise. These rater errors lower the reliability and validity of rating scales and are a standard topic in psychological assessment (Anastasi, Guilford) and in industrial-organisational psychology.

Halo effect (halo error). Named by Edward Thorndike (1920), who found that military officers' ratings of soldiers on separate traits (physique, intelligence, leadership, character) were far too highly correlated. Definition: the tendency to let one salient trait or a general overall impression colour the ratings of all other, unrelated traits. If a candidate is attractive and articulate, the interviewer also rates them as intelligent, honest and hard-working. The negative version is the horn (reverse halo) effect: one bad trait drags all ratings down. In social psychology the halo effect is a person-perception bias (Asch's central traits such as warm/cold work similarly; Dion, Berscheid and Walster 1972 showed "what is beautiful is good").

Leniency (generosity) error: rating everyone too favourably, crowding scores at the high end. Severity (strictness) error: rating everyone too harshly. Central tendency error: avoiding extremes and putting everyone in the middle ("average" on every item), often because the rater does not know the ratee well or wants to avoid justifying extreme ratings. All three restrict the range of ratings, so they reduce discrimination between people.

Other errors. Contrast error: rating someone relative to the person rated just before (an average candidate looks excellent after a weak one), or rating others as opposite to oneself. Similar-to-me error: rating higher those who resemble the rater in background or attitudes. Logical error (Guilford): rating traits similarly because the rater believes they logically go together (e.g., assuming the intelligent are also responsible). Proximity error: items close together on the form get similar ratings. Primacy (first-impression) and recency errors: early information or the most recent events dominate the judgment.

Remedies. Behaviourally anchored rating scales (BARS), forced-choice and forced-distribution methods (counter leniency and central tendency), rating all ratees on one trait at a time (counters halo), multiple independent raters, and rater training. Example: a ward supervisor who rates a nurse highly on punctuality, empathy and documentation simply because she is cheerful is committing halo error; one who gives every nurse 3 out of 5 is committing central tendency error.

Key distinctions

ErrorWhat happensCue word in stem
HaloOne trait or global impression spreads to all others"single attribute", "one positive trait"
HornOne negative trait spreads to all"one bad quality"
LeniencyEveryone rated high"too generous"
SeverityEveryone rated low"too harsh"
Central tendencyEveryone rated in the middle"avoids extremes"
ContrastJudged against the previous ratee or against oneself"compared with the previous candidate"
Similarity (similar-to-me)Favouring people like the rater"same background as rater"
Primacy / first impressionEarly information dominates later judgments"initial impressions"
Logical errorTraits assumed to co-occur are rated alike"believes traits go together"

MCQ traps

  • "Effect of initial impressions on subsequent judgments" describes the primacy effect, not halo. Halo is about one trait spilling over to other traits, not about order.
  • "Judging others based on group membership" is stereotyping, not halo.
  • Central tendency error is about clustering ratings in the middle, it has nothing to do with judging on one attribute, though the word "central" may tempt you.
  • Contrast error involves comparison with someone else; similarity error involves resemblance to the rater; neither is a single-trait spillover.
  • Do not confuse logical error (rater's belief that traits go together) with halo (an overall impression spreads), although both inflate inter-trait correlations.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q54: The tendency of a rater or interviewer to judge an individual on the basis of one single attribute → Halo error, because one salient attribute colours the whole evaluation; central tendency, contrast and similarity errors involve different mechanisms.
  • Q15: What is the halo effect? → The influence of a positive trait on the perception of other traits, Thorndike's definition; "initial impressions on subsequent judgments" is primacy and "group membership" is stereotyping.
40Indian tools (ISAA) and RPwD Act 2016 ★
Assessment · IHBAS 2024 Q85, Q96

From scratch

Indian entrance papers keep testing two linked things: the Indian assessment tools a clinical psychologist uses to certify disability, and the laws that make that certificate matter. Learn them as one chain: law (RPwD Act 2016) → guidelines (2018, which tool for which disability) → tool (ISAA, IQ tests, SLD batteries, IDEAS) → percentage of disability → benefits (if 40% or more).

1. The law: Rights of Persons with Disabilities (RPwD) Act, 2016. Passed by Parliament in December 2016 and in force from April 2017, it replaced the Persons with Disabilities (Equal Opportunities, Protection of Rights and Full Participation) Act, 1995 (PwD Act). The trigger was India's ratification of the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD, 2006), which shifted the idea of disability from a medical, charity model to a rights-based, social model: disability arises when an impairment meets barriers in society.

FeaturePwD Act 1995RPwD Act 2016
Number of disabilities721 (Central Government can add more)
Government job reservation3%4%
Seats in government and aided higher education3%at least 5% (plus 5 years upper age relaxation)
Free educationUp to 18 years6 to 18 years for children with benchmark disabilities
ModelWelfare / medicalRights-based, UNCRPD aligned
OffencesFew penaltiesSpecial courts in each district; penalties for discrimination and atrocities

The 7 disabilities of 1995: blindness, low vision, leprosy-cured, hearing impairment, locomotor disability, mental retardation, mental illness. Hook: "BLL HLMM".

The 21 of 2016, grouped the way the Act groups them:

  • Physical: locomotor disability (including leprosy cured, cerebral palsy, dwarfism, muscular dystrophy, acid attack victims); visual impairment (blindness, low vision); hearing impairment (deaf and hard of hearing); speech and language disability.
  • Intellectual disability group: intellectual disability, specific learning disabilities (dyslexia, dysgraphia, dyscalculia, dyspraxia, developmental aphasia), autism spectrum disorder.
  • Mental behaviour: mental illness (explicitly excluding retardation).
  • Chronic neurological conditions: multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's disease.
  • Blood disorders: haemophilia, thalassaemia, sickle cell disease.
  • Multiple disabilities, including deaf-blindness.

New in 2016, and therefore favourite MCQ material: autism, SLD, acid attack victims, Parkinson's, thalassaemia, haemophilia, sickle cell, dwarfism, muscular dystrophy, multiple sclerosis, speech and language disability. "Mental retardation" became "intellectual disability".

2. Key definitions and numbers. A person with benchmark disability (Section 2(r)) has not less than 40% of a specified disability, as certified by a certifying authority. 40% is the gate to reservation and most schemes. A person with high support needs is one certified as needing intensive support. The 4% job reservation (Section 34) is split as 1% for blindness and low vision, 1% for deaf and hard of hearing, 1% for locomotor disability (including CP, leprosy cured, dwarfism, acid attack, muscular dystrophy), and 1% shared by autism, intellectual disability, SLD and mental illness plus multiple disabilities. That last 1% is why psychologists' certificates now affect jobs. Higher education gets at least 5% seats (Section 32); free education runs from 6 to 18 (Section 31). The Act also limits guardianship (supported decision-making), creates a Chief Commissioner and State Commissioners for Persons with Disabilities, and a Unique Disability ID (UDID) card system runs alongside it.

3. The guidelines and Q96 (the one you missed). The Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment notified guidelines for assessing the extent of specified disabilities in January 2018. These tell the certifying board which tool and which age rules apply. For specific learning disability, the minimum age for assessment and certification is 8 years. Why 8? SLD means persistent failure to acquire reading, writing or arithmetic despite adequate schooling and average intelligence. A 5 or 6 year old who cannot read may simply be a slow starter; you need roughly two to three years of formal instruction before the lag becomes a disorder rather than normal variation. Below 8 a child can be flagged as "at risk" and given remedial teaching, but not certified. The distractors in Q96 each have a tempting story:

OptionWhy it temptsWhy it is wrong
7 yearsDSM-IV ADHD age of onset, and an early school ageToo little schooling to rule out normal lag; guideline says 8
10 yearsFeels like "mid primary"Not the guideline figure; would delay help
14 yearsUpper age of free schooling under the RTE Act 2009 (6 to 14)That is the RTE education age, not SLD certification (RPwD free education goes to 18)
8 yearsEnough schooling to separate disorder from a slow startCorrect: SLD certification minimum under the 2018 RPwD guidelines

The SLD assessment combines an IQ test (to show average intelligence), a curriculum-based or standardised achievement test, and exclusion of sensory, emotional and teaching causes. Indian SLD tools: the NIMHANS Index for Specific Learning Disabilities (Kapur, Hirisave and colleagues), the Diagnostic Test of Learning Disability (Swarup and Mehta), and the Grade Level Assessment Device (GLAD, Narayan, NIMH).

4. Autism and the ISAA (Q85). The Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism (ISAA) was developed by the National Institute for the Mentally Handicapped (NIMH, Secunderabad, now NIEPID, National Institute for the Empowerment of Persons with Intellectual Disabilities) and published around 2009. It is the tool prescribed for diagnosing autism, grading severity and fixing the percentage of disability for certification. Structure: 40 items, each rated 1 to 5 (rarely, up to 20% of the time, through always, 81 to 100%), across six domains:

  1. Social relationship and reciprocity
  2. Emotional responsiveness
  3. Speech, language and communication
  4. Behaviour patterns
  5. Sensory aspects
  6. Cognitive component
ISAA total (40 to 200)InterpretationDisability
Below 70No autismNot certified
70 to 106Mild autism40 to 60%
107 to 153Moderate autism61 to 80%
Above 153Severe autismAbove 80%

Note that mild autism already starts at 40%, i.e. benchmark level. ISAA drew partly on CARS items, which is why CARS (Childhood Autism Rating Scale, Schopler, Reichler and Renner, 1980, 15 items, American) is the classic distractor and, in IHBAS 2024, the official key.

5. Other Indian and NIMH tools. Intellectual disability is certified with IQ and social maturity tests: VSMS (Vineland Social Maturity Scale, Edgar Doll's scale, Indian adaptation by A. J. Malin), giving social age and social quotient; MISIC (Malin's Intelligence Scale for Indian Children, Indian WISC); Binet-Kamat Test (V. V. Kamat's adaptation of the Stanford-Binet); Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests (C. M. Bhatia); Seguin Form Board; Developmental Screening Test (Bharat Raj) and DASII (Developmental Assessment Scales for Indian Infants, Phatak, Bayley-based) for young children. From NIMH: BASIC-MR (Behavioural Assessment Scales for Indian Children with Mental Retardation, Peshawaria and Venkatesan) and FACP (Functional Assessment Checklist for Programming). For mental illness the disability tool is IDEAS (Indian Disability Evaluation and Assessment Scale, Indian Psychiatric Society task force). Other Indian names: PGI Memory Scale (Pershad and Wig), NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery.

6. The confusable laws.

  • RCI Act 1992: set up the Rehabilitation Council of India (statutory body from 1993), which regulates training of rehabilitation professionals, including clinical psychologists, and keeps the Central Rehabilitation Register. Practising without RCI registration is an offence. This is why your MA/M.Phil must be RCI-recognised.
  • National Trust Act 1999: welfare of persons with autism, cerebral palsy, mental retardation and multiple disabilities; legal guardianship through Local Level Committees.
  • Mental Healthcare Act (MHCA) 2017: replaced the Mental Health Act 1987; in force from 2018. Rights-based care for mental illness: right to access mental healthcare, advance directives, nominated representative, Mental Health Review Boards, decriminalised attempted suicide (presumed severe stress, Section 115), and restricts ECT (no unmodified ECT). It is about treatment, not disability certification or reservation.
  • RTE Act 2009: free and compulsory education for ages 6 to 14.

Who said what

Memory hooks. 7 → 21: the Act tripled the list (7 x 3 = 21). Four-Five-Forty: 4% jobs, 5% college seats, 40% benchmark (say "4, 5, 40" like a cricket score). Eight to Educate: SLD certification at 8, after enough schooling to tell a disorder from a slow start. ISAA = Indian; CARS = Carolina (Schopler's TEACCH home), American.

MCQ traps

  • CARS as "the Indian tool" is wrong on the facts: CARS is American (Schopler); the Indian tool is ISAA, even though IHBAS 2024's key chose CARS.
  • CBCL and CGAS are real child scales but neither is autism-specific nor Indian.
  • 14 years for SLD borrows the RTE Act age ceiling; 7 borrows the old DSM-IV ADHD onset age. Neither is the guideline's 8.
  • 7 disabilities belongs to the 1995 Act; the 2016 Act has 21.
  • 3% reservation is the old 1995 figure; RPwD raised jobs to 4% and higher education to 5%.
  • 50% is not the benchmark; benchmark disability is 40%.
  • MHCA 2017 as the law listing 21 disabilities is wrong; MHCA governs mental health treatment, RPwD lists disabilities.
  • RCI Act as the disability-rights law is wrong; RCI regulates professionals and training.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q85 (key disputed): Indian tool to assess severity and disability of children with autism → official key Childhood Autism Rating Scale; textbook-correct is ISAA. CARS is American, CGAS measures global functioning, CBCL is Achenbach's broad checklist. In other papers, answer ISAA whenever "Indian" and "disability" appear in the stem.
  • Q96 (you missed this): minimum age for assessment of SLD as per RPwD Act 2016 → 8 years. 7 is too early to separate disorder from slow start, 14 is the RTE age ceiling, 10 is simply not the guideline figure.

Self-test

  1. How many disabilities in the RPwD Act 2016, and in the PwD Act 1995? (21; 7)
  2. What percentage makes a "benchmark disability"? (40% or more)
  3. Job reservation and higher-education seats under RPwD? (4% jobs; at least 5% seats)
  4. Minimum age for SLD certification under the 2018 guidelines? (8 years)
  5. Who developed ISAA, and how many items and domains? (NIMH Secunderabad, now NIEPID; 40 items, 6 domains)
  6. ISAA score range for moderate autism? (107 to 153, 61 to 80% disability)
  7. Who adapted the Vineland Social Maturity Scale and WISC for India? (A. J. Malin: VSMS and MISIC)
  8. Which Act regulates clinical psychologists' registration, and which decriminalised attempted suicide? (RCI Act 1992; Mental Healthcare Act 2017)
41Graphs and frequency distributions ★
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q21, Q53, Q38, Q37

From scratch

Descriptive statistics begins by organising raw data. Raw scores are the original, unprocessed measurements. Listing each value with how often it occurs gives a frequency distribution. When there are many distinct values, scores are collapsed into class intervals (e.g., 40 to 44, 45 to 49) to form a grouped frequency distribution. Each interval has stated limits (40 and 44), exact or real limits (39.5 and 44.5) and a midpoint (42). Common rules (King and Minium, Garrett): use roughly 10 to 20 intervals of equal width, and choose convenient widths such as 2, 3, 5 or 10. Because the researcher chooses the width and starting point, a set of raw scores does not result in a unique set of grouped scores: the same data can be grouped many ways, and grouping loses information (you no longer know exact scores within an interval).

Kinds of frequency. Frequency (f) is the count in a category. Cumulative frequency (cf) is the running total up to the upper limit of each interval, used for percentiles and medians. Relative frequency is f divided by N, expressed as a proportion (or multiplied by 100 as a percentage). Relative frequencies are especially useful when comparing distributions with different numbers of cases: if Class A has 200 students and Class B has 50, raw counts are not comparable, but proportions put both on the same base. Cumulative relative frequency (cumulative percentage) combines both ideas.

Graphs. (1) Histogram: a series of adjacent rectangles, one per class interval, with height equal to frequency; bars touch because the variable is continuous (interval or ratio data). (2) Bar graph: separated bars for discrete or categorical (nominal, ordinal) data such as diagnosis type. (3) Frequency polygon: points plotted at interval midpoints joined by straight lines, closed at both ends; best for comparing two distributions on one graph. (4) Ogive: the cumulative frequency (or cumulative percentage) curve, S-shaped, plotted at upper exact limits; used to read percentiles and the median. (5) Pie chart: sectors showing parts of a whole for categorical data. (6) Stem-and-leaf display (Tukey 1977): keeps raw values while showing shape.

Scatterplot (scatter diagram). Used for bivariate correlational data: each person is one point, X score on the horizontal axis and Y score on the vertical. An upward-sloping cloud means positive correlation, downward means negative, a shapeless cloud means near zero; the tighter the cloud around a line, the stronger the relationship. Francis Galton used such plots in the 1880s to study regression, and Karl Pearson formalised the correlation coefficient r. Scatterplots also reveal curvilinearity (e.g., the inverted-U of the Yerkes-Dodson law) and outliers that r alone would hide.

Shapes. Normal (bell curve, symmetric, mean = median = mode), positively skewed (tail to the right, mean above median), negatively skewed (tail to the left), and kurtosis: leptokurtic (peaked), mesokurtic (normal), platykurtic (flat).

Key distinctions

Graph or termUseDo not confuse with
HistogramContinuous grouped data, touching rectanglesBar graph (gaps, categorical)
Frequency polygonMidpoints joined by lines; comparing distributionsOgive (cumulative)
OgiveCumulative frequency; percentiles, medianPolygon
ScatterplotTwo variables per person; correlationBell curve (one variable's shape)
Relative frequencyf/N; compares unequal-sized groupsCumulative frequency (running total, still raw counts)
Raw scoresUngrouped originals; can be grouped in many waysGrouped scores

MCQ traps

  • Cumulative frequencies sound like a comparison tool, but they stay on the raw count scale, so they do not solve unequal N. The answer for unequal N is relative frequencies.
  • "Frequency percentage" and "frequency proportion" are informal variants of relative frequency; when the textbook term is offered, pick relative frequencies.
  • Bar graph vs histogram: "rectangles for class intervals" means histogram; separated bars for categories means bar graph.
  • Chi-square appears as a distractor for "graph of correlational data"; it is a test statistic, not a graph. A bell curve shows the shape of one variable.
  • "Frequency polygon" is plotted at midpoints; the ogive at upper exact limits.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q21: A graphical representation of correlational data is called a → Scatterplot, since each point pairs one person's two scores.
  • Q53: Data shown as a series of rectangles, each representing scores in one class interval → Histogram, adjacent bars on a continuous scale.
  • Q38: ___ are particularly helpful when comparing frequency distributions in which the number of cases differs → Relative frequencies, because dividing by N puts both distributions on a common base.
  • Q37: A set of ___ scores does not result in a unique set of grouped scores → official key Raw; this item is listed as debated, but the key matches the textbook (King and Minium): raw data can be grouped many ways depending on interval width and starting point.
  • STAR: Q38 is the one you missed. You picked one of the near-synonyms or cumulative frequencies; the standard term is relative frequencies.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

At level 2 you can draw the common graphs. What still trips students: (1) mixing up which point each line graph is plotted at (frequency polygon at interval midpoints, ogive at upper exact limits); (2) choosing cumulative frequency when the stem says groups of unequal size, when the correct tool is relative frequency (f/N), the one you missed on Q38; (3) treating histogram and bar graph as interchangeable, when histogram bars touch because the variable is continuous and bar-graph bars are separated because the categories are discrete. Remember too that grouping raw scores is not unique: interval width and starting point are the researcher's choice. Also keep the scatterplot (two variables per person, for correlation) apart from the bell curve, which shows the shape of a single variable.

TheoristYearIdea
Francis Galton1880sScatter plots of paired scores to study regression
Karl Pearson1896Product-moment correlation r, the number a scatterplot pictures
Karl Pearson1890sCoined the word "histogram"
John Tukey1977Stem-and-leaf display and exploratory data analysis
Yerkes and Dodson1908Inverted-U law: a curvilinear relation a scatterplot shows but r misses
King and Minium (textbook)Grouping rules; raw scores do not give a unique set of grouped scores
Henry Garrett (textbook)Classic Indian-syllabus source for class intervals, midpoints, ogives
  1. Two classes, N = 200 and N = 50: which frequency makes their distributions comparable? (relative frequency (f divided by N))
  2. Ogive points are plotted at what? (upper exact (real) limits, using cumulative frequency)
  3. Interval 40 to 44: exact limits and midpoint? (39.5 and 44.5; midpoint 42)
