  4. Who invented the stem-and-leaf display? (John Tukey (1977))
  5. Tail to the right, mean above median: which skew? (positive skew)
42Research designs
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q24, Q29, Q60, Q81, Q86

From scratch

A research design is the plan that links a question to the data. The central divide is between experimental designs, which can establish cause and effect, and non-experimental designs (correlational, survey, observational, case study), which describe or predict but cannot prove causation. In a true experiment the researcher (1) manipulates an independent variable (IV), (2) measures its effect on a dependent variable (DV), and (3) controls other influences, chiefly through random assignment. Example: 60 patients with depression are randomly assigned to CBT or a waitlist; if the CBT group improves more on the BDI, the improvement can be attributed to CBT.

Randomisation vs random sampling. Random sampling (random selection) is how people are drawn from the population into the study, so every member has an equal chance of being included; it protects external validity (generalisation). Randomisation (random assignment) is how participants already in the study are allocated to treatment groups so each has an equal probability of any group; it equates groups on all pre-existing variables, known and unknown, and protects internal validity. R. A. Fisher (1935, The Design of Experiments) made randomisation the foundation of experimental design. Stratification divides the population into subgroups (strata) first, then samples within each.

Confounding variables. A confound is an extraneous variable that is related to both the IV and the DV, so its effect cannot be separated from the IV's. If the CBT group happened to be younger and the young recover faster, age confounds the result. Controls: random assignment, holding variables constant, matching, counterbalancing (for order effects in within-subjects designs), blinding (single-blind, double-blind against expectancy and demand characteristics), and statistical control (ANCOVA). A quasi-experiment (Campbell and Stanley 1963; Cook and Campbell 1979) has manipulation or comparison groups but no random assignment, so confounds remain plausible.

Developmental designs. To study change with age: a cross-sectional design tests different age groups at one time (20-, 40-, 60-year-olds in 2026). It is quick and cheap but confounds age with cohort: the groups differ in generation, schooling, nutrition and historical experience, so differences may be cohort differences, not ageing (this is why early cross-sectional studies exaggerated IQ decline). A longitudinal design follows the same people over time, directly showing intra-individual change and stability, but it is slow and costly, and suffers attrition (dropout), practice effects and time-of-measurement effects. K. Warner Schaie's Seattle Longitudinal Study used a cross-sequential (sequential) design, combining both, to separate age, cohort and time effects.

Other designs. Correlational: measures how variables co-vary (r), no manipulation, cannot rule out third variables or reverse causation. Case study: intensive study of one person (Freud's Anna O, H.M.), rich but low generalisability. Between-subjects (different people per condition) vs within-subjects / repeated measures (same people in all conditions, needs counterbalancing). Single-case experimental designs (ABA, ABAB, multiple baseline) are used in behaviour therapy.

Key distinctions

PairFirstSecond
Random sampling vs randomisationWho gets into the study; external validityWho gets which treatment; internal validity
Experimental vs correlationalManipulation plus random assignment; causationNo manipulation; association only
Confound vs extraneous variableVaries systematically with IV and affects DVAny outside variable, may just add noise
Cross-sectional vs longitudinalDifferent people, one time; cohort effectSame people, many times; attrition, practice
True vs quasi-experimentRandom assignmentNo random assignment

MCQ traps

  • Random sampling is the trap for any stem about assigning people to treatment groups; that is randomisation.
  • "A variable that only affects the DV" is just an extraneous influence; a confound must be linked to both IV and DV.
  • For the disadvantage of cross-sectional studies, attrition is the lure, but attrition is a longitudinal problem; the cross-sectional problem is cohort difference.
  • "Changes over time" means longitudinal, even though a cross-sectional study also compares ages.
  • Correlational studies can show strong relationships but not cause; only the experiment gets "cause and effect".

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q24: ___ ensures assignment of subjects to treatment groups so every member has equal probability of any assignment → Randomization, which allocates to groups, unlike random sampling which selects into the study.
  • Q29: What is a confounding variable? → A variable that influences both the independent and dependent variables, so its effect is tangled with the IV's.
  • Q60: Design best suited for cause and effect → Experimental design, because only manipulation plus control and random assignment rule out alternatives.
  • Q81: A disadvantage of cross-sectional studies is that results can be influenced by → Cohort difference, since the age groups also differ by generation.
  • Q86: Design best suited for examining changes over time → Longitudinal design, which follows the same people repeatedly.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

Basics-level students know experiments show cause and effect. The marks slip on: (1) random sampling (who enters the study, protects external validity) versus random assignment or randomisation (who gets which condition, protects internal validity); (2) the exact definition of a confound: it must be related to both the IV and the DV, not merely affect the DV; (3) which weakness belongs to which developmental design: cohort effects to cross-sectional, attrition and practice effects to longitudinal, with Schaie's cross-sequential design built to separate age, cohort and time of measurement. A quasi-experiment differs from a true experiment only by the missing random assignment. Finally, only the experiment licenses a causal claim; a strong correlation still leaves third variables and reverse causation open.

TheoristYearIdea
R. A. Fisher1935The Design of Experiments: randomisation as the basis of valid inference
Campbell and Stanley1963Internal vs external validity; threats to validity; quasi-experiments
Cook and Campbell1979Expanded quasi-experimental design and validity typology
K. Warner Schaie1956 onwardSeattle Longitudinal Study; cross-sequential design separates age and cohort
Martin Orne1962Demand characteristics: participants guess the hypothesis and act on it
Robert Rosenthal1960sExperimenter expectancy effect, controlled by double-blind procedures
Elton Mayo (Hawthorne studies)1920s to 1930sHawthorne effect: behaviour changes because people know they are observed
  1. Allocating participants already in a study to CBT or waitlist by coin toss is called? (randomisation (random assignment), not random sampling)
  2. Random sampling protects which validity? (external validity (generalisation))
  3. Main weakness of a cross-sectional ageing study? (cohort differences (generation effects))
  4. Who combined cross-sectional and longitudinal designs in the Seattle Longitudinal Study? (K. Warner Schaie)
  5. Who wrote The Design of Experiments (1935)? (R. A. Fisher)
43Chi-square df and p-value
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q84, Q90

From scratch

The chi-square test (χ²), introduced by Karl Pearson (1900), is a non-parametric test for frequency (count) data in nominal or ordinal categories. It compares observed frequencies (O) with the expected frequencies (E) predicted by the null hypothesis: χ² = Σ (O minus E)² / E. If observed counts are close to expected, χ² is small; large discrepancies give a large χ² and a small p-value.

Two main uses. (1) Goodness of fit (one variable): do observed counts fit a hypothesised distribution? Example: are patients equally spread across four OPD days? Here df = k minus 1, where k is the number of categories (so 4 days gives df = 3). (2) Test of independence (two variables in a contingency table): is gender related to diagnosis? Expected frequency for each cell = (row total × column total) / N, and df = (number of rows minus 1) × (number of columns minus 1). A 2 × 2 table has df = 1; a 3 × 4 table has df = 2 × 3 = 6. The logic: once the row and column totals are fixed, only (r minus 1)(c minus 1) cells are free to vary. Assumptions: independent observations (each person counted once), and expected frequency of at least 5 in each cell (in practice no more than 20% of cells below 5). For a 2 × 2 table with small expected counts, use Yates' correction for continuity or Fisher's exact test. The chi-square distribution is positively skewed and its mean equals its df. Effect size for association: phi (2 × 2) or Cramér's V.

The p-value. In null hypothesis significance testing (Fisher; Neyman and Pearson), you assume H0 (no effect, no association). The p-value is the probability of obtaining data as extreme as, or more extreme than, those observed, assuming the null hypothesis is true: P(data | H0). If p is less than the pre-set alpha (usually 0.05), the result is "statistically significant" and H0 is rejected. Example: χ²(1) = 6.2, p = .013 means that if gender and diagnosis were truly unrelated, a discrepancy this large would arise only about 1.3% of the time by sampling error.

What p is not. It is not the probability that H0 is true, not the probability that the result was "due to chance", not 1 minus the probability the alternative hypothesis is true, and not the size or importance of the effect. A tiny effect in a huge sample can have p below .001. That is why the APA (and the ASA statement, 2016) asks for effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside p.

Related terms. Alpha = probability of a Type I error (rejecting a true H0, false positive). Beta = probability of a Type II error (failing to reject a false H0, false negative). Power = 1 minus beta, the probability of detecting a real effect; it rises with larger N, larger effect size and higher alpha (Cohen recommended 0.80).

Key distinctions

ConceptFormula or meaning
df, chi-square independence(rows minus 1)(columns minus 1)
df, chi-square goodness of fitk minus 1
df, one-sample or paired tN minus 1
df, independent tN1 + N2 minus 2
df, Pearson rN minus 2
p-valueP(data this extreme | H0 true)
AlphaCut-off chosen in advance; Type I error rate
Power1 minus beta
Effect sizeMagnitude (d, r, phi, Cramér's V), independent of N

MCQ traps

  • (N minus 1) is the df for goodness-of-fit with N categories or for a one-sample t; it is not the contingency-table formula.
  • (N minus 2) is the df for correlation or independent-samples t, a different test.
  • (columns minus 2)(rows minus 2) is a made-up formula; always subtract 1.
  • "The probability that the null hypothesis is true" is the most popular wrong p-value definition; p is about the data given H0, not H0 given the data.
  • p is not the effect size and not power; a significant result can be trivial in size.
  • Chi-square is non-parametric and uses frequencies, never means.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q84: Correct formula for degrees of freedom in a chi-square test for independence → (N columns minus 1)(N rows minus 1), because with margins fixed only that many cells are free to vary.
  • Q90: What is the p-value? → The probability of obtaining the observed data if the null hypothesis is true; it is a conditional probability of the data, not of the hypothesis.
44Meta-analysis
Biostats / Research · IHBAS 2024 Q87

From scratch

Meta-analysis is a quantitative method for statistically combining the results of many independent studies on the same question to produce an overall estimate of an effect. The term was coined by Gene V. Glass (1976), who called it "the analysis of analyses". Its landmark use in clinical psychology was Smith and Glass (1977), which pooled about 375 psychotherapy outcome studies and found that the average treated client was better off than about 75% of untreated controls (mean effect size around 0.68); Smith, Glass and Miller (1980) extended this to 475 studies (effect size about 0.85). This was a direct empirical reply to Hans Eysenck's (1952) claim that psychotherapy was no better than spontaneous remission; Eysenck in turn dismissed meta-analysis as "mega-silliness" and "garbage in, garbage out".

How it works, step by step. (1) Define a precise question (e.g., does CBT reduce depression compared with waitlist?). (2) Conduct a systematic literature search with explicit inclusion and exclusion criteria (reported using PRISMA guidelines). (3) Extract a common effect size from each study: Cohen's d or Hedges' g for mean differences (0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large), r for correlations, odds ratio or risk ratio for binary outcomes. (4) Pool them as a weighted average, giving more weight to larger, more precise studies (inverse-variance weighting). (5) Assess heterogeneity (Cochran's Q, the I² statistic) and choose a fixed-effect model (one true effect, differences are sampling error) or a random-effects model (true effects vary across studies). (6) Run moderator analyses (does the effect differ by age, severity, therapist training?). (7) Check publication bias.

Displays and biases. A forest plot shows each study's effect and confidence interval plus the pooled diamond. A funnel plot plots effect size against study precision; asymmetry suggests publication bias, because small non-significant studies tend to stay unpublished. Robert Rosenthal (1979) named this the file-drawer problem and proposed the fail-safe N (how many null studies would be needed to cancel the effect).

Systematic review vs meta-analysis. A systematic review is the structured, reproducible search and appraisal of all relevant studies; a meta-analysis is the statistical pooling step that may (or may not) follow. A narrative review summarises studies in words, without systematic search or statistics. Meta-analyses of randomised controlled trials sit at the top of the evidence hierarchy (Cochrane Collaboration), which is why they guide evidence-based practice and clinical guidelines (e.g., NICE). Criticisms: "apples and oranges" (mixing studies that measure different things), garbage in garbage out (poor-quality studies), and publication bias.

Key point: meta-analysis uses secondary data; it never collects new primary data and is not one large new experiment. Example: a 2026 meta-analysis of 40 RCTs on mindfulness for anxiety reporting pooled g = 0.45, I² = 60% (moderate heterogeneity) and a funnel plot hinting at missing small negative studies.

Key distinctions

MethodWhat it doesData
Meta-analysisStatistically pools effect sizes across studiesSecondary, quantitative
Systematic reviewSystematic search and appraisal, may be narrativeSecondary
Narrative reviewQualitative summary, no systematic searchSecondary
Multi-centre RCT / mega-trialOne large new experiment across sitesPrimary
Case studyIn-depth study of one individualPrimary, idiographic
Fixed vs random effectsOne true effect vs a distribution of true effectsModel choice
Forest vs funnel plotPooled effect display vs publication-bias checkGraphs

MCQ traps

  • "To conduct a single large experiment" describes a mega-trial or multi-centre RCT, not meta-analysis, which combines already-completed studies.
  • "To collect primary data" is what original studies do; meta-analysis uses secondary data.
  • The funnel plot detects publication bias; the forest plot shows pooled results. Exams swap them.
  • Glass coined the term (1976); Eysenck was the critic, not the founder; Rosenthal named the file-drawer problem.
  • A systematic review is not automatically a meta-analysis; only the statistical pooling makes it one.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q87: Main purpose of meta-analysis in clinical psychology → official key To conduct a single large experiment; this key is debated and is wrong by textbook standards. The textbook-correct answer is To summarize findings from multiple studies, because meta-analysis statistically pools effect sizes from existing studies. Learn the concept; note the paper's key.

Tighten-up (you rated this 2)

At level 2 you know meta-analysis pools many studies. Three things still trip students: (1) the IHBAS Q87 key. The official key says "to conduct a single large experiment", which actually describes a mega-trial or multi-centre RCT. By every textbook, the purpose of meta-analysis is to summarize (statistically combine) findings from multiple studies. Know the correct concept, and note that the key is disputed. (2) The names: Glass (1976) coined the term, Smith and Glass (1977) applied it to psychotherapy, Eysenck was the critic, and Rosenthal named the file-drawer problem. (3) Swapping the plots: the forest plot shows the pooled effect; the funnel plot checks for publication bias.

TheoristYearIdea
Gene V. Glass1976Coined "meta-analysis", the analysis of analyses
Mary Lee Smith and Gene Glass1977First big psychotherapy meta-analysis: about 375 studies, treated client better off than about 75% of controls
Smith, Glass and Miller1980Extended to 475 studies, The Benefits of Psychotherapy
Hans Eysenck1952Claimed therapy was no better than spontaneous remission; later called meta-analysis "mega-silliness"
Robert Rosenthal1979File-drawer problem and fail-safe N (publication bias)
Jacob CohenCohen's d effect size: 0.2 small, 0.5 medium, 0.8 large
Cochrane Collaboration1993Systematic reviews and meta-analyses of RCTs at the top of the evidence hierarchy
  1. Textbook purpose of meta-analysis (Q87)? (to summarize findings from multiple studies; the official key "single large experiment" is disputed)
  2. Who coined the term meta-analysis, and when? (Gene V. Glass, 1976)
  3. Whose 1952 claim did Smith and Glass (1977) answer? (Hans Eysenck's, that psychotherapy did no better than spontaneous remission)
  4. Which plot checks for publication bias? (funnel plot (the forest plot shows the pooled effect))
  5. Primary or secondary data in a meta-analysis? (secondary: it pools already-completed studies)
45Schools: Wundt + Titchener (structuralism), James (functionalism), Watson (behaviourism), G. Stanley Hall
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q11, Q73, Q92, Q7 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 1 gave the headline sequence. Here are the people, the books and the details that MCQs pull out as options.

Wilhelm Wundt (1832 to 1920) wrote Principles of Physiological Psychology (1873 to 1874), the first textbook of psychology as a laboratory science, and opened the Leipzig laboratory in 1879. He also founded the first psychology journal, Philosophische Studien (1881). His own name for his system was voluntarism, because he stressed the will and apperception (actively focusing attention so that elements are combined by creative synthesis into something new). His tridimensional theory of feeling described every feeling on three axes: pleasant to unpleasant, tension to relaxation, excitement to calm. Wundt believed the laboratory could study only simple processes; higher mental processes (language, myth, custom) had to be studied historically and culturally, which he did in the ten-volume Volkerpsychologie (folk or cultural psychology, 1900 to 1920). So Wundt was broader than the "structuralist" label textbooks give him.

Edward Bradford Titchener (1867 to 1927), an Englishman trained by Wundt, worked at Cornell. He named his system structuralism in his 1898 paper "The postulates of a structural psychology", to contrast it with functionalism. He narrowed Wundt: three elements (sensations, images, affections), with affection having only one dimension (pleasant to unpleasant). His Experimental Psychology manuals (1901 to 1905) trained a generation of American laboratory workers. Structuralism ended with his death in 1927.

William James (1842 to 1910), at Harvard, wrote The Principles of Psychology (1890) and the shorter Psychology: Briefer Course (1892, nicknamed "Jimmy" by students, versus "the James" for the big book). Key ideas: stream of consciousness, habit as the "flywheel of society", the self (material, social, spiritual and pure ego), the James-Lange theory of emotion (we feel afraid because we tremble), and pragmatism (an idea is true if it works). James is the inspiration of functionalism, though he never founded a formal school; the organised school was at Chicago (Dewey, Angell, Carr) and Columbia (Cattell, Woodworth, Thorndike).

John B. Watson (1878 to 1958), trained at Chicago, launched behaviourism in his 1913 paper "Psychology as the behaviorist views it" (the "behaviorist manifesto"), at Johns Hopkins. Goals: prediction and control of behaviour; method: observation of stimulus and response; no introspection. He treated thinking as subvocal speech and emotions as conditioned responses (Little Albert, with Rosalie Rayner, 1920). His famous "give me a dozen healthy infants" claim appeared in Behaviorism (1924/1925). After leaving Johns Hopkins in 1920 he worked in advertising. Watson's version is methodological behaviourism; Skinner's radical behaviourism later counted private events (thoughts) as behaviour too.

G. Stanley Hall (1844 to 1924) is the "firsts" man of American psychology: the first American PhD in psychology (Harvard, 1878, under James), studied with Wundt, opened one of the first American psychology labs at Johns Hopkins (1883), founded the American Journal of Psychology (1887), became the first president of the APA (1892), and as president of Clark University invited Freud and Jung to lecture in 1909. His book Adolescence (1904) described the teenage years as "storm and stress", and he applied recapitulation theory (each child relives the stages of human evolution). He founded the child-study movement. He belongs loosely with the functionalist, developmental tradition, not to any one school.

Key distinctions

PersonSchool / labelSignature fact
WundtVoluntarism (textbooks: structuralism's father)Leipzig 1879, apperception, Volkerpsychologie
TitchenerStructuralism (named it, 1898)Cornell, 3 elements, stimulus error
JamesFunctionalism (inspirer)Principles 1890, stream of consciousness, James-Lange
Dewey, Angell, CarrFunctionalism (Chicago school)Reflex arc paper (Dewey, 1896)
WatsonMethodological behaviourism1913 manifesto, Little Albert
SkinnerRadical behaviourismOperant conditioning, Skinner box
G. Stanley HallNo single school; developmental pioneerFirst US PhD, APA president 1892, Adolescence 1904

MCQ traps

  • Titchener, not Wundt, coined "structuralism". If the stem asks who named or systematised it, pick Titchener.
  • Hall is a "firsts" distractor (APA, journal, PhD). He is not the founder of functionalism or behaviourism.
  • Watson vs Skinner: Watson = founder, methodological, 1913; Skinner = radical behaviourism, operant. Q7 tests the Skinner label.
  • "Voluntarism" means Wundt, not William James, even though James wrote much about the will.
  • Stream of consciousness is James. Wundt and Titchener are the opposite view (elements), so they are the usual wrong options.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q11: Functionalism was most closely associated with → William James; Titchener (structuralism), Watson (behaviourism) and G. Stanley Hall (first APA president, adolescence) were the three distractors from this topic.
  • Q73: Who coined "stream of consciousness" → William James; Wundt sat as a distractor because his introspective, element-based approach is the view James was arguing against.
  • Q92: Theory emphasising internal mental processes → Cognitive psychology; behaviourism (Watson) was the distractor because it deliberately excludes mental processes.
  • Q7: Match theorists to theories → Skinner = radical behaviourism (Dollard and Miller = early social learning, Bandura and Walters = later social learning, Maslow = self-actualisation).
46Nature vs nurture: Plato, Descartes, Kant vs Locke
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q27 · added from the options

From scratch

The nature-nurture question asks how much of the mind is given at birth (nature, heredity, innate structure) and how much is built by experience (nurture, environment, learning). Psychology inherited it from philosophy, where it was the battle between nativism/rationalism and empiricism.

Plato (about 427 to 347 BCE) was the first great nativist. In the dialogue Meno, Socrates questions an uneducated slave boy, who, guided only by questions, works out a geometry proof. Plato concluded that knowledge is innate: the soul knew the eternal Forms before birth, and learning is really recollection (anamnesis). The senses give only imperfect copies of the Forms. His student Aristotle moved toward empiricism, comparing the mind to a wax tablet on which experience writes, and gave the first laws of association (contiguity, similarity, contrast).

Rene Descartes (1596 to 1650), the father of modern rationalism, argued in the Meditations (1641) that some ideas (God, the self, infinity, the axioms of geometry) cannot come from the senses and must be innate ideas. He is also the source of mind-body dualism (an immaterial thinking mind interacting with a mechanical body, he suggested through the pineal gland) and of the mechanical reflex idea that later fed physiology.

John Locke (1632 to 1704), in An Essay Concerning Human Understanding (1690), attacked innate ideas directly. The mind at birth is "white paper", the tabula rasa. Ideas come from sensation and reflection; simple ideas combine into complex ones. He separated primary qualities (in the object: size, shape, motion) from secondary qualities (in the perceiver: colour, taste). Locke started British empiricism (Berkeley, Hume, Hartley, the Mills), which became associationism and then learning theory and behaviourism. Leibniz replied that the mind is more like veined marble: experience carves it, but the grain is already there.

Immanuel Kant (1724 to 1804), in the Critique of Pure Reason (1781), offered a synthesis. Knowledge begins with experience, but the mind brings a priori forms (space and time) and categories (causality, unity, substance) that organise raw sensation. In his phrase, "thoughts without content are empty, intuitions without concepts are blind." Kant is counted on the nature side in MCQs because the organising structure is innate. His view inspired Gestalt psychology and, later, Piaget's idea of active mental structures.

In psychology, Francis Galton made the phrase "nature and nurture" popular (English Men of Science, 1874), argued for heredity in Hereditary Genius (1869) and proposed the twin method. Watson's "dozen healthy infants" boast is the extreme nurture claim; Gesell's maturation theory leaned to nature. The modern answer is interaction: heritability estimates from twin and adoption studies, gene-environment correlation and interaction, the diathesis-stress model, and epigenetics (experience changes gene expression). A heritability of .50 describes variation in a population, not "half of one person's trait".

Key distinctions

ThinkerSideKey phrase
PlatoNature (nativist)Innate knowledge, learning is recollection
AristotleLeans nurtureWax tablet, laws of association
DescartesNature (rationalist)Innate ideas, dualism
LockeNurture (empiricist)Tabula rasa, sensation and reflection
LeibnizNatureVeined marble
KantNature (synthesis)A priori categories organise experience
GaltonNature (heredity)Coined "nature and nurture", twin method

MCQ traps

  • Tabula rasa = Locke, even though the image traces back to Aristotle's wax tablet. In options, always Locke.
  • Kant is not an empiricist just because he said knowledge begins with experience; the categories are innate, so he is the nativist/synthesis option.
  • Descartes appears for both dualism and innate ideas; neither makes him a nurture theorist.
  • Rousseau's "noble savage" is about natural goodness, not the blank slate; do not confuse with Locke.
  • Heritability is a population statistic, not a fixed share of an individual's trait.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q27: Which early philosopher held the mind is a "tabula rasa" at birth → John Locke. All three distractors came from this topic and all three are nativists: Plato (innate Forms, recollection), Descartes (innate ideas) and Kant (a priori categories). The question is solved by knowing which camp each belongs to.
47Sheldon's 3 somatotypes in full (endo / meso / ecto)
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q8 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 9 introduced Sheldon briefly. This is the full version, because MCQs quote his temperament adjectives word for word.

William H. Sheldon, an American physician and psychologist, built a constitutional theory of personality: body build and temperament are both expressions of an underlying biological constitution. In The Varieties of Human Physique (1940) he photographed about 4,000 male college students from the front, side and back under standard conditions. In The Varieties of Temperament (1942) he rated temperament, and in Varieties of Delinquent Youth (1949) and the Atlas of Men (1954) he extended the work.

The three components and why they have those names. Sheldon named each component after the embryonic germ layer whose tissues dominate that body:

  • Endomorphy (endoderm → digestive tract and viscera): soft, round, a large abdomen, weak bones and muscles, a "pear" shape.
  • Mesomorphy (mesoderm → bone, muscle, heart, blood vessels): hard, rectangular, heavy muscle and bone, broad shoulders, narrow waist.
  • Ectomorphy (ectoderm → skin, nervous system, sense organs): thin, long-limbed, fragile, flat-chested, the largest skin surface and brain relative to body mass.

Somatotyping. These are dimensions, not boxes. Each person is rated 1 to 7 on each component, giving a three-number somatotype in the order endo-meso-ecto: 7-1-1 extreme endomorph, 1-7-1 extreme mesomorph, 1-1-7 extreme ectomorph, 4-4-4 average. Sheldon also used secondary components such as dysplasia (different regions of the body having different somatotypes) and gynandromorphy (presence of features typical of the other sex). The modern anthropometric version is the Heath-Carter method (1967), still used in sports science.

The three temperaments. Sheldon began with 650 trait words, reduced them to 60 traits, 20 per component, and rated men on them in long interviews:

PhysiqueTemperamentSignature traits (Sheldon's terms)When troubled, needs...
EndomorphViscerotoniaRelaxed posture, love of physical comfort, love of eating, sociophilia, amiability, tolerance, complacency, slow reaction, deep sleepPeople
MesomorphSomatotoniaAssertive posture, love of physical adventure and exercise, energetic, love of dominance and risk, bold directness, competitive aggressiveness, psychological callousness, unrestrained (loud) voice, claustrophobiaAction
EctomorphCerebrotoniaRestraint, fast reactions, love of privacy, mental over-intensity, apprehensiveness, secretive feelings, self-consciousness, sociophobia, vocal restraint, sensitivity to pain, poor sleep, chronic fatigueSolitude

Sheldon reported correlations of about .80 between physique and temperament. The weakness: he rated both himself, so his knowledge of a man's body could bias his temperament ratings. Independent studies (for example Child, 1950) found correlations in the predicted direction but much weaker. In Varieties of Delinquent Youth he reported that delinquent boys were more mesomorphic, a finding partly echoed by the Gluecks (1950), but with social explanations equally likely (muscular boys get rewarded for toughness). He also linked extreme components to psychiatric patterns, a speculative claim not supported today.

Lineage. Sheldon refined Kretschmer's types (1921): pyknic ≈ endomorph, athletic ≈ mesomorph, asthenic/leptosomic ≈ ectomorph. Kretschmer linked them to psychosis (pyknic → manic-depressive, asthenic → schizophrenia); Sheldon linked them to normal temperament.

Key distinctions

  • Viscerotonia vs somatotonia: both may be "sociable", but viscerotonics seek comfort and affection, somatotonics seek dominance and action.
  • Cerebrotonia is not "intelligent"; it is restrained, inhibited, hypersensitive. The name refers to the nervous system, not IQ.
  • Sheldon vs Kretschmer: Sheldon = continuous 1 to 7 ratings of normal men; Kretschmer = categories linked to psychiatric illness.
  • Type vs trait: Sheldon is taught under type theories, but his ratings are actually dimensional.

MCQ traps

  • "Noisy, callous, fond of physical activity, assertive, adventurous" → mesomorph (somatotonia).
  • "Loves food, comfort, people, relaxed, even-tempered" → endomorph (viscerotonia).
  • "Restrained, private, sensitive, prefers solitude, fatigues easily" → ectomorph (cerebrotonia).
  • Do not pair mesomorph with cerebrotonia because "meso" sounds central; the germ-layer link is the reliable route.
  • The order in a somatotype code is always endo-meso-ecto.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q8: Which type is described as noisy, callous and fond of physical activity → Mesomorph (somatotonia: unrestrained voice, psychological callousness, love of physical adventure). Endomorph was the distractor because viscerotonics are relaxed comfort-lovers, and ectomorph because cerebrotonics are restrained and quiet. "None of the above" fails because the description is a textbook somatotonic.
48Culture and perception: Bruner & Goodman coin study, Segall + Herskovits (Müller-Lyer, carpentered world)
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q10, Q9 · added from the options

From scratch

Two research lines show that perception is not identical across people. The New Look showed that needs and values shape perception within one culture; the cross-cultural illusion studies showed that the visual environment a person grows up in shapes it across cultures.

Bruner and Goodman (1947), "Value and need as organizing factors in perception". Ten-year-old children in Boston adjusted the diameter of a circle of light to match coins (1, 5, 10, 25 and 50 cents) held in the palm. A control group matched grey cardboard discs of the same sizes. Coins were overestimated, discs were not; the more valuable the coin, the bigger the overestimation (except the 50-cent piece); and poor children overestimated more than rich children. Conclusion: economic need and social value enlarge perceived size. Follow-ups: Carter and Schooler (1949) found the effect mainly when judging from memory, not with the coin present; Ashley, Harper and Runyon (1951) hypnotised adults to feel "poor" or "rich" and found the "poor" state increased coin-size estimates. The study launched the New Look, which treats perception as top-down and motivated.

Segall, Campbell and Herskovits (paper 1963; book The Influence of Culture on Visual Perception, 1966) tested nearly 2,000 people across many societies (mostly African groups, plus the Philippines and Evanston, USA) on geometric illusions. Marshall Segall was the psychologist, Donald Campbell the methodologist, and Melville Herskovits the anthropologist known for cultural relativism. Their key finding: Western urban samples were more susceptible to the Müller-Lyer illusion (the line with outward fins looks longer) than many rural African samples.

The carpentered-world hypothesis explains this. People raised among rectangular buildings, straight edges and right-angled corners learn to read acute and obtuse angles in a 2D image as right angles receding in depth. The outward-fin figure resembles the inside corner of a room (far away), the inward-fin figure an outside corner of a building (near). Applying size constancy (Gregory's "misapplied size constancy" account), the "far" line is judged longer. People living in round huts with curved fields, like the Zulu, have less of this habit and show a smaller illusion. Related: Allport and Pettigrew (1957) found rural Zulu less likely to see the rotating trapezoidal window as a rectangle swinging back and forth.

The same study tested the horizontal-vertical illusion (the vertical looks longer) and proposed the foreshortening hypothesis: people from open plains, where vertical lines on the retina stand for long receding distances, are more susceptible than people living in dense forest. Other classics: Turnbull (1961), whose BaMbuti guide Kenge, raised in the forest, saw distant buffalo as insects (poor size constancy at long distance); Hudson (1960), showing that some unschooled African viewers did not read pictorial depth cues in drawings.

Key distinctions

StudyVariableFinding
Bruner and Goodman, 1947Need and value (poverty)Poor children overestimate coin size more
Segall, Campbell, Herskovits, 1963/1966Ecology (carpentered world)Western samples more susceptible to Müller-Lyer
Allport and Pettigrew, 1957Rectangular environmentRural Zulu less prone to trapezoidal window illusion
Turnbull, 1961Forest life, no long vistasSize constancy fails at distance
Hudson, 1960Schooling, picturesPictorial depth not read by some unschooled viewers

MCQ traps

  • "Economic deprivation affects perception" → Bruner and Goodman, not Segall or Herskovits, who studied culture and ecology, not poverty.
  • Herskovits was an anthropologist, co-author with Segall and Campbell; options often list "Segall" and "Herskovits" separately to tempt you.
  • Carpentered world predicts more illusion for city dwellers, not less.
  • Müller-Lyer vs horizontal-vertical: carpentered world explains the first, foreshortening (open vistas) the second.
  • The New Look is top-down processing; Gibson's direct perception is the bottom-up opposite.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q10: Who demonstrated that economic deprivation affects perception → Bruner and Goodman (coin study). Segall and "Hers Kovits" (Herskovits) were distractors from the cross-cultural illusion work, which concerns ecology and the carpentered world, not poverty. Broota and Ganguli, an Indian pairing, was the other distractor; it is not the classic deprivation study.
  • Q9: Perception as a complex of sensory and imaginal events → Titchener; Bruner appeared only as a distractor here, since his New Look stresses needs and values, not sensory plus imaginal elements.
49All the Gestalt laws: continuity, similarity, closure
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q52 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 4 listed the laws. This topic drills the three that are most often confused with each other and with proximity: continuity, similarity and closure, plus the rest of the full list, so any one of them can be recognised from a one-line description.

Where the laws come from. Max Wertheimer set out the principles of grouping in his 1923 paper "Laws of organization in perceptual forms". Koffka (Principles of Gestalt Psychology, 1935) and Kohler (Gestalt Psychology, 1929) spread them. All are expressions of one master principle, Pragnanz (the law of good form or simplicity): the perceptual field organises itself into the simplest, most regular, most stable whole the stimulus allows. The laws describe grouping: which parts of a scene the brain treats as belonging together, before any recognition of what the objects are.

Law of continuity (good continuation). Elements arranged along a smooth line or curve are grouped, and we prefer interpretations in which lines keep their direction. Two lines crossing to form an X are seen as two straight lines, not as two V shapes touching at the tip. A wire running behind a pole is seen as one wire. In camouflage, a continuous contour running across an animal's outline breaks the figure apart. The key word is direction or flow.

Law of similarity. Elements that look alike (in colour, shape, size, orientation, texture or brightness) are grouped. A grid of evenly spaced dots in which alternate rows are filled and empty is seen as rows, even though spacing is identical. Team jerseys in a football match let you see two groups. The key word is alike. When similarity and proximity conflict, the result depends on which difference is stronger.

Law of closure. We tend to complete incomplete figures, filling gaps to see a whole object. A circle drawn with a few breaks is seen as a circle; the WWF panda is seen although large parts of its outline are missing. The Kanizsa triangle (Gaetano Kanizsa, 1955) goes further: three "Pac-Man" discs produce a bright white triangle with illusory (subjective) contours that do not exist on the page. The key word is incomplete or gaps.

The other laws. Proximity: items close together in space or time form a group (the key word is near). Common fate: items moving in the same direction at the same speed group (a flock of birds). Symmetry: symmetrical regions are seen as figures and grouped. Past experience (familiarity): Wertheimer listed this too, so learned shapes such as letters are grouped. Modern additions: common region (Palmer, 1992: items enclosed in the same boundary group) and uniform connectedness (Palmer and Rock, 1994: physically connected items group most strongly). Figure-ground (Rubin, 1915) is the prior split into object and background, not a grouping law as such.

Why it matters clinically and practically. Closure and good continuation are tested in perceptual tasks such as the Gestalt Completion test (Street, 1931) and Hooper Visual Organization Test (pictures cut into pieces). The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (Lauretta Bender, 1938) uses nine of Wertheimer's 1923 figures; patients with organic brain damage often fail to reproduce the gestalt correctly.

Key distinctions

LawStem cueExample
ProximityClose together in space or timeDots in pairs seen as pairs
SimilaritySame colour, shape, sizeAlternating filled rows seen as rows
ContinuitySmooth line, same directionX seen as two crossing lines
ClosureFilling gaps, completingBroken circle, Kanizsa triangle
Common fateMoving togetherFlock of birds
PragnanzSimplest, most stable formOlympic rings as five circles
Common regionSame enclosed areaItems inside one box

MCQ traps

  • "Close together in space and time" is proximity, even though "continuity" contains the idea of time flowing; continuity is about direction.
  • Closure vs continuity: closure completes a shape that has gaps; continuity follows a path. A dashed line seen as one line is continuity (and proximity), not closure.
  • Similarity vs common fate: if the stem mentions moving together, it is common fate even if the items also look alike.
  • Pragnanz is the overarching law; if the options include it with "simplest organisation", pick it.
  • Perceptual constancy and depth cues are not Gestalt grouping laws.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q52: Items close together in space and time are perceived as an organised group → Proximity. The three distractors were the three laws of this topic: continuity (wrong, it concerns smooth direction of lines), similarity (wrong, it concerns likeness of features, not nearness) and closure (wrong, it concerns completing gaps in a figure).
50Memory models: connectionist (Rumelhart & McClelland) vs network (Collins & Quillian) vs associative
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q45 · added from the options

From scratch

All three families describe long-term knowledge as things linked to other things, which is why one stem can fit several options. The difference is what a node stands for and where knowledge lives.

Associative models are the oldest family. Aristotle proposed that ideas are linked by contiguity, similarity and contrast; the British associationists (Locke, Hume, Hartley, James Mill) built the mind out of associations. Ebbinghaus (1885) brought association into the laboratory with nonsense syllables and serial learning; paired-associate learning (learn A-B, then recall B given A) became the standard method for studying interference. Formal descendants are HAM, Human Associative Memory (Anderson and Bower, 1973), which stored propositions as linked concept nodes, and Anderson's ACT and ACT* (1976, 1983), which add spreading activation and a separate procedural memory of production rules. Core claim: memories are discrete items joined by learned links whose strength grows with practice and co-occurrence.

Network (semantic network) models are associative models specialised for meaning. Quillian's (1968) Teachable Language Comprehender was implemented in Collins and Quillian (1969) as a hierarchical network: each node is a concept (CANARY, BIRD, ANIMAL), links are "is a" and property links, and cognitive economy stores each property once at the highest level. Topic 7 covered the sentence-verification evidence and the failures (typicality, "a chicken is an animal" faster than "a chicken is a bird"). Collins and Loftus (1975) loosened it into a spreading-activation web. A rival account, the feature comparison model (Smith, Shoben and Rips, 1974), dropped links altogether and compared lists of defining and characteristic features. In network models the representation is local: one node equals one concept.

Connectionist (PDP) models come from Rumelhart, McClelland and the PDP Research Group, Parallel Distributed Processing (two volumes, 1986). A forerunner was the interactive activation model of word recognition (McClelland and Rumelhart, 1981), with feature, letter and word levels exciting and inhibiting each other. Key properties:

  • Units are neuron-like, not concepts; a concept is a pattern of activation across many units (distributed representation).
  • Knowledge lives in the connection weights, not in any one node; the same weights store many memories (superimposed).
  • Parallel processing: many units update at once.
  • Learning changes weights: Hebb's rule (1949, units active together strengthen their link), the delta rule, and backpropagation (Rumelhart, Hinton and Williams, 1986).
  • Graceful degradation: damage to some units makes performance worse gradually, not all-or-none, like real brain damage.
  • Content-addressable memory and pattern completion: a partial cue retrieves the whole pattern; generalisation to new cases is automatic.

Modern deep learning is a descendant of this PDP tradition.

Key distinctions

FeatureAssociativeSemantic networkConnectionist (PDP)
Key namesAristotle, Ebbinghaus, Anderson and Bower (HAM)Collins and Quillian 1969; Collins and Loftus 1975Rumelhart and McClelland 1986
A node isAn idea or itemA conceptA neuron-like unit with no meaning alone
RepresentationLocalLocalDistributed
Knowledge stored inLink strengthLinks and node propertiesConnection weights
ProcessingSequential chainingSearch or spreading activationMassively parallel
EvidencePaired associates, interferenceSentence verification, primingGraceful degradation, learning from examples

MCQ traps

  • "Interconnected nodes" fits all three in plain English. Look for extra cues: "hierarchy" or "cognitive economy" → Collins and Quillian; "weights", "parallel", "distributed" or "neural" → connectionist.
  • Rumelhart and McClelland ≠ Collins and Quillian. PDP is 1986; the hierarchical network is 1969.
  • Spreading activation belongs to Collins and Loftus (and ACT*), not to the original 1969 model.
  • "Linkage model" is not a standard memory model; it is filler.
  • Atkinson and Shiffrin's multi-store model is about stores (sensory, STM, LTM), not the organisation of knowledge.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q45: Which model proposes memory is organised in a network of interconnected nodes → official key Connectionist model. Network model (Collins and Quillian) is the strongest distractor, since its nodes are also linked; associative model is the older parent family; linkage model is filler. Textbook note: the wording fits a semantic network too, so in the exam go with the key, but in other papers read for "parallel" or "weights" (connectionist) versus "hierarchy" or "concepts" (network).
51Forgetting: proactive vs retroactive interference, decay, retrieval failure, Tulving's encoding specificity
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q95, Q82, Q71 · added from the options

From scratch

Forgetting theories answer one question: when you cannot remember something, is the trace gone (not available) or merely unreachable (available but not accessible)? Tulving's distinction between availability and accessibility is the frame for everything below.

The forgetting curve. Hermann Ebbinghaus (On Memory, 1885) learned lists of nonsense syllables and measured savings (how much less time relearning took). Forgetting is fastest in the first hour, then levels off: a negatively accelerated curve.

Decay (trace decay) theory. Memory traces fade with time if not used, like footprints in sand. Brown (1958) and Peterson and Peterson (1959) gave a consonant trigram (such as KQF), then had participants count backward by threes to block rehearsal; recall fell to about 10 percent after 18 seconds, which they read as rapid decay in STM. Problem: Keppel and Underwood (1962) showed that on the first trial there was almost no forgetting; it built up over trials, which means earlier trigrams were interfering (proactive interference), not time. McGeoch (1932) had already argued that time itself does nothing; events during time cause forgetting, as rust is caused by oxidation, not by time. Jenkins and Dallenbach (1924): less forgetting after sleep than after waking activity.

Interference theory. Similar memories compete. Experimental designs:

Retroactive (RI)Proactive (PI)
Experimental groupLearn A → learn B → recall ALearn B → learn A → recall A
Control groupLearn A → rest → recall ARest → learn A → recall A
DirectionNew disrupts old (backward)Old disrupts new (forward)
ExampleNew password makes the old one hard to recallOld password keeps intruding when typing the new one

Interference is greatest when the two lists are similar (A-B, A-D paired associates: same stimulus, new response). Underwood (1957) showed that much forgetting in classic studies was PI from earlier lists. Release from PI (Wickens, 1972): switching category (fruits to professions) restores recall.

Retrieval failure (cue-dependent forgetting). The memory is available but the right cue is missing. Tulving and Pearlstone (1966): people who learned categorised word lists recalled far more when given the category names as cues. The tip-of-the-tongue state (Brown and McNeill, 1966) shows partial access: you know the first letter and number of syllables but not the word.

Encoding specificity principle (Tulving and Thomson, 1973). A cue helps retrieval only if it was encoded together with the item at learning. Whatever is present at encoding (context, mood, physical state) becomes part of the trace. Forms: context-dependent memory (Godden and Baddeley, 1975: divers who learned words underwater recalled them better underwater, and vice versa); state-dependent memory (Goodwin and colleagues, 1969: material learned under alcohol recalled better under alcohol); mood-congruent and mood-dependent memory (Bower, 1981). The related transfer-appropriate processing idea (Morris, Bransford and Franks, 1977) says memory is best when the type of processing at test matches that at study.

Other accounts: consolidation failure (Muller and Pilzecker, 1900: new traces need time to set; ECT and head injury cause retrograde amnesia); motivated forgetting / repression (Freud) and directed forgetting.

Key distinctions

  • Decay = trace fades with time; interference = other memories block; retrieval failure = trace intact, cue missing.
  • Retroactive = new hurts old; proactive = old hurts new.
  • Encoding specificity is the general principle; context-dependent and state-dependent memory are special cases.
  • Serial position is not a forgetting theory, but the recency part disappears with a distractor task, which is interference/displacement in STM.

MCQ traps

  • "Interference from new information" → retroactive, even though "pro" sounds like "progress". Retro = backward.
  • Peterson and Peterson's result looked like decay but was largely proactive interference (Keppel and Underwood).
  • "Principle of congruity" is Osgood and Tannenbaum's attitude-change model, not a memory principle.
  • Recognition beats recall because recognition supplies the cue, evidence for retrieval failure over loss.
  • Exam context effects (same room) are encoding specificity, not exam anxiety or personality.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q95: Difficulty recalling due to interference from new information → Retroactive interference. Distractors from this topic: proactive interference (wrong direction, old on new), decay theory (fading with time, no interference involved) and retrieval failure (missing cue, not competition).
  • Q82: Rahul does better in the exam held in his usual classroom → The principle of encoding specificity (context-dependent memory). "Principle of congruity" was the trap: it is an attitude-change model. Examination fear and anxious personality are non-memory explanations.
  • Q71: First and last items remembered better → Primacy-recency effect; the link to this topic is that recency is the part lost to interference when a distractor task follows the list.
52Operant terms: generalization, discrimination, shaping
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q77, Q65 · added from the options

From scratch

These are the "process" words of learning. They appear in both classical and operant conditioning, and MCQs reuse them as distractors whenever extinction or reinforcement is asked.

Stimulus generalisation. Once a response has been learned in the presence of one stimulus, similar stimuli also produce it, and the more similar they are, the stronger the response. Plotting response strength against similarity gives a generalisation gradient. Classic operant study: Guttman and Kalish (1956) reinforced pigeons for pecking a key lit at about 580 nm (yellow-orange); in extinction tests with other wavelengths, pecking fell off smoothly as the colour moved further from the training value. Classical example: Little Albert (Watson and Rayner, 1920), conditioned to fear a white rat, also feared a rabbit, a dog, a fur coat and a Santa Claus mask. Generalisation is adaptive (one bad snake teaches caution with all snakes) but underlies phobias and PTSD triggers. Response generalisation is the mirror case: a reinforced response spreads to similar responses.

Discrimination. Learning to respond to one stimulus and not to similar ones. In operant terms, the stimulus signalling that reinforcement is available is the discriminative stimulus (SD); the one signalling no reinforcement is S-delta (SΔ). A rat presses only when the light is on because presses in the dark never pay. When behaviour reliably depends on the SD, it is under stimulus control. A finding: after discrimination training, the peak of the generalisation gradient shifts away from S-delta (peak shift, Hanson, 1959). Pavlov's experimental neurosis: dogs forced to discriminate a circle from an ellipse that was made more and more circular became agitated and disorganised. Generalisation and discrimination are opposites: discrimination training sharpens the gradient.

Shaping (method of successive approximations). Skinner's technique for producing a behaviour that the organism would never emit spontaneously. Reinforce any response roughly in the right direction, then raise the criterion step by step: for a rat, reinforce facing the lever, then approaching it, then touching it, then pressing it. Chaining links several shaped responses into a sequence, each response producing the SD for the next; backward chaining teaches the last link first. Prompting and fading help early responses and then remove the help. Limits: Breland and Breland (1961), "The misbehavior of organisms", showed instinctive drift: raccoons trained to drop coins in a box began rubbing them together as they would wash food.

Applications. Shaping and chaining are core to applied behaviour analysis: teaching speech and self-care in autism (Lovaas), skill-building in intellectual disability in special schools, and the token economy (Ayllon and Azrin, 1968) in psychiatric wards. Discrimination training is used in teaching reading; generalisation training ensures skills carry over from clinic to home.

Key distinctions

TermWhat happensExample
GeneralisationResponse spreads to similar stimuliAlbert fears the rabbit
DiscriminationResponse only to the reinforced stimulusPressing only when the light is on
ShapingReinforce successive approximationsTeaching a rat to press a lever
ExtinctionResponse fades when reinforcement stopsNo more pellets, pressing stops
Spontaneous recoveryExtinguished response reappears after restPressing returns next day

MCQ traps

  • "Previously reinforced behaviour no longer reinforced and fades" → extinction, not discrimination (discrimination involves two stimuli, one reinforced and one not).
  • Generalisation is not forgetting; it is responding to new similar stimuli.
  • Shaping reinforces approximations; chaining links complete responses. Do not swap them.
  • SD does not cause the response (it is not a CS); it signals that reinforcement is available.
  • Both generalisation and discrimination occur in classical and operant conditioning; shaping is operant only.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q77: In operant conditioning, a previously reinforced behaviour no longer reinforced gradually fades → Extinction. All three distractors were this topic's terms, each wrong: generalisation (spreading to similar stimuli), discrimination (responding only to the reinforced stimulus) and shaping (building a new response by successive approximations).
  • Q65: Main difference between classical and operant conditioning → Classical pairs two stimuli; operant relies on reinforcement and punishment. Shaping, SD and discrimination training all sit on the operant side of that line.
53Positive psych names: Seligman, Csikszentmihalyi (flow), Fredrickson, Bryant (savoring), plus Beck's cognitive distortions
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q56, Q13 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 10 covered what savoring, flow and broaden-and-build are. This topic is about the people: what else each name is known for, because IHBAS writes "X is known for" stems and fills the options with the other names.

Martin Seligman began in the clinic, not in happiness. With Steven Maier (and Overmier) in 1967 he described learned helplessness: dogs given inescapable shocks in a harness later failed to escape in a shuttle box even when escape was easy, while dogs given escapable shocks learned normally (the "triadic design": escapable, inescapable, no-shock groups). Helplessness became a model of depression. In 1978 Abramson, Seligman and Teasdale reformulated it in attributional terms: people who explain bad events as internal, stable and global are at risk of depression. This became explanatory style, measured by the Attributional Style Questionnaire (Peterson et al., 1982), and the popular "3 Ps" (personal, permanent, pervasive) of Learned Optimism (1990). Only then, as APA President (1998), did he launch positive psychology, later writing Authentic Happiness (2002: the pleasant, engaged and meaningful lives) and Flourish (2011: PERMA).

Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi is the flow man, but also the inventor (with Reed Larson) of the Experience Sampling Method (ESM): participants carry a pager and, at random signals through the day, record what they are doing and feeling. ESM is how flow was found in daily life (more often at work than in passive leisure such as television, the "paradox of work"). He also described the autotelic personality, people who seek flow for its own sake.

Barbara Fredrickson is broaden-and-build (1998, 2001), the undoing hypothesis and the upward spiral. Later work: a 2008 field experiment showing that loving-kindness meditation increased daily positive emotions, which in turn built resources (mindfulness, social support, purpose) and raised life satisfaction, a direct test of "build". Her book Positivity (2009) popularised the now-discredited 3:1 ratio.

Fred Bryant (with Joseph Veroff) is savoring. He also built the Savoring Beliefs Inventory (2003), which measures perceived ability to savor across anticipation, the moment and reminiscence, and the Ways of Savoring Checklist for strategies.

Aaron Beck is not a positive psychologist; he is the founder of cognitive therapy (1960s; Cognitive Therapy of Depression, Beck, Rush, Shaw and Emery, 1979). His model has three layers: schemas (deep core beliefs, e.g. "I am unlovable"), cognitive distortions (systematic errors in processing), and negative automatic thoughts. The content of depression is the cognitive triad: negative views of the self, the world and the future. Beck's original distortions:

DistortionMeaningExample
Arbitrary inferenceConclusion without evidence"She did not reply, she hates me"
Selective abstractionOne detail out of context defines the wholeOne critical remark in a good review
OvergeneralisationA rule from one incident"I failed one mock, I always fail"
Magnification / minimisationErrors blown up, successes shrunkCatastrophising a small mistake
PersonalisationTaking responsibility for external events"The team lost because of me"
Dichotomous thinkingAll-or-nothing categories"If not first rank, I am a failure"

David Burns (Feeling Good, 1980) added labelling, mind reading, fortune telling, "should" statements, emotional reasoning, mental filter and disqualifying the positive.

Key distinctions

  • Beck vs Ellis: Beck's distortions are tested empirically ("collaborative empiricism", Socratic questioning); Albert Ellis's REBT (ABC model) disputes irrational beliefs ("musts") more directly.
  • Learned helplessness (Seligman) vs learned optimism (Seligman): same man, same attribution dimensions, opposite poles.
  • Flow vs savoring: flow is absorption with loss of self-awareness; savoring needs deliberate awareness of the pleasure.
  • Csikszentmihalyi's ESM vs diary methods: ESM uses random real-time signals, reducing recall bias.

MCQ traps

  • "Cognitive distortions" as an option next to Fredrickson or Seligman is a Beck distractor, never positive psychology.
  • Learned helplessness is Seligman, not Skinner or Bandura; and its depression link runs through attributions (internal, stable, global).
  • Seligman founded positive psychology; Csikszentmihalyi co-authored the 2000 launch paper. "Father of positive psychology" = Seligman.
  • Savoring = Bryant; do not hand it to Fredrickson just because both are about positive emotion.
  • Beck's triad is self, world, future; not "thoughts, feelings, behaviour" (that is the CBT triangle).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q56: Barbara Fredrickson is known for → Broaden-and-build theory. Distractors: cognitive distortions (Beck), flow theory (Csikszentmihalyi), psychodynamic theory (Freud). Every wrong option is a name from this topic.
  • Q13: Savoring involves → Enhancing the enjoyment of positive experiences (Bryant and Veroff). Avoiding negatives, relaxation and reflecting on trauma are not savoring.
54Theory of mind
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q70 · added from the options

From scratch

Theory of mind (ToM) is the ability to attribute mental states (beliefs, desires, intentions, knowledge, emotions) to oneself and others, and to understand that others' mental states can differ from one's own and from reality. It is called a "theory" because mental states are unobservable: we infer them, as a scientist infers unseen causes.

The term comes from David Premack and Guy Woodruff (1978), "Does the chimpanzee have a theory of mind?", who showed a chimpanzee (Sarah) videos of a human struggling with a problem and asked whether she could pick the solution, implying she understood the human's goal. The philosopher Daniel Dennett then argued that the real test is false belief: you only prove you represent someone's mind if you can predict behaviour based on a belief you know to be false.

Classic tasks:

  • Unexpected transfer (Wimmer and Perner, 1983): Maxi puts chocolate in a cupboard and leaves; his mother moves it. Where will Maxi look? Most 3-year-olds say the new place; most 4 to 5-year-olds correctly say the old place.
  • Sally-Anne test (Baron-Cohen, Leslie and Frith, 1985): Sally puts a marble in her basket and leaves; Anne moves it to her box. About 80 percent of autistic children failed, whereas about 86 percent of children with Down syndrome and 85 percent of typically developing 4-year-olds passed. Because the Down syndrome group had lower mental age, the deficit was specific to mentalising, not general intelligence.
  • Unexpected contents / Smarties task (Perner, Leekam and Wimmer, 1987): a Smarties tube contains pencils; 3-year-olds say a friend will think it holds pencils, and even misremember their own earlier belief.
  • Second-order false belief ("John thinks that Mary thinks..."): passed around 6 to 7 years (Perner and Wimmer, 1985).
  • Advanced tests: Faux Pas test; Reading the Mind in the Eyes test (Baron-Cohen et al., 2001), used with adults.

Developmental path: joint attention and gaze following (about 9 to 14 months, the "nine-month revolution"), pretend play (18 to 24 months; Leslie argued pretence requires "metarepresentation"), desire psychology (about 2 years: people want different things), belief psychology and first-order false belief (about 4 years), second-order (6 to 7), sarcasm and faux pas (later childhood). ToM development is helped by language, especially mental-state verbs ("think", "know") and sentence complements, and by having older siblings.

Clinical relevance. Simon Baron-Cohen's "mindblindness" account (1995) proposed a core ToM deficit in autism, fitting DSM-5-TR criterion A (deficits in social-emotional reciprocity, nonverbal communication, relationships). In India, autism is assessed with ISAA. ToM deficits are also reported in schizophrenia (Chris Frith, 1992, linked them to paranoia and negative symptoms) and in frontotemporal dementia. Neural correlates: temporoparietal junction (Rebecca Saxe), medial prefrontal cortex and the superior temporal sulcus.

Key distinctions

ConceptCore idea
Theory of mindKnowing others have beliefs and intentions (cognitive)
EmpathySharing or responding to others' feelings (affective); autistic people may have intact affective empathy but weak cognitive ToM
Egocentrism (Piaget)Preoperational child cannot take another's visual perspective (three mountains task, Piaget and Inhelder, 1956); ToM research showed belief understanding emerges earlier, around 4
Mental representationGeneral capacity to hold internal models; ToM is a special case (representing representations)
Phonological awarenessHearing and manipulating speech sounds; the foundation of reading, not ToM

MCQ traps

  • False belief, not true belief, is the critical test: predicting behaviour from a correct belief can be done by just tracking reality.
  • Typical age for first-order false belief is about 4; 3-year-olds fail.
  • Sally-Anne is Baron-Cohen, Leslie and Frith (1985); the Maxi task is Wimmer and Perner (1983). Premack and Woodruff coined the term with chimpanzees.
  • ToM is a social-cognitive skill; it supports reading comprehension of characters' motives, but the basic skill underlying learning to read is spoken language / phonology.
  • The autism ToM deficit is specific: Down syndrome children of lower mental age pass.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q70: Which cognitive skill is involved in learning to read → Spoken language (official key). Theory of mind appeared only as a distractor (option b): it underpins social understanding and inferring characters' intentions, but decoding print rests on oral language, phonological awareness and the alphabetic principle. Mental representation (too broad) and imagination were the other distractors.
55Branches: counselling, forensic, developmental, I/O
General / Cognitive · IHBAS 2024 Q61, Q1 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 12 covered clinical vs psychiatry and the basics of I/O. Here the focus is on the three branches that were wrong options in Q61, plus the deeper history of I/O.

Counselling psychology helps relatively well-functioning people with adjustment, developmental, educational, vocational and relationship problems. It grew from three roots: Frank Parsons (Choosing a Vocation, 1909, father of vocational guidance; matching traits to jobs), the mental-hygiene and psychometric movements, and Carl Rogers (Counseling and Psychotherapy, 1942), whose non-directive approach challenged E. G. Williamson's directive "trait-factor" (Minnesota) counselling. Career theories that show up in MCQs: John Holland's RIASEC types (Realistic, Investigative, Artistic, Social, Enterprising, Conventional) and Donald Super's life-span, life-space theory (career stages: growth, exploration, establishment, maintenance, decline). Counselling psychology emphasises strengths, prevention and normal development; clinical psychology emphasises assessment and treatment of psychopathology. The line blurs in practice, but in India only RCI-registered clinical psychologists are licensed for clinical practice.

Forensic psychology applies psychology to the legal system. Hugo Münsterberg (On the Witness Stand, 1908) argued eyewitness testimony is unreliable. Elizabeth Loftus showed this experimentally: in Loftus and Palmer (1974), the verb "smashed" vs "hit" changed speed estimates and made people falsely recall broken glass (the misinformation effect). Forensic tasks include fitness to stand trial, assessment of criminal responsibility (the M'Naghten rule, 1843: did the accused know the nature of the act or that it was wrong; in India IPC Section 84, now Section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023), risk assessment, custody evaluations, offender rehabilitation and police selection. Indian landmark: Selvi v. State of Karnataka (2010), where the Supreme Court held that involuntary narco-analysis, polygraph and brain-mapping tests violate the right against self-incrimination.

Developmental psychology studies change across the lifespan. G. Stanley Hall (Adolescence, 1904) described "storm and stress"; Arnold Gesell stressed maturation; Piaget, Erikson, Vygotsky and Kohlberg supplied stage theories. Paul Baltes framed the life-span perspective: development is lifelong, multidimensional, multidirectional (gains and losses), plastic and contextual. Research designs are a favourite MCQ: cross-sectional (different ages at one time; quick but confounded by cohort effects), longitudinal (same people over time; shows individual change but suffers attrition and practice effects), and sequential / cohort-sequential (K. Warner Schaie's Seattle Longitudinal Study combined both).

Industrial-organisational (I/O) psychology deepened: Münsterberg is also the father of industrial psychology (Psychology and Industrial Efficiency, 1913), Walter Dill Scott applied psychology to advertising and selection, and World War I's Army Alpha and Beta tests (Yerkes, 1917) launched mass personnel testing. The Hawthorne studies (Western Electric, 1924 to 1932, Elton Mayo and colleagues) shifted attention from lighting and pay to social factors and gave us the Hawthorne effect. I/O has three wings: personnel (selection, appraisal, training), organisational (motivation, leadership, culture, stress) and human factors / ergonomics.

Key distinctions

BranchTypical client / questionName to remember
ClinicalDiagnosable mental disorderWitmer, 1896
CounsellingAdjustment, career, normal-range distressParsons, Rogers, Super, Holland
ForensicCourts, police, prisons, eyewitnessesMünsterberg, Loftus
DevelopmentalChange with ageHall, Piaget, Baltes
I/OWorkplace selection, productivity, well-beingMünsterberg, Scott, Mayo
HealthBehaviour and physical illnessBiopsychosocial model (Engel, 1977)

MCQ traps

  • "Optimising behaviour in workplaces" or "organisational management" signals I/O, never counselling, even though career counselling touches work.
  • Münsterberg appears twice: father of forensic (1908) and of industrial (1913) psychology.
  • Cross-sectional studies confound age with cohort; longitudinal studies confound age with time of measurement and lose participants.
  • Forensic psychologists assess sanity; the legal decision of insanity belongs to the court.
  • Counselling is not "lighter clinical psychology" in law: in India the RCI licence is tied to clinical/rehabilitation qualifications.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q61: Branch concerned with optimising human behaviour in workplaces → Industrial-organisational psychology. Counselling (adjustment and career), forensic (legal system) and developmental (lifespan change) appeared only as distractors.
  • Q1: Clinical psychology primarily focuses on → Assessment and treatment of mental illness; "industrial applications" and "organisational management" were I/O distractors, and "behavioural changes in animals" pointed to comparative psychology.
56Attribution: self-serving bias, fundamental attribution error, stereotyping
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q51, Q15 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 15 defined FAE and the actor-observer effect. This topic goes deeper into why the FAE happens, the self-serving bias, and stereotyping, the three options that lost in Q51.

Fundamental attribution error, deeper. Beyond Jones and Harris (1967), the key demonstration is Ross, Amabile and Steinmetz (1977), the quizmaster study: participants were randomly assigned to be questioners (who wrote hard questions from their own knowledge) or contestants. Observers, and even contestants, rated questioners as more knowledgeable, ignoring the obvious role advantage. Daniel Gilbert's two-step model explains it: we automatically characterise the person ("he is nervous"), then effortfully correct for the situation. Under cognitive load (busyness, time pressure) the correction step fails, so the FAE grows. Gilbert and Malone (1995) listed causes: lack of awareness of situational constraints, unrealistic expectations, inflated categorisation of behaviour, and incomplete correction. The FAE is weaker in collectivist cultures (Miller, 1984, with Indian participants; Morris and Peng, 1994).

Self-serving bias (Miller and Ross, 1975): we attribute our successes to internal causes (ability, effort) and our failures to external causes (bad luck, unfair paper). "I topped because I studied; I failed because the questions were out of syllabus." It protects self-esteem (motivational account) but also reflects expectation (we expect success, so failure seems situational). A meta-analysis by Mezulis and colleagues (2004) found it large and near-universal, weaker in some Asian samples, and weakest in depression, which shows the reverse pattern (Seligman's internal, stable, global style for failures). Group-level version: group-serving bias or the ultimate attribution error (Pettigrew, 1979): in-group successes are dispositional, out-group successes are luck. Relatives: false consensus effect (Ross, Greene and House, 1977: overestimating how many share our views), just-world belief (Melvin Lerner: victims "must have deserved it"), and defensive attribution.

Stereotyping. Walter Lippmann (1922) coined "stereotype" as "pictures in our heads". Katz and Braly (1933) measured them with adjective checklists for ethnic groups. The ABC triad: stereotype = cognitive belief about a group; prejudice = affective, usually negative attitude; discrimination = behaviour. Gordon Allport (The Nature of Prejudice, 1954) saw categorisation as normal and proposed the contact hypothesis (equal status, common goals, cooperation, institutional support). Mechanisms: illusory correlation (Hamilton and Gifford, 1976: rare behaviours by minority groups seem linked), out-group homogeneity effect ("they are all alike"), Devine's (1989) finding that stereotypes are activated automatically even in low-prejudice people who then suppress them, and measurement with the Implicit Association Test (Greenwald et al., 1998). Consequences: stereotype threat (Steele and Aronson, 1995: Black students underperformed when a test was framed as diagnostic of ability) and the self-fulfilling prophecy (Rosenthal and Jacobson, 1968, "Pygmalion in the Classroom"; Word, Zanna and Cooper, 1974, interview behaviour). In India, caste, regional and gender stereotypes are the standard textbook examples.

Key distinctions

BiasWhose behaviourPattern
FAEOthersOver-dispositional, under-situational
Actor-observerSelf vs othersSelf: situation; others: disposition
Self-servingOwn outcomesSuccess internal, failure external
Ultimate attribution errorIn-group vs out-groupGroup-level self-serving
StereotypingMembers of a groupTraits assigned by group membership
Halo effectOne personOne good trait colours all others

MCQ traps

  • Self-serving bias depends on outcome (success vs failure); actor-observer depends on perspective (self vs other). A stem about "own behaviour external, others internal" without mention of success or failure is actor-observer.
  • FAE is about others only; it says nothing about how you explain yourself.
  • Stereotyping is a belief about a group; prejudice is the feeling; discrimination is the act.
  • "Judging someone based on group membership" is stereotyping, not the halo effect.
  • Depressed people do not show the self-serving bias (sometimes called depressive realism, Alloy and Abramson, 1979).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q51: Attributing own behaviour to external causes and others' to internal causes → Actor-observer effect. Self-serving bias (wrong: it hinges on success vs failure), fundamental attribution error (wrong: only covers others' behaviour) and stereotyping (wrong: not an attribution about causes) were distractors.
  • Q15: The halo effect is → influence of a positive trait on perception of other traits. Option a, "judging others based on their group membership", is the definition of stereotyping, used here as a distractor.
57Groups: deindividuation, group polarisation, sleeper effect, diffusion of responsibility (Darley & Latané)
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q22, Q34, Q79, Q57 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 16 gave one-line definitions. Here are the studies and theories behind each.

Deindividuation is a state of reduced self-awareness and weakened restraint that occurs in groups, especially under anonymity, arousal and diffused responsibility. Gustave Le Bon (The Crowd, 1895) described the "group mind"; Festinger, Pepitone and Newcomb (1952) coined the term. Zimbardo (1969): women wearing hoods and lab coats gave longer "shocks" than women with name tags. Diener and colleagues (1976), Halloween study: trick-or-treating children who were anonymous and in groups took extra sweets far more often (over half) than identified children alone (under one in ten). Leon Mann (1981) found crowds baited would-be suicide jumpers more when large and at night. But Johnson and Downing (1979) showed anonymity in nurses' uniforms reduced aggression compared with Ku Klux Klan-style robes, and the SIDE model (Reicher, Spears and Postmes, 1995) reinterprets deindividuation: anonymity makes people follow the group's norm more, which may be good or bad.

Group polarisation: group discussion pushes members' average opinion further in the direction it already leaned.
