It began as the risky shift (James Stoner, 1961, choice-dilemma items), then Moscovici and Zavalloni (1969) showed French students became more positive about de Gaulle and more negative about Americans after discussion, and Myers and Bishop (1970) showed low-prejudice students grew less prejudiced and high-prejudice students more prejudiced. Explanations: persuasive arguments theory (Burnstein and Vinokur: you hear new arguments on your side), social comparison (you want to be a bit more extreme than average in the valued direction), and self-categorisation (conforming to a polarised in-group prototype). It feeds groupthink and online echo chambers.

Sleeper effect (Hovland, Lumsdaine and Sheffield, 1949, Army "Why We Fight" films; Hovland and Weiss, 1951): a persuasive message from a low-credibility source has little impact at first, but its impact increases over time because the discounting cue (the untrustworthy source) is forgotten or dissociated faster than the message content. Pratkanis and colleagues (1988) showed it is strongest when the discounting cue comes after the message. It is a persuasion phenomenon from the Yale communication programme, not a group-effort effect.

Diffusion of responsibility (Darley and Latané, 1968): with more bystanders, each feels less personally responsible. In the seizure study, participants over an intercom heard a "student" have a seizure; by the end of the fit, 85 percent of those who believed they were alone sought help, compared with 62 percent in three-person and 31 percent in six-person groups. Related: pluralistic ignorance (smoke-filled room, Latané and Darley, 1968: 75 percent reported smoke when alone, about 10 percent with two passive confederates) and the "lady in distress" study (Latané and Rodin, 1969). Counter-finding: in the Piliavin, Rodin and Piliavin (1969) subway study, help was fast and frequent with no diffusion, explained by an arousal: cost-reward model. Ways to reduce diffusion: single out one person ("you in the blue shirt, call an ambulance").

Key distinctions

PhenomenonCore mechanismWhat it affects
DeindividuationAnonymity → loss of self-awarenessRestraint, norm-following
Group polarisationDiscussion → more extreme positionGroup decisions
Sleeper effectSource forgotten, message rememberedPersuasion over time
Diffusion of responsibilityResponsibility split among bystandersHelping
Social loafingPooled, unidentifiable effortEffort on collective tasks
Social facilitationPresence of others → arousalPerformance (simple tasks improve)

MCQ traps

  • Group polarisation is not "groups always become riskier": groups become more extreme in their initial direction, which can be more cautious.
  • The sleeper effect is about delayed persuasion, so it can never be the answer to an effort, helping or performance stem.
  • Diffusion of responsibility is the main cause of the bystander effect; pluralistic ignorance is the interpretation-stage cause.
  • Bystander effect concerns helping; noticing is only slower in groups, so "less likely to notice" is not the definition.
  • Deindividuation needs anonymity or immersion in a group; mere presence of others is social facilitation.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q22: Less effort when working with others → Social loafing. Deindividuation and sleeper effect were distractors (wrong mechanisms: anonymity-driven disinhibition; delayed persuasion).
  • Q34: Improved performance in presence of others → Social facilitation. Group polarisation and diffusion of responsibility were distractors.
  • Q79: The bystander effect occurs because of → Diffusion of responsibility; group polarisation was a distractor.
  • Q57: The bystander effect suggests → People are more likely to help when alone than in a group, the direct result of Darley and Latané's diffusion findings.
58Classic studies: Sherif's Robbers Cave, Milgram, Zimbardo
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q25 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 17 named these studies. Here are the designs and numbers, because IHBAS matches study to concept.

Sherif's Robbers Cave experiment (1954; Sherif, Harvey, White, Hood and Sherif, 1961). Muzafer Sherif took 22 eleven-year-old boys, similar in background and mutual strangers, to a summer camp at Robbers Cave State Park, Oklahoma, in two separately housed groups. Stage 1, in-group formation: each group bonded and named itself (the Eagles and the Rattlers). Stage 2, friction: a tournament with prizes only for winners produced name-calling, flag burning and raids. Stage 3, integration: mere contact (shared meals, films) failed; what worked was superordinate goals, goals neither group could achieve alone (repairing the camp water supply, pooling money for a film, pulling a stalled food truck). The study supports realistic conflict theory: competition for scarce resources breeds hostility. Contrast with Tajfel's minimal groups, where mere categorisation is enough for in-group favouritism.

Milgram's obedience study (Yale, 1963; book 1974). 40 men aged 20 to 50 from New Haven, recruited for a "study of memory and learning", were assigned (rigged) as "teacher". The "learner" (a confederate) was strapped in next door. The shock generator ran from 15 V to 450 V in 15 V steps, labelled from "Slight shock" to "Danger: severe shock" and finally "XXX". At 300 V the learner pounded on the wall; after 315 V he fell silent. If teachers hesitated, the experimenter used four standard prods, ending with "You have no other choice, you must go on." Result: all went to 300 V, and 26 of 40 (65 percent) went to 450 V, though psychiatrists had predicted that only about one in a thousand would. Variations: same room as learner 40 percent; hand-forcing (touch) 30 percent; orders by telephone about 20 percent; a run-down Bridgeport office about 48 percent; two peer teachers who rebelled 10 percent. Milgram's explanation: the agentic state, where people see themselves as instruments of an authority, versus the autonomous state. Diana Baumrind (1964) criticised its ethics (deception, distress), helping shape modern ethics codes. Jerry Burger (2009) replicated it to 150 V with similar rates.

Zimbardo's Stanford Prison Experiment (1971). 24 male student volunteers, screened as psychologically healthy, were randomly assigned as guards or prisoners. Prisoners were "arrested" at home, stripped, deloused, given smocks and numbers; guards had khaki uniforms, batons and mirrored sunglasses (deindividuation). Guards became abusive; prisoners rebelled on day 2, then became passive, and several broke down. Planned for two weeks, it was stopped after six days, after Christina Maslach objected. Conclusion: the power of roles and situations. Critiques: demand characteristics, Zimbardo acting as superintendent, evidence that guards were coached, and the BBC Prison Study (Reicher and Haslam, 2006), which argued behaviour depends on social identification, not automatic role-taking. Zimbardo later linked it to Abu Ghraib (The Lucifer Effect, 2007).

The "Zilstein" option. Dr Gregor Zilstein was the fictitious, intimidating experimenter in Stanley Schachter's (1959) affiliation study: women told they would receive painful shocks (high fear) chose to wait with others far more often (about 63 percent) than low-fear women (about 33 percent): "misery loves miserable company".

Key distinctions

StudyConcept shownKey manipulation
Robbers Cave (Sherif)Intergroup conflict, realistic conflict, superordinate goalsCompetition then cooperation
Autokinetic (Sherif, 1935)Norm formation, informational influenceAmbiguous moving light
Obedience (Milgram, 1963)Obedience to authorityExperimenter's prods
Small world (Milgram, 1967)Social networks, "six degrees"Letters passed via acquaintances
Prison (Zimbardo, 1971)Power of rolesRandom role assignment
Affiliation (Schachter, 1959)Fear increases affiliation"Dr Zilstein" shock threat
Line judgement (Asch, 1951)Normative conformityUnanimous wrong majority

MCQ traps

  • Sherif has two famous studies: autokinetic (conformity) and Robbers Cave (intergroup conflict). Read the stem.
  • Milgram's "message" experiment is the small-world study, not obedience; the obedience study used shocks.
  • Zimbardo = roles; Milgram = obedience; Asch = conformity; Sherif Robbers Cave = intergroup conflict.
  • Contact alone did not end the Robbers Cave conflict; superordinate goals did.
  • 65 percent is Milgram's baseline (voice-feedback) figure; quoting 65 percent for Asch is wrong (Asch: about 37 percent of critical trials).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q25: Example of the power of roles → Zimbardo's prison experiment. Distractors: Robbers Cave (intergroup conflict and superordinate goals), "Milgram's message experiment" (small-world networks, or loosely obedience, which is authority not roles) and "Zilstein's shock research" (Schachter's fear-and-affiliation study, using the fictitious Dr Zilstein).
59Informational vs normative influence (Deutsch & Gerard), and obedience vs conformity vs compliance
Social · IHBAS 2024 Q41, Q94 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 17 introduced the definitions. Here is the original experiment, the finer taxonomy, and the extensions examiners borrow distractors from.

Deutsch and Gerard (1955) distinguished two motives for yielding: informational influence, accepting information from others as evidence about reality, and normative influence, conforming to the positive expectations of others, to be liked and avoid rejection. Their experiment modified Asch's line task. Conformity was reduced when responses were anonymous (private) compared with face-to-face, showing a normative component. It increased when participants were told the group would be rewarded for accuracy as a team (stronger normative pressure), and it increased when judgements were made from memory rather than with lines visible (more ambiguity, stronger informational pressure). Even anonymous participants conformed somewhat, showing informational influence operates even without social surveillance.

When each dominates: informational influence is strongest when the task is ambiguous, in a crisis, or when others are experts (Sherif's autokinetic effect). It leads to private acceptance. Normative influence is strongest when the group is important, responses are public, and the answer is clear (Asch). It leads to public compliance without private acceptance. Baron, Vandello and Brunsman (1996) showed the interaction: when accuracy was made important, conformity rose on a hard eyewitness task (informational) but fell on an easy one (normative pressure overridden).

Herbert Kelman (1958): three processes of attitude change. Compliance (to gain reward or avoid punishment; lasts only under surveillance), identification (to be like an admired person or group; lasts while the relationship matters), internalisation (because the content fits one's values; most durable).

Three forms of social influence compared:

FeatureConformityComplianceObedience
SourceGroup norm, peersA person making a requestAn authority figure
Explicit demand?No, implicit pressureYes, a requestYes, a command
Status relationEqualsUsually equalsHierarchical
BehaviourBecomes similar to othersDoes what is askedDoes what is ordered
Classic studyAsch (1951), Sherif (1935)Foot-in-the-door (Freedman and Fraser, 1966)Milgram (1963)
How people explain it laterOften deny being influencedAttribute to persuasion techniquesBlame the authority

Extensions. Social impact theory (Latané, 1981): influence = strength x immediacy x number of sources, with diminishing returns as numbers grow. Minority influence (Moscovici, Lage and Naffrechoux, 1969): a consistent minority of two confederates calling blue slides "green" produced about 8 percent green responses, versus about 1 percent for an inconsistent minority; Moscovici's conversion theory says minorities produce private, delayed change through informational processes, while majorities produce public compliance. Culture: Bond and Smith's (1996) meta-analysis of Asch-type studies across 17 countries found higher conformity in collectivist cultures, relevant to Indian samples. Cialdini and Goldstein (2004) recast the motives as accuracy, affiliation and maintaining a positive self-concept.

Key distinctions

  • Informational vs normative: "want to be right" vs "want to be liked"; private acceptance vs public compliance; ambiguous vs clear task.
  • Compliance (Kelman) vs compliance (request): Kelman's compliance is a depth of acceptance; compliance as a social-influence type is responding to a request. Both imply outward change without inner conviction.
  • Obedience vs compliance: both follow explicit demands; obedience requires authority.
  • Conformity vs obedience: peers vs authority; implicit vs explicit.

MCQ traps

  • "Desire to be correct and accurate" is informational, the classic swap for a normative-influence stem.
  • "Influence based on perceived power and authority" describes obedience, not normative influence.
  • Conformity is not always involuntary, and compliance is not "peer pressure"; peer pressure is conformity.
  • Conformity, compliance and obedience are three different forms; "both refer to obedience" is never right.
  • Sherif's autokinetic study shows informational influence; Asch shows mainly normative.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q41: Normative social influence is → Influence based on the desire to be accepted and liked by others. Distractors: desire to be correct (informational influence), personal beliefs and values (internalisation, not social influence), perceived power and authority (obedience).
  • Q94: Difference between conformity and compliance → Conformity is behaviour change due to social pressure; compliance is following direct requests. Wrong options reversed peer pressure onto compliance, merged both into obedience, and claimed conformity is always involuntary.
60Kohlberg's moral stages + Freud's psychosexual stages
Developmental · IHBAS 2024 Q16, Q49, Q2 · added from the options

From scratch

Lawrence Kohlberg extended Piaget's work on moral judgement. Piaget (The Moral Judgment of the Child, 1932) described two stages: heteronomous morality or moral realism (younger children: rules are fixed and given by authority, wrongness is judged by consequences, belief in immanent justice) and autonomous morality or moral relativism (from about 10: rules are social agreements, intentions matter). Kohlberg's 1958 doctoral dissertation interviewed 72 Chicago boys aged 10, 13 and 16 using moral dilemmas, most famously the Heinz dilemma (should Heinz steal an overpriced drug to save his dying wife?), and he followed them for about 20 years (Colby et al., 1983). What is scored is the reasoning, not the yes/no answer.

LevelStageReasoning
I. Preconventional1. Obedience and punishment orientationWrong if punished
2. Individualism and exchange (instrumental)"You scratch my back"; self-interest
II. Conventional3. Good interpersonal relationships ("good boy, nice girl")Approval, being seen as good
4. Maintaining social order ("law and order")Duty, laws must be upheld
III. Postconventional5. Social contract and individual rightsLaws serve people; can be changed democratically
6. Universal ethical principlesSelf-chosen principles of justice and dignity

Stages are claimed to be invariant in order and universal; most adults reach stage 3 or 4; stage 6 was so rare it was later dropped from scoring. Measures: the Moral Judgment Interview and James Rest's Defining Issues Test (DIT). Critiques: Carol Gilligan (In a Different Voice, 1982) argued the scheme favours a male ethic of justice and undervalues the ethic of care; cross-cultural reviews (Snarey, 1985) found stages 1 to 4 widely but stage 5 rare in traditional societies; and moral reasoning predicts moral behaviour only modestly.

Sigmund Freud's psychosexual stages (Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality, 1905): personality develops as libido (sexual energy, broadly "pleasure") focuses on different erogenous zones. Too much or too little gratification causes fixation, leaving libido "stuck" and producing adult traits; under stress people may regress to the fixation point.

Stage (approx. age)Zone and taskFixation traits
Oral (0 to 1)Mouth; weaningOral-receptive: dependency, overeating, smoking; oral-aggressive: sarcasm
Anal (1 to 3)Anus; toilet trainingAnal-retentive: orderly, stingy, obstinate; anal-expulsive: messy, careless
Phallic (3 to 6)Genitals; Oedipus complex (Electra complex, Jung's term, for girls); castration anxiety, penis envyVanity, recklessness; resolved by identification with the same-sex parent, forming the superego
Latency (6 to puberty)Libido dormant, sublimated into school and friendshipsNot a true stage, no fixation
Genital (puberty on)Mature sexuality and loveGoal of healthy development

Structural link: the id is present at birth, the ego develops in infancy to meet the id's demands realistically, and the superego forms at the end of the phallic stage.

Key distinctions

TheoristStages ofNumberDriver
FreudPsychosexual development5Libido and erogenous zones
EriksonPsychosocial development8, lifespanSocial crises
PiagetCognitive development4Assimilation, accommodation
KohlbergMoral reasoning3 levels, 6 stagesCognitive maturity, role-taking
GilliganEthic of care3 levelsRelationships, responsibility

Freud's oral stage overlaps Erikson's trust vs mistrust; anal overlaps autonomy vs shame; phallic overlaps initiative vs guilt; latency overlaps industry vs inferiority; genital overlaps identity vs role confusion.

MCQ traps

  • Kohlberg studies moral reasoning, not moral behaviour, and scores the justification, not the decision.
  • "Law and order" is stage 4 (conventional), not postconventional; "social contract" is stage 5.
  • Gilligan criticised Kohlberg (gender bias), not Piaget or Freud.
  • Latency is a period of dormancy, not a fixation stage; "Electra complex" is Jung's term, Freud used "feminine Oedipus attitude".
  • Freud = psychosexual; Erikson = psychosocial. One letter apart, a frequent swap.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q16: Who developed stages of cognitive development → Jean Piaget. Sigmund Freud (psychosexual stages) and Lawrence Kohlberg (moral stages) appeared only as distractors, alongside Erikson (psychosocial).
  • Q49: Part of personality mediating between desires and social rules → Ego. Related: the superego that the ego mediates against forms at the resolution of the phallic stage.
  • Q2: Erikson's trust vs mistrust → Infancy; the same period is Freud's oral stage, a cross-theory link examiners like.
61The four lobes + thalamus, hypothalamus, cerebellum
Biopsych · IHBAS 2024 Q3, Q28, Q43, Q74 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 20 covered the prefrontal cortex, amygdala, hippocampus and medulla. Here we finish the map: the other three lobes in depth, plus the three structures that kept turning up as distractors (thalamus, hypothalamus, cerebellum). The cortex is divided by two landmark grooves: the central sulcus (fissure of Rolando) separates frontal from parietal, and the lateral sulcus (Sylvian fissure) separates the temporal lobe below from frontal and parietal above. The parieto-occipital sulcus marks off the occipital lobe at the back.

Parietal lobe. Just behind the central sulcus lies the postcentral gyrus, the primary somatosensory cortex (touch, pain, temperature, body position). Wilder Penfield, stimulating the cortex of awake epilepsy patients (Penfield and Boldrey, 1937; Penfield and Rasmussen, 1950), mapped it as the distorted homunculus: lips, tongue and fingers get huge areas because they are densely innervated. The posterior parietal cortex integrates space and attention. Right parietal damage produces hemispatial neglect (ignoring the left side of space, eating only the right half of the plate) and often anosognosia. Left parietal damage near the angular gyrus produces Gerstmann syndrome: agraphia, acalculia, finger agnosia and left-right disorientation.

Temporal lobe. Contains the primary auditory cortex (Heschl's gyrus, superior temporal gyrus) and, on the left, Wernicke's area (Carl Wernicke, 1874): damage gives fluent but meaningless speech with poor comprehension (receptive aphasia). The inferior temporal cortex is the endpoint of the ventral "what" stream of vision (object and face recognition, fusiform face area). The medial temporal lobe holds the hippocampus and amygdala (memory, fear).

Occipital lobe. The smallest lobe, at the back, devoted to vision. The primary visual cortex (V1, striate cortex) lines the calcarine sulcus and receives input from the lateral geniculate nucleus. Damage gives contralateral visual field loss or cortical blindness; Anton syndrome is cortical blindness with denial of blindness. From V1 two streams leave (Ungerleider and Mishkin, 1982): dorsal to the parietal lobe ("where/how", motion, V5/MT) and ventral to the temporal lobe ("what", colour V4, objects, faces).

Thalamus. A paired egg-shaped mass in the diencephalon, the sensory relay station: every sense except smell synapses here before reaching cortex. Memory aid: Lateral geniculate = Light (vision), Medial geniculate = Music (hearing); ventral posterior nuclei relay body sensation. It also gates attention and generates sleep spindles; the dorsomedial nucleus is damaged in Korsakoff's syndrome.

Hypothalamus. Below the thalamus, the size of an almond, yet it runs motivation and the body's internal balance: the "four Fs" (feeding, fighting, fleeing, mating), temperature, thirst, sleep-wake timing (suprachiasmatic nucleus) and the pituitary (it is the brain-endocrine link). Classic lesion work: damage to the ventromedial hypothalamus produces overeating and obesity (Hetherington and Ranson, 1940); damage to the lateral hypothalamus produces refusal to eat (Anand and Brobeck, 1951), giving the old "dual-centre" theory of hunger.

Cerebellum. "Little brain" at the back of the hindbrain, holding more than half the brain's neurons. It coordinates timing and precision of movement, balance and posture, and procedural/motor learning; Richard Thompson showed the eyeblink classically conditioned response depends on it. Damage gives ataxia (wide-based drunken gait), intention tremor, dysdiadochokinesia, past-pointing and scanning speech, all on the same side as the lesion.

Key distinctions

StructureSignature functionDamage gives
FrontalPlanning, problem-solving, motor, BrocaDysexecutive syndrome, expressive aphasia
ParietalTouch, body sense, spatial attentionNeglect, Gerstmann, astereognosis
TemporalHearing, language comprehension, memory, "what"Wernicke's aphasia, agnosias, amnesia
OccipitalVisionCortical blindness, field defects
ThalamusSensory relay (not smell)Sensory loss, thalamic pain, amnesia
HypothalamusHunger, thirst, temperature, sex, pituitary, circadianOver- or under-eating, endocrine and sleep disorders
CerebellumCoordination, balance, motor learningIpsilateral ataxia, intention tremor
MedullaHeart rate, breathingDeath

MCQ traps

  • Thalamus relays, it does not "control" vital functions. Heart rate and breathing are the medulla; the thalamus is a switchboard.
  • Smell bypasses the thalamus. "Which sense is not relayed through the thalamus" → olfaction.
  • Hypothalamus vs amygdala for fear. The hypothalamus executes the bodily side of fear and aggression (autonomic and hormonal output); the amygdala detects threat and is the textbook answer for "regulating fear and aggression".
  • Cerebellum learns too. It stores procedural and motor learning (riding a bicycle), but new declarative memories need the hippocampus.
  • Cerebellar signs are ipsilateral; cortical motor and sensory signs are contralateral.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q3: Which part of the brain is associated with problem-solving → Frontal lobe; occipital (vision), temporal (hearing, memory) and parietal (touch, spatial attention) were the distractor lobes.
  • Q28: Structure regulating fear and aggression → Amygdala; hypothalamus was a tempting distractor (it carries out the autonomic fight-or-flight output) and cerebellum (coordination) was plainly wrong.
  • Q43: Structure for forming new memories → Hippocampus; cerebellum (motor learning only) and thalamus (relay; only its dorsomedial nucleus matters in Korsakoff's) sat as distractors.
  • Q74: Structure controlling heart rate and breathing → Medulla; thalamus (sensory relay), cerebellum (balance) and hypothalamus (temperature, hunger, hormones, not the breathing rhythm) were all distractors here.
62Homeostasis vs allostasis, circadian rhythm, melatonin (pineal gland)
Biopsych · IHBAS 2024 Q44, Q23 · added from the options

From scratch

Homeostasis. The French physiologist Claude Bernard (19th century) argued that the body keeps a constant milieu intérieur (internal environment). Walter Cannon coined the word homeostasis (1926) and popularised it in The Wisdom of the Body (1932). Homeostasis means keeping a variable (body temperature, blood glucose, blood pH, water balance) near a fixed set point through negative feedback: a sensor detects a deviation, a control centre (often the hypothalamus) triggers a correction, and the correction switches itself off once the set point is restored. A home thermostat is the standard analogy. In psychology, homeostasis underlies Hull's drive-reduction theory (1943): a need creates a drive, and behaviour that reduces it is reinforced.

Allostasis. Sterling and Eyer (1988) introduced allostasis, "stability through change". The set points themselves are moved, by the brain, in anticipation of demands. Before an interview your blood pressure, heart rate, glucose and cortisol rise; that is not a failure of homeostasis but an adaptive, predictive adjustment. The brain is the central organ: it appraises the situation and drives the SAM and HPA axes (adrenaline, cortisol) to meet it, then should switch them off. Bruce McEwen (McEwen and Stellar, 1993; McEwen, 1998) added allostatic load: the cumulative "wear and tear" when allostatic responses are called on too often or are not shut off. McEwen listed four patterns: (1) repeated hits from many stressors, (2) lack of adaptation to the same repeated stressor, (3) prolonged response (failure to shut off), and (4) inadequate response (which lets other systems overreact). Consequences include hypertension, abdominal obesity, insulin resistance, immune suppression and hippocampal atrophy, linking chronic stress to both medical and psychiatric illness.

Circadian rhythm. From Latin circa diem, "about a day" (term from Franz Halberg). Biological rhythms come in three sizes: circadian (about 24 h: sleep-wake, body temperature, cortisol peaking in the early morning), ultradian (shorter than a day: the roughly 90-minute NREM-REM sleep cycle) and infradian (longer than a day: the menstrual cycle, seasonal patterns). The master clock is the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) of the hypothalamus, sitting just above the optic chiasm. It receives light directly from the retina via the retinohypothalamic tract (from melanopsin-containing ganglion cells, not rods and cones). Without time cues the human clock "free-runs" slightly longer than 24 h, as shown by cave and bunker isolation studies (Michel Siffre; Jürgen Aschoff). Environmental cues that reset it are zeitgebers ("time-givers", Aschoff's term), light being the strongest. The 2017 Nobel Prize (Hall, Rosbash and Young) was for the molecular clock genes (e.g. period) in fruit flies. Disruptions: jet lag, shift-work disorder, delayed sleep phase in adolescents, and seasonal affective disorder (Rosenthal and colleagues, 1984), treated with bright-light therapy.

Melatonin and the pineal gland. The pineal gland is a tiny midline endocrine gland in the epithalamus. It secretes melatonin, the "hormone of darkness": the SCN signals via a sympathetic route to the pineal, which releases melatonin at night; light at the eyes suppresses it. Melatonin is synthesised from serotonin (tryptophan → serotonin → N-acetylserotonin → melatonin). It signals "biological night", promotes sleep onset and shifts the clock. Uses: jet lag, delayed sleep phase, sleep problems in autism and in elderly people. Ramelteon is a melatonin receptor agonist hypnotic; agomelatine is an antidepressant acting as a melatonin agonist plus 5-HT2C antagonist.

Key distinctions

ConceptWhoCore idea
HomeostasisBernard; Cannon, 1926/1932Hold a variable constant at a fixed set point by negative feedback (reactive)
AllostasisSterling and Eyer, 1988Brain shifts set points via hormones to anticipate demands (predictive)
Allostatic loadMcEwen, 1993/1998Cumulative cost of chronic or poorly regulated stress responses
General Adaptation SyndromeSelye, 1936Alarm, resistance, exhaustion stages of stress
Circadian rhythmSCN of hypothalamusAbout 24-hour cycle, entrained by zeitgebers
NeuroplasticityHebb, 1949 (idea)Brain rewires with experience; not about hormones

MCQ traps

  • "Regulating stress responses by controlling hormone release" = allostasis. Homeostasis is about keeping a constant, not actively changing hormones to meet stress.
  • Melatonin is pineal, not adrenal. The adrenal cortex gives cortisol and aldosterone; the medulla gives epinephrine and norepinephrine.
  • Clock vs gland. The SCN (hypothalamus) is the clock; the pineal is only the output gland. "Master clock" → SCN, not pineal.
  • Melatonin comes from serotonin, not dopamine or tryptophan directly in one step.
  • Light suppresses melatonin; darkness releases it. Blue screen light at night delays sleep for this reason.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q44: The brain's process of regulating its responses to stress by controlling hormone release → Allostasis; homeostasis was the main distractor (fixed set point maintenance, not anticipatory hormonal adjustment), circadian rhythm was a distractor (24-hour timing, not stress regulation), and neuroplasticity (structural rewiring) was the fourth.
  • Q23: Which hormone is secreted by the adrenal gland → Cortisol and epinephrine (option c); melatonin sat as a distractor because it is secreted by the pineal gland under SCN control.
63Glutamate, GABA, norepinephrine
Biopsych · IHBAS 2024 Q59, Q80, Q91 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 21 covered acetylcholine, dopamine and serotonin. The three transmitters here appeared only as distractors, but they are high-yield in their own right. A useful frame: glutamate is the brain's accelerator, GABA its brake, and norepinephrine its alarm and alertness dial. Most brain synapses use glutamate or GABA; the monoamines (dopamine, norepinephrine, serotonin) are made by small brainstem clusters but project widely to modulate whole systems.

Glutamate is the main excitatory neurotransmitter in the CNS, an amino acid used at the majority of fast excitatory synapses. Its receptors are ionotropic (AMPA, NMDA, kainate) and metabotropic (mGluR). The NMDA receptor is the key to learning: at rest its channel is plugged by a magnesium ion, and it opens only when glutamate is bound and the cell is already depolarised, so it acts as a coincidence detector (a molecular version of Hebb's 1949 rule "cells that fire together wire together"). Calcium entry through NMDA channels triggers long-term potentiation (LTP), first reported in the rabbit hippocampus by Bliss and Lømo (1973), the leading cellular model of memory. Too much glutamate kills neurons (excitotoxicity, Olney): this damages tissue after stroke and head injury and is implicated in Huntington's disease. Clinical links: memantine (an NMDA antagonist) for moderate to severe Alzheimer's; PCP and ketamine (NMDA antagonists) produce psychosis-like states, which grounds the glutamate (NMDA hypofunction) hypothesis of schizophrenia; esketamine is approved for treatment-resistant depression.

GABA (gamma-aminobutyric acid) is the main inhibitory neurotransmitter of the brain (glycine plays that role mainly in the spinal cord). Interestingly it is made from glutamate by the enzyme glutamic acid decarboxylase. GABA-A receptors are ionotropic chloride channels: chloride rushes in, hyperpolarising the cell. Several drug classes act there: benzodiazepines (diazepam, lorazepam) increase the frequency of channel opening, barbiturates increase the duration, and alcohol also enhances GABA-A, which is why combining them is dangerous and why benzodiazepines are used for alcohol withdrawal. Flumazenil reverses benzodiazepine overdose. GABA-B is metabotropic (baclofen is an agonist, used for spasticity). Low GABA activity is linked to anxiety, insomnia and epilepsy (many anticonvulsants such as valproate boost GABA). In Huntington's disease the GABAergic neurons of the striatum die.

Norepinephrine (NE, noradrenaline) is a catecholamine. The synthesis chain is worth memorising: tyrosine → L-DOPA → dopamine → norepinephrine (via dopamine beta-hydroxylase) → epinephrine. In the brain, NE comes mainly from the locus coeruleus in the pons and governs arousal, vigilance, attention and the fight-or-flight response; peripherally it is the transmitter of sympathetic postganglionic neurons and is also released by the adrenal medulla. Clinical links: the catecholamine hypothesis of depression (Schildkraut, 1965) proposed too little NE in depression and too much in mania; reserpine, which depletes monoamines, could cause depression; MAOIs, tricyclics and SNRIs (venlafaxine, duloxetine) raise NE. Atomoxetine, a selective NE reuptake inhibitor, treats ADHD. Excess NE activity fuels panic and PTSD hyperarousal; propranolol (beta-blocker) blunts performance anxiety symptoms, prazosin (alpha-1 blocker) is used for PTSD nightmares, and clonidine (alpha-2 agonist) reduces NE release in opioid withdrawal and ADHD.

Key distinctions

TransmitterTypeMain roleClinical tie
GlutamateAmino acid, excitatoryLearning, LTP via NMDAExcitotoxicity; memantine, ketamine
GABAAmino acid, inhibitoryCalming, braking excitationAnxiety, epilepsy; benzodiazepines, alcohol
NorepinephrineCatecholamineArousal, alertness, fight-or-flightDepression, ADHD, panic; SNRIs, atomoxetine
AcetylcholineEsterMuscle contraction, memoryAlzheimer's
DopamineCatecholamineReward, movementParkinson's, schizophrenia
SerotoninIndolamineMood, appetite, sleepDepression, OCD

MCQ traps

  • Glutamate supports memory too, but "muscle contraction plus memory" points only to acetylcholine (the neuromuscular junction transmitter).
  • GABA is linked to sleep and anxiety, but "mood, appetite and sleep" as a trio is serotonin.
  • NE vs dopamine for reward. NE is about alertness and stress; reward and pleasure is dopamine (mesolimbic pathway).
  • Benzodiazepines do not bind the GABA site itself; they bind a separate site on GABA-A and boost GABA's effect (positive allosteric modulators).
  • Norepinephrine vs epinephrine. The brain mostly uses NE; epinephrine is mainly an adrenal hormone.
  • GABA is made from glutamate, not from serotonin or dopamine.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q59: Transmitter for muscle contraction, also linked to learning and memory → Acetylcholine; glutamate was a strong distractor (it drives LTP and learning) but it does not trigger skeletal muscle contraction.
  • Q80: Transmitter of reward and pleasure → Dopamine; norepinephrine sat as a distractor because it governs arousal and fight-or-flight, not the mesolimbic reward circuit.
  • Q91: Transmitter regulating mood, appetite and sleep → Serotonin; GABA (general inhibition, anxiety, sleep via sedatives) and glutamate (excitation) were both distractors here, neither covers the appetite-and-mood triad.
64Huntington's, multiple sclerosis
Biopsych · IHBAS 2024 Q63 · added from the options

From scratch

Both conditions sat as distractors next to Korsakoff's syndrome (topic 24). Each is a neurological disease with major psychiatric and cognitive features, which is exactly why clinical psychology exams like them.

Huntington's disease (HD). Described by the American physician George Huntington (1872), it was once called Huntington's chorea (Greek choreia, dance). It is autosomal dominant: each child of an affected parent has a 50% risk, and anyone carrying the mutation will develop it if they live long enough (full penetrance). The gene (HTT, coding huntingtin) was mapped to chromosome 4 in 1983 (Gusella and colleagues, using families from Lake Maracaibo, Venezuela, studied by Nancy Wexler) and identified in 1993. The mutation is an expanded CAG trinucleotide repeat: 40 or more repeats is fully penetrant, 36 to 39 reduced penetrance. Longer repeats mean earlier onset, and repeats tend to expand across generations (especially through the father), giving anticipation; the juvenile (Westphal) form with rigidity often has a paternal origin.

Onset is typically in the 30s to 40s and the illness is fatal about 15 to 20 years later. The classic triad: (1) motor: chorea (brief, irregular, dance-like movements), later rigidity, dysarthria and dysphagia; (2) cognitive: a subcortical dementia with slowed processing, executive dysfunction and retrieval-type memory problems, but without the aphasia, apraxia and agnosia of cortical (Alzheimer-type) dementia; (3) psychiatric: depression, irritability, apathy, obsessive features, sometimes psychosis, and a high suicide risk. Psychiatric and cognitive change often precede chorea. Pathology: atrophy of the caudate nucleus and putamen (striatum), with loss of GABAergic medium spiny neurons; on imaging the lateral ventricles look box-shaped. Treatment is symptomatic: tetrabenazine (dopamine depleter) for chorea, antidepressants, antipsychotics. Predictive genetic testing raises serious counselling issues (the singer Woody Guthrie died of HD). DSM-5-TR codes the cognitive picture as major or mild neurocognitive disorder due to Huntington's disease.

Multiple sclerosis (MS). Characterised by Jean-Martin Charcot (1868) as "sclérose en plaques". It is a chronic autoimmune demyelinating disease of the central nervous system: the immune system attacks myelin made by oligodendrocytes in the brain, optic nerves and spinal cord, leaving hardened scars (plaques). Because myelin speeds conduction (saltatory conduction between nodes of Ranvier), demyelination slows or blocks signals. Diagnosis needs lesions disseminated in time and space (McDonald criteria, MRI showing periventricular white-matter plaques, oligoclonal bands in CSF). It mostly begins at 20 to 40 years, affects women two to three times more than men, and is commoner far from the equator (vitamin D and Epstein-Barr virus are risk factors).

Courses: relapsing-remitting (most common, about 85% at onset), secondary progressive, and primary progressive. Symptoms depend on lesion site: optic neuritis (painful blurred vision in one eye, often the first sign), double vision, numbness and tingling, weakness, fatigue, bladder problems, Lhermitte's sign (electric shock down the spine on bending the neck) and Uhthoff's phenomenon (worsening with heat, e.g. a hot bath). Charcot's triad: nystagmus, intention tremor and scanning speech (cerebellar signs). Psychiatric: depression is very common (lifetime around 50%), along with anxiety, pseudobulbar affect (uncontrollable laughing or crying) and occasionally euphoria; cognitive impairment hits processing speed, attention and working memory. Treatment: steroids for relapses, disease-modifying therapies (interferon-beta and others), and psychological support.

Key distinctions

FeatureHuntington's diseaseMultiple sclerosisKorsakoff's syndrome
CauseGenetic, CAG repeat, chromosome 4Autoimmune demyelinationThiamine deficiency, chronic alcohol
SiteCaudate and putamenCNS white matterMammillary bodies, medial thalamus
Onset30s to 40s20s to 40s, mostly womenAfter years of drinking
HallmarkChorea + dementia + psychiatric changeRelapsing neurological deficits, optic neuritisAnterograde amnesia + confabulation
CourseRelentlessly progressiveRelapsing-remitting or progressiveLargely irreversible

MCQ traps

  • MS is central, not peripheral. Peripheral demyelination (Schwann cells) is Guillain-Barré syndrome.
  • HD is autosomal dominant; do not pick "recessive" or "X-linked". It is a trinucleotide repeat disorder, like fragile X (CGG).
  • Chorea vs tremor. Chorea (jerky, dance-like) is HD; resting tremor is Parkinson's; intention tremor is cerebellar (including MS).
  • Subcortical vs cortical dementia. HD, Parkinson's and HIV give subcortical (slowing, retrieval problems); Alzheimer's gives cortical (aphasia, agnosia, storage failure).
  • Alcohol + amnesia + confusion → Korsakoff's, never HD or MS.
  • HD involves dopamine excess relative to lost GABA neurons; Parkinson's is dopamine loss. Opposite movement pictures.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q63: Disorder with severe memory impairment and confusion, often caused by chronic alcohol abuse → Korsakoff's syndrome; Huntington's disease was a distractor (genetic CAG-repeat disorder with chorea; its memory problems come with motor signs and no link to alcohol), and multiple sclerosis was a distractor (autoimmune demyelination with relapsing sensory, visual and motor deficits). Schizophrenia was the fourth option. Both topics appeared only as distractors.
65Eye problems (hypermetropia, astigmatism, visual field defects) + agnosias
Biopsych · IHBAS 2024 Q67, Q83 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 24 covered myopia and prosopagnosia. Here we cover the rest of Q67's options and the wider family of agnosias behind Q83. The key split: refractive errors and field defects are failures of sensation (the image does not reach or register correctly), while agnosias are failures of recognition (sensation is fine, meaning is lost).

Refraction basics. The cornea and the lens bend (refract) light to focus it on the retina. The ciliary muscles change the lens shape for near versus far targets (accommodation). A normal eye is emmetropic. Myopia (near-sightedness): eyeball too long or cornea too curved, so distant images focus in front of the retina; distance is blurred; corrected by a concave (minus) lens. Hypermetropia/hyperopia (far-sightedness): eyeball too short, focus falls behind the retina; near objects are hardest; corrected by a convex (plus) lens. Presbyopia: age-related stiffening of the lens (from the 40s) so near focusing fails; also corrected by convex reading glasses, but the cause is the lens, not eyeball length. Astigmatism: the cornea or lens is unevenly curved (like a rugby ball rather than a football), so light along one axis focuses differently from the other; vision is blurred or distorted at all distances, and lines in one orientation look sharper; corrected with a cylindrical lens. Other eye conditions: cataract (clouded lens), glaucoma (raised intraocular pressure damaging the optic nerve, loss of peripheral vision first).

Visual field defects come from damage along the visual pathway, not from optics, and the pattern localises the lesion. The fibres from the nasal half of each retina cross at the optic chiasm; temporal fibres stay on the same side. So: optic nerve lesion → blindness in one eye; chiasm lesion (classically a pituitary tumour) → bitemporal hemianopia (tunnel-like loss of both outer fields); optic tract, radiation or occipital cortex lesion → contralateral homonymous hemianopia (same half of the field lost in both eyes), often with macular sparing in occipital stroke; temporal lobe (Meyer's loop) lesion → contralateral superior quadrantanopia ("pie in the sky"); parietal radiation lesion → inferior quadrantanopia. A scotoma is a local blind spot. Blindsight (Weiskrantz, patient D.B.): after V1 damage, patients deny seeing but point to targets above chance.

Agnosias. Freud (1891) coined "agnosia" (not knowing). Lissauer (1890) split visual agnosia into apperceptive (cannot form a whole percept, cannot copy or match shapes) and associative (can copy a drawing perfectly but cannot say what it is). Types: visual object agnosia (objects); prosopagnosia (faces, fusiform gyrus, Bodamer 1947); achromatopsia (colour, V4); akinetopsia (motion, V5/MT; patient L.M., Zihl and colleagues, 1983, saw pouring tea as frozen); simultanagnosia (cannot see more than one object at once, part of Balint's syndrome with optic ataxia and ocular apraxia, bilateral parieto-occipital); topographical agnosia (cannot recognise places); auditory agnosia and amusia; tactile agnosia/astereognosis (parietal); finger agnosia (Gerstmann); anosognosia (Babinski, 1914: unawareness of one's own deficit, e.g. denying a left hemiplegia after right parietal stroke). Oliver Sacks's The Man Who Mistook His Wife for a Hat describes visual agnosia.

Key distinctions

ConditionProblemCorrection / lesion
MyopiaDistant objects blurredConcave lens
HypermetropiaNear objects blurredConvex lens
PresbyopiaNear focus fails with ageConvex reading lens
AstigmatismDistortion at all distancesCylindrical lens
Bitemporal hemianopiaBoth outer fields lostOptic chiasm (pituitary)
Homonymous hemianopiaSame half-field lost in both eyesBehind the chiasm, opposite side
ProsopagnosiaFaces not recognisedFusiform gyrus
AgeusiaLoss of tasteGustatory, not an agnosia

MCQ traps

  • Hyper = far-sighted, sees far well, struggles near. Students flip myopia and hypermetropia; anchor on the British terms: myopia = "short-sighted" (sees only short distances), hypermetropia = "long-sighted".
  • Presbyopia is not hypermetropia. Both need convex lenses, but presbyopia is age-related lens rigidity.
  • Astigmatism is not "distance only". It blurs at every distance.
  • Field defects are neural, not optical. Glasses never fix a hemianopia.
  • Agnosia requires intact sensation. A blind patient who cannot name objects has no agnosia.
  • Prosopagnosia = faces. Taste loss is ageusia; inability to show facial expressions is facial paralysis or hypomimia (e.g. Parkinson's mask), not prosopagnosia.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q67: Difficulty seeing objects at a distance → Myopia; hypermetropia (near blur), astigmatism (blur at all distances from uneven curvature) and visual field defects (pathway lesions, not focusing) were the distractors.
  • Q83: Prosopagnosia results in an inability to → the official key says (b) distinguish tastes, which looks wrong. The textbook answer is (a) recognize faces. "Distinguish tastes" describes ageusia, "recognize objects" (d) is visual object agnosia (a sister agnosia, not face-specific), and "express feelings through facial expressions" (c) is an output problem, not a recognition failure. Write (a) in any descriptive answer.
66Neurodevelopmental disorders (ADHD etc.) vs OCD and BPD
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q18, Q5, Q78 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 30 covered autism. This topic is about classification: which DSM-5-TR chapter a disorder belongs to, because Q18 was really a "which chapter" question. DSM-5 (2013) reorganised the manual on a developmental, lifespan logic, and the first chapter became Neurodevelopmental Disorders: conditions with onset in the developmental period, typically before the child enters school, producing deficits in personal, social, academic or occupational functioning. They often co-occur with one another.

The neurodevelopmental chapter contains: (1) intellectual developmental disorder (intellectual disability, graded mild to profound by adaptive functioning, not IQ alone) and global developmental delay; (2) communication disorders (language disorder, speech sound disorder, childhood-onset fluency disorder or stuttering, social pragmatic communication disorder); (3) autism spectrum disorder; (4) ADHD; (5) specific learning disorder (reading, written expression, mathematics: dyslexia, dyscalculia); (6) motor disorders (developmental coordination disorder, stereotypic movement disorder, and tic disorders including Tourette's). DSM-IV had kept these under "Disorders Usually First Diagnosed in Infancy, Childhood, or Adolescence", a chapter DSM-5 abolished. In India, autism, intellectual disability and specific learning disabilities are among the 21 disabilities recognised by the RPwD Act 2016.

ADHD in depth. A persistent pattern of inattention and/or hyperactivity-impulsivity. DSM-5-TR requires 6 or more symptoms in a domain (5 for those aged 17 and older) for at least 6 months, several symptoms present before age 12 (raised from 7 in DSM-IV), present in two or more settings (home, school, work), with clear impairment. Presentations: combined, predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive. DSM-5 also allowed ADHD to be diagnosed alongside autism for the first time. Prevalence is about 7% of children and 2.5% of adults (DSM-5-TR), with boys diagnosed about twice as often in childhood. Russell Barkley models ADHD as a core deficit in behavioural inhibition that disrupts executive functions; neuroimaging shows delayed cortical maturation and frontostriatal dopamine and norepinephrine involvement. Treatment: stimulants (methylphenidate, amphetamines), non-stimulants (atomoxetine), parent training and classroom behaviour management.

Why OCD is not neurodevelopmental. OCD can begin in childhood (and is linked to tics: a "tic-related" specifier exists), but onset is typically in adolescence or early adulthood and it is not a disorder of developmental brain maturation in DSM terms. DSM-5 moved OCD out of the anxiety disorders into its own chapter, Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders, together with body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding disorder, trichotillomania (hair-pulling) and excoriation (skin-picking). It carries an insight specifier (good/fair, poor, absent/delusional).

Why BPD is not neurodevelopmental. Borderline personality disorder is a Cluster B personality disorder (with antisocial, histrionic, narcissistic). Personality disorders are enduring patterns beginning by adolescence or early adulthood. BPD needs 5 of 9 criteria: frantic efforts to avoid abandonment; unstable intense relationships swinging between idealisation and devaluation; identity disturbance; impulsivity in at least two self-damaging areas; recurrent suicidal behaviour or self-harm; affective instability; chronic emptiness; intense inappropriate anger; transient stress-related paranoia or dissociation. Adolf Stern used "borderline" in 1938; Otto Kernberg described borderline personality organisation; Marsha Linehan's biosocial theory and Dialectical Behaviour Therapy (DBT) are the evidence-based treatment. GAD sits in the anxiety disorders chapter.

Key distinctions

DisorderDSM-5-TR chapterTypical onsetOverlap that confuses
ADHDNeurodevelopmentalBefore 12Impulsivity, emotional dysregulation
OCDObsessive-Compulsive and RelatedAdolescence to early adulthood (some childhood)Concentration problems from obsessions
BPDPersonality Disorders (Cluster B)Adolescence / early adulthoodImpulsivity, mood swings
GADAnxiety DisordersAny, median around 30Restlessness, poor concentration
Tourette'sNeurodevelopmental (motor/tic)Before 18High OCD comorbidity

MCQ traps

  • "Childhood onset" does not equal neurodevelopmental. Separation anxiety and childhood OCD start young but sit in other chapters.
  • Tourette's is neurodevelopmental (a motor disorder), even though it travels with OCD.
  • OCD left the anxiety chapter in DSM-5; PTSD also left it (to Trauma- and Stressor-Related).
  • Age cut-offs: ADHD symptoms before 12 (DSM-5) not 7; PD diagnosis under 18 needs features for at least 1 year, and antisocial PD needs age 18.
  • Impulsivity is shared. ADHD impulsivity is developmental and pervasive since childhood; BPD impulsivity is tied to relationship stress and identity instability.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q18: Which disorder is a neurodevelopmental disorder → ADHD; OCD (Obsessive-Compulsive and Related chapter), borderline personality disorder (Cluster B personality disorder) and GAD (anxiety disorders) were the distractors.
  • Q5: Persistent sadness and loss of interest → Depression; OCD sat as a distractor (obsessions and compulsions, not anhedonia), alongside schizophrenia and bipolar disorder.
  • Q78: Defining feature of somatic symptom disorder → Physical symptoms without a medical cause (strictly, DSM-5-TR now requires excessive thoughts, feelings or behaviours about symptoms rather than "no medical cause"); "obsessive behaviours" was a distractor pointing to OCD.
67Delusions of reference, control, grandiosity
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q36, Q78 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 27 listed the delusional themes. Here we go deeper into the three that sat as distractors beside persecution in Q36: reference, control and grandiosity. Recall the base definition (Jaspers, 1913): a delusion is a fixed false belief, held with certainty, incorrigible by evidence, and out of keeping with the person's cultural or religious background.

Ideas vs delusions of reference. In reference, neutral events are experienced as personally meaningful: a newsreader is talking about me, strangers laughing on the street are laughing at me, a song on the radio is a message to me. Psychopathology separates ideas of reference (held with doubt, can be reality-tested, "I know it's silly but I feel they're talking about me", seen in social anxiety, depression and schizotypal personality disorder) from delusions of reference (held with full conviction). DSM-5-TR's schizotypal criteria literally say "ideas of reference (excluding delusions of reference)". Reference is closely related to delusional perception, a Schneiderian first-rank symptom: a normal perception is suddenly given a private, unrelated delusional meaning (the traffic light turned red, so I knew I was chosen to save the world). Reference delusions are common in schizophrenia and in the early "delusional mood" (Wahnstimmung), where everything feels charged with meaning.

Delusions of control (passivity). The belief that one's thoughts, feelings, impulses or actions are being controlled by an outside agency (radio waves, a chip, a god, a neighbour's machine). The core is a loss of ego boundary: experiences that are one's own are felt as alien and imposed. Kurt Schneider grouped these "made" experiences into his first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia: made feelings, made impulses, made volitional acts, somatic passivity, plus thought insertion, thought withdrawal and thought broadcasting, audible thoughts, voices arguing, voices commenting, and delusional perception. Mellor (1970) gave the classic patient examples ("I cry, tears roll down my cheeks and I look unhappy, but inside I have a cold anger because they are using me in this way"). First-rank symptoms are no longer pathognomonic (they also occur in mania and other psychoses), and DSM-5 removed the special weight once given to bizarre delusions, but they remain central in Indian MSE teaching and viva questions.

Grandiose delusions. A fixed belief in inflated worth, power, knowledge, identity or a special relationship with a deity or famous person ("I am the reincarnation of Vivekananda", "I have discovered the cure for cancer"). They are classically mood-congruent in mania (DSM-5-TR "with mood-congruent psychotic features"), also occur in schizophrenia, in delusional disorder, grandiose type, and historically in general paresis of the insane (neurosyphilis). Separate them from grandiosity as a trait: in narcissistic personality disorder the person has a grandiose sense of self-importance but it is not a psychotic, fixed false belief, and in hypomania grandiosity may be inflated self-esteem without delusional conviction.

Delusional disorder (DSM-5-TR): one or more delusions for at least 1 month, functioning not markedly impaired apart from the delusion, and never meeting criterion A for schizophrenia. Subtypes: erotomanic, grandiose, jealous, persecutory, somatic, mixed, unspecified, with a "with bizarre content" specifier.

Key distinctions

ExperienceCore beliefTypical setting
PersecutionI am being harmed, plotted against, discriminated againstSchizophrenia, delusional disorder (commonest)
ReferenceNeutral events are about or aimed at meSchizophrenia; ideas of reference in schizotypal PD, social anxiety
Control / passivityMy thoughts, feelings or actions are made by an outside forceSchizophrenia (first-rank)
GrandioseI am special, powerful, famous, divinely chosenMania, delusional disorder
OCD intrusive thoughtRecognised as a product of my own mindOCD, not a delusion

MCQ traps

  • "Interfered with, plotted against, threatened, discriminated against" = persecution, even though "interfered with" sounds like control. Control is about one's own will being taken over.
  • Reference vs persecution. "The TV is talking about me" is reference; it becomes persecutory only if the content is harm.
  • Ideas vs delusions of reference: conviction decides it. Schizotypal PD has ideas, not delusions.
  • Thought insertion vs obsession. In OCD the person knows the thought is their own (ego-dystonic); in thought insertion it is felt as someone else's.
  • Grandiose delusion vs narcissism. Fixed, false and psychotic vs an inflated self-image without loss of reality testing.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q36: Belief that one is deliberately interfered with, discriminated against, plotted against, threatened or mistreated → Persecution; reference (events about me, not harm), control (external agency running my thoughts or actions) and grandiosity (inflated importance) were all distractors. This topic appeared here only as distractors.
  • Q78: Defining feature of somatic symptom disorder → Physical symptoms without a medical cause; "delusions of grandeur" was a distractor because SSD involves distressing bodily preoccupation without psychotic conviction; a fixed false bodily belief would be a somatic delusion, not SSD.
68Panic ("fear of fear"), PTSD flashbacks, OCD checking
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q66, Q93, Q100 · added from the options

From scratch

Q66 asked for GAD (topic 26), but each wrong option was a textbook signature phrase of another disorder: "fear of fear" (panic disorder), "continual flashbacks" (PTSD), "a desire to check that the environment is safe" (OCD checking). Learn the three signatures and the models behind them.

Panic disorder and "fear of fear". A panic attack is an abrupt surge of intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, with at least 4 of 13 symptoms: palpitations, sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, choking, chest pain, nausea, dizziness, chills or heat, paresthesias, derealisation or depersonalisation, fear of losing control or "going crazy", and fear of dying. Panic disorder (DSM-5-TR) requires recurrent unexpected attacks, with at least one followed by 1 month or more of persistent worry about further attacks or their consequences and/or maladaptive behaviour change (avoiding exercise, carrying a phone everywhere). That anticipatory dread of the next attack is the "fear of fear", a phrase associated with Goldstein and Chambless (1978). David Clark's cognitive model (1986): panic arises from the catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations (racing heart → "I'm having a heart attack" → more fear → more sensations, a vicious circle). Anxiety sensitivity (Reiss and McNally, 1985), the belief that anxiety sensations are dangerous, is the trait vulnerability. Treatment: CBT with interoceptive exposure and SSRIs.

PTSD and flashbacks. DSM-5-TR moved PTSD to Trauma- and Stressor-Related Disorders. Criterion A: exposure to actual or threatened death, serious injury or sexual violence (direct, witnessed, learned of for a close person, or repeated professional exposure to details). Four symptom clusters lasting more than 1 month: intrusion (memories, nightmares, flashbacks, cue-triggered distress), avoidance, negative alterations in cognition and mood (including inability to remember an important aspect of the trauma, persistent blame, detachment), and arousal and reactivity (hypervigilance, exaggerated startle, sleep and concentration problems, irritability). A flashback is a dissociative reaction in which the person feels or acts as if the trauma is happening now, ranging from brief sensory intrusions to complete loss of awareness of present surroundings. That "nowness" separates it from an ordinary intrusive memory, which is known to be past. Models: Brewin's dual representation theory (sensory, image-based memories cut off from contextual memory) and Ehlers and Clark (2000) (a poorly elaborated trauma memory plus negative appraisals produce a sense of current threat). If symptoms last 3 days to 1 month it is acute stress disorder. Treatments: prolonged exposure (Foa), cognitive processing therapy (Resick), trauma-focused CBT, EMDR (Shapiro).

OCD checking. Checking is the commonest compulsion after cleaning. The cycle: an obsession of doubt and harm ("Did I leave the gas on? The house will burn and it will be my fault") → anxiety → compulsion (checking the knob, often in rituals of a set number) → brief relief → the relief negatively reinforces checking (Mowrer's two-factor theory: fear acquired by classical conditioning, maintained by operant avoidance). Salkovskis (1985) and Rachman stressed inflated responsibility: checkers believe they are responsible for preventing harm. Paradoxically, repeated checking reduces memory confidence (van den Hout and Kindt, 2003), so the more one checks, the less sure one feels. Treatment is exposure and response prevention (Victor Meyer, 1966): leave the house without checking, let anxiety rise and fall (habituation), and learn that the feared outcome does not follow (extinction).

Key distinctions

Signature phraseDisorderFocus of fearTime frame
Free-floating apprehension about many thingsGADMany future events6 months
Fear of fearPanic disorderThe next attack, bodily sensations1 month of worry after attacks
Flashbacks, relivingPTSDPast trauma felt as presentMore than 1 month
Checking to prevent harmOCDResponsibility for harmTime-consuming (over 1 h/day) or impairing
Gaps in personal memory after traumaDissociative amnesiaNot fear; memory lossAny

MCQ traps

  • Panic attack is not panic disorder. Attacks occur across many disorders (a specifier in DSM-5-TR); the disorder needs unexpected attacks plus a month of fear of fear.
  • Flashback vs intrusive memory. The flashback is dissociative reliving; an intrusive memory is recognised as a memory.
  • PTSD includes amnesia for parts of the trauma, but when the stem centres on inability to recall important personal information, the answer is dissociative amnesia.
  • ERP works by habituation and extinction, not reciprocal inhibition (Wolpe's systematic desensitisation).

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q66: GAD involves → Continued apprehension about unspecified things; "fear of fear" (panic disorder), "continual flashbacks to past events" (PTSD) and "a desire to check that the environment is safe" (OCD checking) were the three distractors, each a signature of another disorder.
  • Q93: Inability to remember important personal information, often following trauma → Dissociative amnesia; PTSD was the near-miss distractor because it can include amnesia for part of the trauma, but its core is intrusion, avoidance and hyperarousal.
  • Q100: ERP is based on which principle → Habituation and extinction; this is the treatment for OCD checking described above, with counter-conditioning and reciprocal inhibition (desensitisation) as distractors.
69Confabulation vs malingering vs factitious disorder
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q68, Q63, Q78 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 28 set out the basic grid (conscious or not, internal or external motive). Here we go deeper into the three conditions that MCQs love to swap: confabulation (a memory disorder), factitious disorder (a psychiatric disorder) and malingering (a behaviour, not a disorder).

Confabulation is the production of false or distorted memories about oneself or the world, stated with full conviction and without any intent to deceive. The patient is not lying; he genuinely believes what he says. It was described in Korsakoff's syndrome (Sergei Korsakoff, 1887), where thiamine deficiency damages the mammillary bodies and dorsomedial thalamus. Kopelman (1987) split it into two kinds: provoked (momentary) confabulation, plausible errors produced only when questioned ("Yesterday I went to the market"), common in many amnesias; and spontaneous (fantastic) confabulation, unprompted and often bizarre, sometimes acted upon. Spontaneous confabulation is tied to damage of the ventromedial and orbitofrontal prefrontal cortex and basal forebrain, classically after rupture of an anterior communicating artery aneurysm. The underlying problem is a failure of source and temporal monitoring: old memories are retrieved but not tagged as old, so they are reported as current. Related phenomena: Anton syndrome (cortical blindness with denial and confabulated descriptions of what the patient "sees"), anosognosia after right parietal stroke, and Gazzaniga's split-brain "interpreter", where the left hemisphere invents a reason for actions it did not initiate.

Factitious disorder (DSM-5-TR, Somatic Symptom and Related Disorders) has two forms. Imposed on self: (A) falsification of physical or psychological signs or symptoms, or induction of injury or disease, associated with identified deception; (B) the person presents himself as ill, impaired or injured; (C) the deceptive behaviour is evident even in the absence of obvious external rewards; (D) not better explained by another disorder such as delusional disorder. Imposed on another: the same, but the victim is another person (usually a child); the perpetrator, not the victim, receives the diagnosis. Methods include injecting saliva or faeces, taking insulin or warfarin, heating thermometers, contaminating urine with blood. The severe chronic form, Munchausen syndrome (Richard Asher, 1951), features wandering between hospitals, dramatic but vague histories, pseudologia fantastica (elaborate pathological lying), many surgical scars, eagerness for invasive procedures, and discharge against advice when confronted. Motive: the psychological need to occupy the sick role.

Malingering is the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated symptoms motivated by external incentives: compensation, avoiding military duty or work, evading prosecution, obtaining drugs. DSM-5-TR codes it among "other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention" (Z76.5), not as a mental disorder. Suspicion should arise with a medicolegal context, marked discrepancy between claimed distress and objective findings, poor cooperation, and antisocial personality disorder. Detection uses symptom validity tests: the Test of Memory Malingering (TOMM, Tombaugh 1996), Rey's 15-Item Test, forced-choice tests where scoring below chance implies knowing the right answer, MMPI-2 over-reporting scales (F, Fb, Fp, FBS), and the Structured Interview of Reported Symptoms (SIRS, Rogers).

Ganser syndrome sits in between: approximate answers (vorbeireden, "2 + 2 = 5", "a horse has 5 legs"), clouding of consciousness, pseudohallucinations, first described in prisoners (Ganser, 1898); now classed as other specified dissociative disorder.

Key distinctions

FeatureConfabulationFactitious disorderMalingering
Awareness of falsityNone, sincereConscious productionConscious production
MotiveNone (brain dysfunction)Internal: sick roleExternal gain
StatusNeuropsychological signDSM mental disorderZ-code, not a disorder
Typical settingKorsakoff, ACoA aneurysm, frontal injuryHospitals, health-care workersCourt, insurance, army, prison
Ends whenMonitoring recovers, or neverRarely, even when exposedOnce the incentive is gone

MCQ traps

  • "Fabrication of memories" without the word "deliberate" = confabulation; it is a memory sign, not deception.
  • "Consciously faking ... for a nonmedical objective" = malingering. "Nonmedical" is the giveaway that the goal is external.
  • In factitious disorder imposed on another, the diagnosis goes to the caregiver who fakes the child's illness.
  • Below-chance scores on a two-choice memory test suggest malingering, not severe amnesia.
  • Ganser's approximate answers are not confabulation; the patient gives near-miss answers to simple questions.
  • Pseudologia fantastica (lying about life history) belongs to factitious disorder, not to Korsakoff.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q68: consciously faking illness to achieve a specific nonmedical objective → Malingering. Confabulation sat as a distractor and was wrong because it is unintentional memory filling; "altered identities" (DID) and "false identification" (misidentification) were also distractors.
  • Q63: severe memory impairment and confusion from chronic alcohol use → Korsakoff's syndrome; confabulation does not appear as an option but is the hallmark sign named in the explanation.
  • Q78: defining feature of somatic symptom disorder → Physical symptoms without a medical cause; malingering and factitious disorder appear only in the explanation as the consciously produced conditions SSD must be separated from.
70Derealization vs depersonalization, dissociative fugue
Abnormal · IHBAS 2024 Q88, Q93, Q69 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 29 gave the definitions. This topic adds phenomenology, history, causes and the differential diagnosis that NIMHANS-style viva and MCQs probe.

Depersonalization is an altered experience of the self: feeling detached from one's own mind or body, as if an outside observer. Patients say "I feel like a robot", "my voice does not sound like mine", "my hands look like someone else's", "I have no feelings, I am numb", "I am watching myself in a film". Five components are usually described: anomalous body experience, emotional numbing, anomalous subjective recall (memories feel as if they did not happen to me), alienation from surroundings, and a sense of unreality of the self. Derealization is an altered experience of the world: surroundings look flat, two-dimensional, foggy, dreamlike, artificial, "like a stage set" or "behind glass", with distortions of size, distance and time. Both are described in "as if" language. The term depersonalization was introduced by the French psychiatrist Ludovic Dugas (1898); the Cambridge group (Sierra and Berrios) built the Cambridge Depersonalization Scale (2000).

Depersonalization/derealization disorder (DSM-5-TR): (A) persistent or recurrent depersonalization, derealization or both; (B) reality testing remains intact; (C) clinically significant distress or impairment; (D) not due to a substance (cannabis, hallucinogens, ketamine) or medical condition (temporal lobe epilepsy, migraine, head injury); (E) not better explained by another disorder (schizophrenia, panic disorder, depression, PTSD, another dissociative disorder). DSM-5 merged the two experiences into one disorder because they co-occur so often. Lifetime transient experiences are very common; the disorder has a prevalence of roughly 2 percent, with a mean onset in mid-adolescence and equal sex ratio. Triggers include severe stress, emotional abuse in childhood, panic attacks and drug use. Treatment is mainly CBT (reattributing the symptoms, reducing avoidance and checking); no drug is clearly established.

Dissociative fugue (Latin fuga, flight) is sudden, unexpected travel away from home or purposeful-seeming wandering, with amnesia for one's identity or past, sometimes with assumption of a partial or wholly new identity. During the fugue the person appears normal to strangers. Recovery is often abrupt, after which the person is confused about where he is and has no memory of the fugue period. Historically, the French "mad traveller" epidemic began with Albert Dadas, described by Philippe Tissie (1887). DSM-IV listed fugue as a separate disorder; DSM-5 made it a specifier: dissociative amnesia with dissociative fugue. Precipitants: war, disasters, marital or financial crisis, legal threat. Differential: complex partial (temporal lobe) seizures with postictal automatism (brief, no complex goal-directed travel, EEG changes), alcoholic blackouts, transient global amnesia (identity preserved, repetitive questioning, lasts hours), and malingering when there is legal trouble.

Key distinctions

TermWhat feels alteredReality testingMemory
DepersonalizationSelf, body, own feelingsIntactIntact
DerealizationExternal worldIntactIntact
Nihilistic (Cotard) delusion"I am dead, my organs are rotting"Lost (psychotic)Intact
Dissociative fugueIdentity, with travelIntactAmnesia for identity and for the fugue
Transient global amnesiaNew learningIntactAnterograde; identity kept
  • "As if I am unreal" = depersonalization; "I am unreal" held as fact = delusion.
  • Jamais vu (familiar feels new) and deja vu are related temporal-lobe experiences, not the same as derealization.

MCQ traps

  • The word "unreality" alone does not decide; ask unreality of what. Self → depersonalization, surroundings → derealization.
  • Insight is preserved in DPDR; an option saying "loss of reality testing" is wrong.
  • Fugue requires travel or wandering; amnesia for identity without travel is generalized dissociative amnesia.
  • In DSM-5-TR fugue is a specifier, not a separate diagnosis; a question asking for the "DSM-5 disorder" will not list fugue on its own.
  • DID needs two or more personality states; a fugue's new identity is usually single and replaces the old one.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q88: profound disconnection or estrangement from oneself, unreality of one's own thoughts, feelings or identity → Depersonalization. Derealization was the closest distractor (unreality of the surroundings, not the self); fugue state was wrong because nothing in the stem mentions travel or loss of identity; dissociative amnesia was wrong because memory is intact.
  • Q93: inability to remember important personal information after trauma → Dissociative amnesia; fugue is not offered, but it is the travel specifier of this very disorder.
  • Q69: the DID identity carrying the person's real name → Host identity; useful contrast because a fugue's assumed identity is not an "alter".
71Defences: rationalization, displacement, sublimation
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q30, Q4 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 31 introduced id, ego, superego and the main defences. These three appeared as wrong options next to projection, so here each gets the depth an examiner can test: origin, mechanism, subtypes and classic examples.

Rationalization. The term was introduced into psychoanalysis by Ernest Jones (1908). The ego supplies a logical, socially acceptable explanation for behaviour whose real motive is unacceptable or unconscious. It differs from lying because the person believes the explanation. Two folk forms: sour grapes (from Aesop: the fox decides the unreachable grapes were sour anyway; "I did not really want that seat at NIMHANS") and sweet lemon (insisting that an unwanted outcome is actually good: "failing was the best thing that happened to me"). Vaillant places it among the neurotic (intermediate) defences. Rationalization is the psychoanalytic cousin of Festinger's dissonance reduction: both produce justifications after the act. Janis listed collective rationalization as a symptom of groupthink, where the group explains away warnings.

Displacement. The drive and the feeling stay the same, but the target changes from a dangerous object to a safer one. Freud first described it in The Interpretation of Dreams (1900) as part of the dream-work (with condensation, symbolisation and secondary revision): emotional intensity is shifted from an important dream thought onto a trivial detail. In phobias, anxiety about one object is displaced onto another: in the Little Hans case (1909) a boy's fear of his father (castration anxiety) was displaced onto horses. In everyday life it is the "kick the dog" chain. In social psychology the same idea underlies scapegoating: the frustration-aggression hypothesis (Dollard, Doob, Miller, Mowrer and Sears, 1939) says frustration produces aggression, which is often displaced onto weak, available targets such as minorities. Turning against the self (anger at others redirected onto oneself) is a special case linked by Freud to melancholia.

Sublimation. An unacceptable sexual or aggressive impulse is channelled into a socially valued, productive activity, and the impulse is partly gratified rather than blocked. Freud regarded it as the basis of art, science and civilisation; in his essay on Leonardo da Vinci (1910) he argued that Leonardo's sexual curiosity was sublimated into scientific curiosity. Examples: aggression into surgery, boxing, debating or policing; voyeuristic curiosity into photography or research. Anna Freud added it to the list as the most adaptive defence. In Vaillant's hierarchy (Adaptation to Life, 1977, from the Harvard Grant Study) sublimation is mature, with altruism, humour, suppression and anticipation, and men who used mature defences had better health, marriages and careers in later life. DSM-IV's Defensive Functioning Scale called this the "high adaptive level".

All three are unconscious, all reduce anxiety, and they can co-occur: a man humiliated by his boss (impulse: anger) may yell at his son (displacement), explain that the boy needed discipline (rationalization), or take up competitive sport (sublimation).

Key distinctions

DefenceWhat changesVaillant levelSignature clue
ProjectionOwner of the impulse ("he hates me")ImmatureAttributes
DisplacementTarget of the impulseNeuroticSafer substitute object
SublimationForm of the impulse, into valued activityMatureSocially useful outlet
RationalizationThe explanation for behaviourNeuroticPlausible false reason
IntellectualizationEmotion stripped, abstract talkNeuroticCold, technical language

MCQ traps

  • Displacement vs sublimation: if the substitute outlet is merely safer (the dog, the sibling), it is displacement; if it is admired and productive (art, surgery), it is sublimation.
  • Rationalization vs intellectualization: rationalization gives excuses; intellectualization removes feeling by theorising.
  • Rationalization is not conscious lying; deliberate excuse-making is not a defence.
  • Phobia in classic psychoanalysis is explained by displacement (Little Hans), not projection.
  • Sublimation is the only classic Freudian defence regarded as fully healthy; do not label it immature.

How IHBAS 2024 asked it

  • Q30: unconsciously attributing one's own unacceptable thoughts or feelings to someone else → Projection. Rationalization (option a), displacement (option c) and sublimation (option d) were all distractors: rationalization gives a false reason, displacement keeps the feeling as one's own but moves its target, and sublimation turns it into a valued activity. None of them attributes the feeling to another person.
  • Q4: groupthink → collective decision-making that discourages dissent; rationalization appears only in the explanation, as Janis's symptom of collective rationalization.
72Maslow, Skinner's radical behaviourism, early vs later social learning
Therapy · IHBAS 2024 Q7, Q20, Q12, Q73, Q92, Q58 · added from the options

From scratch

Topic 33 solved the Q7 match. This topic teaches each theorist in the list properly, so that any rearranged version of the match can be solved from understanding.

Abraham Maslow, "A Theory of Human Motivation" (1943) and Motivation and Personality (1954), a founder of humanistic psychology (the "third force" after psychoanalysis and behaviourism). His hierarchy of needs: physiological → safety → belongingness and love → esteem → self-actualization. The lower four are deficiency (D) needs: their lack creates tension, and once satisfied they stop motivating. Self-actualization is a growth or being (B) need, which grows stronger as it is fed.
